# Sūrah al-Tawbah (Chapter 9) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:1

> ˹This is˺ a discharge from all obligations, by Allah and His Messenger, to the polytheists you ˹believers˺ have entered into treaties with:

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Public Release From a Public Obligation**

The opening noun is barāʾah, a declaration that one party is no longer bound by a former connection. It is stronger than private disappointment and more exact than a general warning. A treaty had created obligations between the Muslim community and certain polytheist tribes; this verse announces that those obligations have been discharged. The wording also makes the authority clear: the release is from Allah and His Messenger. It is not a local commander improvising a policy, nor a crowd abandoning a promise in anger. A covenant of public consequence is ended by a public authority and in a form that can be heard by those affected.

That legal precision matters because the surrounding verses are severe. The severity is not a license to erase the difference between a faithful treaty partner and a violator. The verse identifies the people connected by treaties; the next verses set out notice, time, exceptions, and the continuing force of agreements that were kept. Read alone, the first line can sound like a blanket severance. Read as the beginning of a carefully bounded proclamation, it is the first entry in a legal record. The opening does not celebrate treachery. It names the moment at which the Muslim side is no longer required to carry an arrangement whose political basis has failed.

**Barāʾah Means Separation With Consequences**

The root b-r-ʾ conveys freedom from something that has attached itself to a person: a liability, a fault, or a relationship. In this setting, barāʾah is not a claim that every individual in the other community has the same inward state. It is a separation from a treaty framework. The distinction protects two truths at once. A government may withdraw from an agreement for a lawful reason, while individuals among the other side may still be treated justly. A political relationship and a human soul are not identical objects.

The verse also shows that covenants are not decorative language. In the Qur'an, a promise creates a real moral burden. Allah commands, Keep the covenant of Allah when you have pledged it, and do not break oaths after confirming them (16:91). That command remains relevant even when an agreement later has to be ended. Ending a pact is therefore not the same as pretending it never existed. The very need for a declaration proves that the pact had weight. A community that must announce its release acknowledges that the other side had been entitled to expect performance until a lawful change was communicated.

**The Historical Setting Is Part of the Meaning**

The verse is tied to the period after the conquest of Makkah and to the collapse of the treaty order that had followed Ḥudaybiyah. The well-known breach involved Banū Bakr attacking Banū Khuzāʿah, an allied tribe, with Quraysh support. The incident was not an isolated quarrel between private men. It threatened to turn a truce into a cover for renewed warfare. The Prophet's response made the principle visible: a party that uses a treaty while helping its allies attack the other side cannot demand the treaty's protection as though nothing happened.

Exegetes nevertheless differ over which groups verse 1 directly addresses and how its four-month notice relates to treaties of different lengths. Some connect it closely to the broken pact of Ḥudaybiyah. Others hold that the proclamation concerned several Arabian groups after Makkah, while faithful treaty holders retained their agreements. The second reading fits the explicit exception in verse 4, but the first preserves the immediate historical trigger. The stronger conclusion is not that every disputed date must be forced into one scheme. It is that the revelation addresses a changed political reality while refusing to hide the legal differences among the tribes involved.

**The Messenger as Herald of the Notice**

The proclamation was delivered during the pilgrimage season, when representatives of the Arabian tribes could hear the new policy directly. Abū Bakr led the pilgrimage in the ninth year after the Hijrah, and ʿAlī was sent to proclaim the relevant terms. This arrangement gave the announcement a public character and removed uncertainty about who spoke for the Muslim authority. Reports preserved in the major hadith collections describe the announcement as including the end of pagan pilgrimage practices and the protection of treaties that had not expired through breach.

That setting illuminates the verse's political ethics. A powerful community may be tempted to use changed circumstances as an excuse for surprise. The proclamation instead gives affected parties knowledge of their position. They are not left to infer a new policy from rumors or from an unexpected attack. Even when a covenant is being withdrawn, notice protects human choice and limits confusion. The verse begins a passage in which justice is expressed not only by the final decision but also by the manner in which the decision is communicated.

**A Boundary, Not a Blank Cheque**

Verse 1 must therefore be read as a boundary statement. It establishes that the Muslim polity is no longer bound to certain treaty relations, but it does not itself identify every person who may be fought, nor does it cancel the command to honor agreements that remain sound. The rest of the passage supplies those distinctions. Verse 4 protects the treaty keeper. Verse 6 protects the seeker of asylum and commands safe escort. Verse 13 names breach, attempted expulsion, and first aggression as the features of the hostile case.

This verse also supplies a lasting lesson about public trust. Political communities sometimes speak as if agreements are useful only while convenient. The Qur'an rejects that casualness in both directions. A treaty cannot be broken secretly, and it cannot be treated as sacred after the other party has decisively destroyed its basis. Lawful separation requires truth about the breach, a clear announcement, and a disciplined distinction between the guilty arrangement and every person who might be caught near it. Barāʾah is grave because covenants are grave. The chapter begins by making that gravity impossible to miss.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:2

> “You ˹polytheists˺ may travel freely through the land for four months, but know that you will have no escape from Allah, and that Allah will disgrace the disbelievers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Freedom of Movement Before the Deadline**

The first command after the treaty release is not an order to seize people. It is a period of movement: the affected polytheists may travel through the land for four months. The verb behind this freedom suggests roaming without being stopped. In a tense political setting, that detail is a form of protection. A tribe given notice needs time to leave, negotiate, settle property, find allies, or reconsider its position. The Qur'an places a known interval between the announcement and the next stage of the policy.

That interval also prevents a claim of surprise. The people concerned are not told that a treaty has ended and then attacked before they can understand what the notice means. The four months become a public measure of time. The exact calendar calculations were debated by early exegetes because the proclamation occurred during the pilgrimage season and the phrase later used for sacred months can be read in more than one way. Those debates do not weaken the central point. The period was announced, limited, and real. A warning that cannot be dated is a threat; this warning is a legal notice.

**The Land Is Wide, But It Is Not an Escape**

The command to travel is followed by a warning: know that you cannot thwart Allah. The contrast is deliberate. The people may move across the land, but no distance places them beyond divine knowledge or judgment. Human geography gives them room to act; it does not give them a place outside accountability. The verse holds mobility and helplessness together without contradiction. They have a genuine choice about what to do during the four months, yet they cannot turn the existence of that choice into immunity from its consequences.

The Qur'an uses the same idea elsewhere when it says that no one can escape Allah in the earth or in the sky (29:22). That cross-reference clarifies the warning here: it is not a prediction that every road will be physically blocked. It is a denial that flight can defeat the moral reality revealed by God. A person may cross a border, alter an alliance, or find a temporary refuge, but none of these changes the truth of a broken covenant. The notice therefore calls for action rather than panic. Travel is permitted; evasion is impossible.

**Disgrace Is the Exposure of a False Claim**

The closing promise speaks of Allah disgracing the disbelievers. The Qur'anic idea of disgrace is not merely defeat in a contest. It is the public collapse of a claim to honor. A community that presents itself as trustworthy while using treaties for advantage eventually exposes the distance between its words and conduct. The warning is especially fitting here because the passage has focused on covenants, the public language by which one side asks another to trust it.

Disgrace should not be confused with humiliation for its own sake. The verse connects it to a moral disclosure. What was hidden in private dealings becomes visible in the outcome. The same Qur'an that warns of disgrace also says that Allah does not let the deeds of the virtuous go to waste (9:120). The contrast is between conduct and its revealed worth. A people who spend the notice period in repentance can change their standing; a people who treat the notice as another opportunity for manipulation move toward the exposure named here.

**A Notice Period Is Also a Mercy**

The four months have a practical and spiritual function. They protect the innocent from confusion, but they also leave room for a change of heart. The next verse explicitly says that repentance is better. Thus the notice is not only a military preparation. It is an interval in which the recipients can ask whether their inherited loyalties, alliances, and worship are defensible before Allah. Divine judgment appears in the passage, but it is preceded by information and time.

The Qur'an often gives warning before punishment. Noah warned his people, Moses was sent to Pharaoh, and the Messenger was commanded to make the message clear. The principle is stated plainly: We never punish until We have sent a messenger (17:15). Verse 2 applies that principle to a political transition. A community informed of its changed status is more capable of making a responsible choice than one left to guess. Grace does not mean that every outcome is removed. It means that the decision is made with the necessary knowledge.

**What the Four Months Ask of the Reader**

The verse's immediate audience received a literal period of travel. Its wider lesson concerns the way people use time after a warning. A deadline can be treated as permission to postpone change, or as a gift in which change becomes possible. The four months are not described as evidence that the old conduct was acceptable. They are a final space in which the consequences have been announced but not yet fully carried out.

For believers, the moral question is familiar even when the setting is different. When a person learns that a promise, habit, or source of income is wrong, does he use the time before the consequence to repair it, or does he search for a route that hides the problem? The warning that no one can thwart Allah blocks the second strategy. The mercy of a defined interval opens the first. The land remains wide enough to move through, but the truth remains close enough to follow. Four months of freedom cannot become four months of self-deception.
The notice therefore combines legal clarity with a final opening for repentance. Time is given so that a choice can be made knowingly, not so that accountability disappears.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:3

> A declaration from Allah and His Messenger ˹is made˺ to all people on the day of the greater pilgrimage that Allah and His Messenger are free of the polytheists. So if you ˹pagans˺ repent, it will be better for you. But if you turn away, then know that you will have no escape from Allah. And give good news ˹O Prophet˺ to the disbelievers of a painful punishment.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Announcement Belongs in the Open**

This verse names a declaration made to all people on the day of the greater pilgrimage. Its setting is not incidental. Pilgrimage gathered tribes that otherwise lived far apart, and the sacred season provided a recognized period in which public speech could travel without being mistaken for a private raid. A change in relations affecting many communities required a message that many communities could hear. The verse therefore presents the policy as an open announcement, not as a secret instruction passed from one faction to another.

The word translated declaration is related to a public call or proclamation. It carries the sense of making something known so that no one can reasonably claim ignorance of the position. In the ninth year after the Hijrah, Abū Bakr led the pilgrimage and ʿAlī proclaimed the terms associated with this passage. The reports about that proclamation are preserved in the hadith tradition, including the rule that a treaty holder would be protected until the agreed term. The public delivery gives the verse a legal character: the people are being informed of a changed relationship before they are judged for refusing to respond.

**Freedom From a Political Alliance**

The declaration says that Allah and His Messenger are free of the polytheists. This is the same separation introduced by barāʾah in verse 1, now stated in a form that reaches the entire pilgrimage audience. It does not mean that every polytheist is denied food, conversation, or protection. Verse 6 will require safe escort for one who asks to hear the message. Nor does it mean that every pact has been treated identically. Verse 4 will preserve the agreements of those who have kept their terms.

The separation is from a collective political arrangement that can no longer be relied upon. That distinction is essential to the verse's justice. A state may announce that it is no longer responsible for a hostile alliance while still recognizing the rights of a person who is not participating in that hostility. In modern terms, severing diplomatic obligations is not the same as declaring every civilian an enemy. The Qur'anic passage makes this distinction through its sequence of announcement, exception, protection, and accountability.

**Repentance Is Placed Before Retaliation**

Immediately after the declaration comes the invitation: if you repent, it is better for you. The verse does not conceal the seriousness of the breach, but neither does it make the past an unchangeable identity. Tawbah means turning back. Here it can include abandoning the beliefs and political loyalties that sustain hostility and entering a different relation with Allah and the believers. The important point is the order. Repentance is not offered after every path has been closed. It is named while the hearers still have time to choose.

The Qur'an uses this structure repeatedly. Warning is paired with a route back, and judgment is paired with a clear account of what has been refused. The invitation is not empty optimism. It has a concrete result: the person who turns back is no longer standing in the same category as the person who insists on the hostile arrangement. Verse 11 will describe that result as brotherhood in faith. The passage thus refuses both fatalism and sentimentality. Sin is serious, but a person is not locked permanently inside a former allegiance while repentance remains possible.

**The Irony of “Good News”**

The verse ends by telling the Prophet to give the disbelievers good news of a painful punishment. The Arabic verb normally belongs to glad tidings, so the phrase is deliberately sharp. Its irony exposes the absurdity of treating a grave warning as though it were pleasant news. The same rhetorical reversal appears when the Qur'an describes the hypocrites as rejoicing in their safety while the result of their choice is ruin. Here the hearer is confronted with the certainty of the announcement and the ugliness of what he has chosen.

The irony also reveals two kinds of hearing. Someone who still wants truth may hear the warning as a reason to repent. Someone committed to denial may hear only an insult. The message itself has not changed; the condition of the listener determines whether the warning becomes rescue or evidence against him. The Qur'an says that the same revelation increases believers in faith and increases diseased hearts in defilement (9:124–125). Verse 3 begins that distinction in the public square.

**The Limits of Human Power**

The repeated warning that there is no escape from Allah gives the declaration its final ground. A people can be beyond the reach of a particular army, but they cannot be beyond the reach of the One who judges intention, oath, and repentance. The political notice matters because human beings need to know their worldly obligations. The theological warning matters because worldly movement never settles the ultimate account.

For believers, this verse gives a pattern for difficult public truth. State clearly what has changed. Give the affected people knowledge of the decision. Leave a genuine door for repentance. Do not confuse firmness with private vengeance, and do not confuse mercy with pretending that betrayal has no consequences. The pilgrimage declaration held all four together. It was public without being chaotic, stern without hiding the path back, and decisive without granting any human party the illusion that it could escape Allah.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:4

> As for the polytheists who have honoured every term of their treaty with you and have not supported an enemy against you, honour your treaty with them until the end of its term. Surely Allah loves those who are mindful ˹of Him˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception Is the Rule’s Moral Center**

This verse prevents the opening proclamation from being read as a command to abandon every treaty with every polytheist. It identifies the protected group by conduct: they have honored the agreement and have not aided an enemy. Their religious identity does not erase the obligation created by their faithful behavior. The passage is therefore not sorting people only by creed. It is also sorting political relationships by trustworthiness.

The first condition is broad: they have not diminished the treaty in any respect. The second adds an indirect form of breach: they have not supported someone acting against the Muslims. A party cannot claim neutrality in its own name while secretly supplying the force that attacks its treaty partner. By naming both direct and indirect betrayal, the verse closes a familiar loophole. Loyalty is measured by what an alliance enables, not only by the words written on its face.

**Complete the Agreement to Its End**

The command to honor the treaty until its term is complete uses the language of bringing something to fulfillment. A covenant is not merely tolerated until a more profitable arrangement appears. Its endpoint is part of its meaning. If the other party has not broken it, the Muslim side is required to carry it through. The promise binds even when the partner is not a believer, because the real witness to the promise is Allah.

This principle echoes the Qur'anic command, Fulfill the covenant of Allah when you have pledged it (16:91). It also accords with the Prophet's public proclamation during the pilgrimage, which preserved the agreements of those who had not violated them. The verse gives a concrete test for piety. A person may appear religious in worship and speech, yet the truth of his God-consciousness is tested when keeping his word costs political advantage. The faithful treaty partner receives protection because fidelity itself has moral weight.

**The Passage Distinguishes Groups Carefully**

The surrounding verses contain strong language about hostile idolaters, but verse 4 refuses collective blame. The chapter does not say that because some tribes helped break a pact, all tribes must be treated as though they did. A sound agreement remains sound until its own conditions change. This is a principle of legal evidence: responsibility follows a demonstrated act, not an inherited label.

Early commentators discussed which tribe or tribes were intended by the exception. Some connected it with members of Banū Bakr who did not join the attack that violated the wider arrangement. Others proposed different historical identifications. Their disagreement does not remove the verse's operative rule. Whatever the precise referent, the text itself establishes the standard: those who stand by their terms are to be met with the same steadiness. The historical question narrows the application; it does not weaken the obligation.

**Taqwā Appears in Diplomacy**

The closing statement, Allah loves those who are mindful of Him, gives treaty law a spiritual foundation. Taqwā is often associated with private worship, but here it appears in public relations. Keeping an agreement is an act of reverence because it requires a person to remember Allah while dealing with people who cannot enforce every detail. If the only reason to keep a promise is fear of retaliation, the promise reveals little about character. Faithfulness when oversight is limited shows that the covenant is being kept for the One who sees.

The verse also corrects a narrow idea of loyalty. Some people reserve honesty for members of their own group and regard outsiders as legitimate targets of manipulation. This verse reverses that instinct. A treaty partner who has kept faith has a claim upon fair treatment precisely because the covenant has made him a partner in an obligation. Religious difference does not make a fulfilled promise disposable.

**Trust Requires Both Mercy and Memory**

Honoring the term does not mean ignoring evidence of breach. The verse is careful about its conditions, and the next verses address what happens when those conditions disappear. Mercy is not blindness. It is disciplined fidelity toward the person who has earned the protection promised. Conversely, suspicion is not wisdom when it treats every partner as already guilty. The believer must remember both sides: investigate conduct honestly, then act according to what the evidence establishes.

This has applications beyond treaties. An employee who has fulfilled a contract, a borrower who is meeting the agreed schedule, a neighbor who has respected a boundary, and a spouse who has kept a promise are all examples of people whose reliability should not be punished because someone else behaved badly. The verse gives a general moral rule: do not make the faithful pay for the faithless. Allah's love is attached to the person who keeps the line when the easier path would be to erase it.

**Faithfulness Is Not Naivety**

The verse does not ask the believers to surrender judgment. It asks them to judge accurately. A treaty that has been honored remains binding; a treaty that has been broken is treated under different provisions. That distinction is the opposite of naivety. Naivety ignores danger. Taqwā refuses to invent danger where the record does not show it.

The verse's final lesson is therefore both political and personal. Public strength must be restrained by a promise. Private piety must become dependable conduct. A believer cannot claim to fear Allah while using a covenant as a temporary tool and discarding it when convenient. The people of this verse are called worthy of protection, and the believers who protect them are called loved by Allah. The chapter's stern opening is thus governed from within by a simple demand: where the agreement has been kept, keep it too.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:5

> But once the Sacred Months have passed, kill the polytheists ˹who violated their treaties˺ wherever you find them, capture them, besiege them, and lie in wait for them on every way. But if they repent, perform prayers, and pay alms-tax, then set them free. Indeed, Allah is AllForgiving, Most Merciful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command Has a Defined Setting**

This is the passage's hardest sentence, and it must be read with every condition the verse itself supplies. The command concerns the polytheists who violated their treaties, and it follows a public notice and a period of security. It is not a free-standing order directed at every non-Muslim in every place and time. Verse 4 has just protected treaty keepers, and verse 6 will require asylum and safe escort for one who asks to hear the message. Those neighboring verses are not optional commentary added later; they are part of the same legal unit.

The opening time condition is also significant. Fighting is not presented as the first response to the treaty crisis. A known period must pass. Classical exegetes differed over whether the sacred months here are the four traditionally designated months or the four-month safe period announced earlier. Both readings preserve a limit. The verse does not describe an unbounded season in which force may begin whenever a commander chooses. Its chronology belongs to the law of notice, breach, and response.

**The Hostile Case Is Defined by Breach**

The translation itself supplies an important clarification by identifying the polytheists as those who violated their treaties. That reading fits the chapter's internal argument. Verse 13 names three features of the hostile group: they broke their oaths, sought to expel the Messenger, and attacked first. Verse 4 protects those who remained faithful. Verse 7 repeats the rule that loyalty is answered with loyalty. Taken together, these statements make it difficult to treat idolatry alone as the operative cause of the fighting described here.

Some jurists did read the verse more broadly, and their reasoning must be acknowledged rather than hidden. They focused on the force of the command and on the political transformation of Arabia after years of conflict. Other jurists emphasized the explicit exceptions and the prophetic practice of honoring treaties and granting protection. The stronger contextual reading sees a war order directed at a hostile political configuration, not a permission to kill any person whose creed differs. Verse 5 itself gives the terms of release: when the hostile alignment is abandoned through repentance, prayer, and alms, the person is to be set free.

**The Exit Clause Is Inside the Sentence**

The verse moves through the harsh verbs of war and then immediately states what happens when the accused leave the hostile category. They are not merely spared from that moment onward; the command says to set them free. Repentance changes their legal and spiritual position. The same sentence that authorizes capture refuses to make captivity the final purpose. Its ending invokes forgiveness and mercy rather than triumphal boasting.

This structure matters for any ethical reading of force. A war order that has no stated conditions for stopping can become an appetite. Here the command includes its own exit. The people who repent, pray, and pay the alms-tax are released. Verse 11 will describe them as brothers in faith. Even if one debates how the conditions operated in the historical setting, the text's direction is unmistakable: the purpose is not destruction for its own sake. The hostile arrangement is to end; the person who abandons it is not to be treated as though he remains inside it.

**Why the Verse Cannot Be Separated From Verse 6**

The next verse addresses an idolater who asks for protection so that he may hear God's Word. The Prophet is commanded to grant protection and then escort the person to safety, whether or not he accepts the message. This case shows that the war command is not triggered by the bare fact that a person is an idolater. A person within the same broad religious category can receive safe conduct because his actual action is to seek information rather than to continue the hostile campaign.

The passage therefore contains a legal scale. Faithful treaty keeping receives protection. A seeker of asylum receives protection. A treaty-breaking leadership that attacks first is confronted. The categories are determined by conduct and circumstance. Removing verse 5 from that scale creates a rule the chapter itself does not state. Applying it without the notice period, treaty exception, and asylum command is not strictness; it is textual distortion.

**Mercy Does Not Deny the Cost of Aggression**

The verse should not be softened into a statement that says nothing about war. The historical community faced armed alliances that had repeatedly used treaties to prepare further attacks. A political order has a duty to protect its people. The Qur'an recognizes that patience can become a way of abandoning those who are exposed to aggression. Force may be required to end an organized threat, but the legitimacy of that force depends on the conditions that produced it and the limits governing it.

The verse's final names, All-Forgiving and Most Merciful, do not erase the preceding command. They explain its moral horizon. Forgiveness remains available to the individual who turns back, and mercy governs how the former enemy is treated after the change. A just response can be severe toward an active threat while remaining open to repentance. That combination is harder than either sentimental permissiveness or permanent vengeance.

**The Verse Must Not Be Turned Into a Modern Slogan**

For readers today, the main obligation is interpretive honesty. Do not use this verse to justify violence against civilians, neighbors, or people who have not entered the historical hostile category. Do not use it to deny the Qur'an's recognition that organized aggression sometimes must be stopped. And do not remove the conditions that make the verse legally and morally intelligible: a broken treaty, an announced interval, a hostile campaign, a path of repentance, and release for those who take it.

A verse this forceful requires more context, not less. Its lesson is that justice protects the faithful, confronts treachery, and preserves a way back. The sword is not the chapter's only instrument. Notice, distinction, asylum, repentance, and mercy surround it. Read within that architecture, verse 5 is a command about a defined war. Read as a detached slogan, it becomes the opposite of what the surrounding verses teach.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:6

> And if anyone from the polytheists asks for your protection ˹O Prophet˺, grant it to them so they may hear the Word of Allah, then escort them to a place of safety, for they are a people who have no knowledge.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Person Who Asks Is Protected**

Verse 6 places a striking limit beside the fighting command of the previous verse. If a polytheist asks for protection, the response is not suspicion or detention. Protection must be granted. The Arabic command is direct and leaves little room for the commander to make safety depend on a prior promise of conversion. The request itself begins a duty. In a setting of war, the person who approaches under a plea for security is moved from the category of active threat to the category of one whose case must be heard.

This is the Qur'anic basis for a safe-conduct arrangement. A person may cross into the Muslim community for a stated purpose and cannot be harmed while that protection remains in force. The rule is attached to the act of seeking protection, not to the power or status of the visitor. A weak individual can create a binding obligation by asking. Later jurists discussed who could grant such security and how widely it applied, but the principle was already clear: a promise of safety is not a courtesy that can be withdrawn when the guest becomes inconvenient.

**Hearing the Word Is a Right, Not a Trap**

The purpose named in the verse is that the visitor may hear the Word of Allah. This does not describe forced instruction. It describes access to the message. A person who has been shaped by hostile reports, inherited custom, or incomplete information is allowed to encounter the Qur'an directly. The protection creates the conditions in which listening can be meaningful. Fear of immediate violence would turn the hearing into coercion; safety allows the person to consider what he has heard.

Exegetes differed over how much of the Qur'an the visitor must be allowed to hear. Some understood the Word broadly, while others focused on the material most relevant to the visitor's situation, including this passage's explanation of treaties and war. The disagreement concerns scope, not the duty to provide a real hearing. The verse does not say that the visitor must accept the message after hearing it. It makes knowledge available and leaves the response to the person and to Allah.

**Safe Return Completes the Obligation**

The command after hearing is equally important: escort the person to a place of safety. The visitor is not protected only while standing before the Muslim authorities. The protection continues through the journey back to security. In the language of the verse, the Muslim community becomes responsible for the safe completion of the person's passage. The person may reject the message and still be returned safely.

That final duty prevents the audience from becoming a hidden form of captivity. If a ruler could invite an enemy to listen and then keep him indefinitely, the invitation would be a snare. By requiring escort, the Qur'an removes that possibility. Safe conduct has a beginning in the request, a middle in the hearing, and an end at a secure destination. The shape of the rule anticipates a principle recognized in later law: a person who is admitted under protection cannot be delivered back into danger by the very authority that promised to protect him.

**Ignorance Calls for Explanation Before Judgment**

The verse explains the command by saying that these people have no knowledge. The statement identifies a condition, not a permanent defect. Their hostility may be sustained by ignorance of the actual revelation and of the reasons for the Muslim community's actions. If they have not heard the message clearly, allowing them to hear it is part of justice. The Qur'an says, We never punish until We have sent a messenger (17:15). Verse 6 gives that principle a practical form: knowledge is not presumed when a person has a genuine opportunity to receive it.

This diagnosis also disciplines the believer's attitude. A person who disagrees after understanding the argument is not the same as a person who has been taught a caricature. The response to the second is explanation and safety, not contempt. The verse does not excuse violent action by labeling every outsider ignorant, nor does it treat ignorance as proof of bad character. It identifies a gap that the community must help to close.

**A Dispute About Abrogation, Resolved by Practice**

Some commentators asked whether this verse was superseded by later rules about prisoners and warfare. The dominant view preserved it as a specific protection for someone who comes to hear the message, while recognizing that other verses address different categories of captives and combatants. That distinction is sound. A person captured in battle is not automatically the same as a person who requests an audience under safe-conduct. The Qur'an can regulate both cases without forcing one to cancel the other.

The Prophet's practice gives the verse its clearest explanation. He made treaties, accepted envoys, answered questions from opponents, and protected people who came under an assurance of safety. The principle is not that every interaction becomes harmless. It is that the authority to fight does not remove the duty to honor a specific promise of protection. Security given in the name of Allah remains security until the person reaches safety.

**A Standard for Religious Conversation**

For believers today, verse 6 applies first to the public ethics of protection and then to ordinary speech. When someone asks to understand Islam, the response should make understanding possible. That means truthful explanation, patience with difficult questions, and freedom from threats. A conversation conducted through fear may win compliance, but it has not fulfilled the verse's purpose, which is hearing the Word.

The verse also rebukes communities that invite outsiders into a religious space and then treat them as objects to be controlled. The stranger is a listener with rights. He may leave unconvinced. The community's honor lies in keeping its promise after the visitor has heard. Protection, explanation, and safe return form a single moral act. The chapter that contains commands about battle places this command beside them so that no one can mistake strength for the whole religion.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:7

> How can such polytheists have a treaty with Allah and His Messenger, except those you have made a treaty with at the Sacred Mosque? So, as long as they are true to you, be true to them. Indeed Allah loves those who are mindful ˹of Him˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Exposes a Contradiction**

The verse begins with how can, a question that does not seek information. It asks how a continuing treaty can be imagined with people whose conduct has made the treaty meaningless. The question directs attention to the contradiction between a formal promise and a record of betrayal. A covenant with Allah and His Messenger is not a phrase that protects a party while that party destroys the conditions under which it was made.

Yet the question is immediately qualified by an exception. Some treaty partners remain outside the general judgment because they have kept their word. The verse therefore does not say that no polytheist can have a treaty. It asks how such a treaty can continue for those who have made the relationship empty through their own actions, then protects those whose behavior has remained sound. The rhetoric is severe toward treachery and precise toward the faithful.

**A Covenant With God Has a Human Form**

The verse speaks of a treaty with Allah and His Messenger. This wording lifts a political agreement above ordinary convenience. When the Messenger makes a binding arrangement, the believers do not regard it as a private contract that can be adjusted whenever public opinion changes. Allah is named as the ultimate witness and guarantor. Breaking such an agreement is therefore not merely a failed negotiation; it is a violation of an obligation made under divine oversight.

The Qur'an uses similar language for allegiance: Those who pledge allegiance to you pledge allegiance only to Allah; Allah's Hand is above their hands (48:10). That verse does not turn human beings into gods. It teaches that a promise made to the Messenger carries a higher accountability because the Messenger's authority is entrusted by Allah. Verse 7 applies the same principle to a treaty. The parties deal with one another, but the covenant is recorded in a court larger than both parties.

**The Exception Is Based on Steadfast Conduct**

Classical exegetes differed over the historical group identified by the exception. Some associated it with members of Banū Bakr who did not participate in the breach connected to Ḥudaybiyah. Others mentioned Banū Khuzāʿah or particular Qurashī groups who received a period of notice and then changed their position. The reports vary, but the rule that unites them is stated in the verse itself: as long as they remain true, remain true to them.

The reciprocal verbs make the principle memorable. Their upright conduct is answered by upright conduct. This is not a demand that the Muslim side imitate every belief or custom of the partner. It is a demand that the Muslim side match fidelity with fidelity. A community does not become less principled by honoring a promise made to someone of another creed. It becomes more principled because its conduct is governed by Allah rather than by tribal resemblance.

**Taqwā Is Visible in Keeping Faith**

The closing reference to Allah's love for the reverent explains why the verse treats diplomacy as worship. Taqwā means guarding oneself from what displeases Allah. In a treaty, that guarding includes restraint from opportunism. The believer may be tempted to interpret another people's weakness as permission to ignore the agreement. The verse says the opposite: weakness in the other party does not remove the obligation that still stands.

A promise kept only while profitable is not the form of reverence praised here. Taqwā appears when the community honors the agreed duration despite changing calculations. This does not mean that a party must allow an actual breach to continue without response. It means that the response follows evidence, not appetite. Where the terms are being observed, the believer observes them. Where the terms are broken, the believer proceeds under the rules for breach. Precision is part of piety.

**The Sacred Mosque Does Not Cancel the Rule**

The mention of the Sacred Mosque recalls the setting of the treaty and the pilgrimage proclamation. The sanctuary was a place where old tribal hostilities were restrained and agreements could be declared before many witnesses. The location gives the verse a public and solemn background. A treaty connected with that setting cannot be treated as casual speech made in an alley. It had been attached to a recognized place, a recognized season, and a public relationship between communities.

At the same time, the sacred location does not make every arrangement permanent regardless of conduct. The verse's exception clause shows that sanctity and accountability work together. The Mosque is not a charm that turns bad faith into good faith, and the treaty is not a burden that binds one side after the other has openly destroyed it. What remains sacred is the obligation to judge honestly and to keep faith as long as faith remains.

**The Rule Travels Beyond the Original Dispute**

Verse 7 provides a standard for contracts, alliances, and personal promises. Before ending an agreement, ask whether the other party has actually breached it. Before making an accusation, distinguish direct action from rumor and indirect support from mere association. After finding a breach, communicate the change openly rather than taking secret advantage. These habits are not modern additions to the verse; they are the practical shape of its command.

The verse also protects believers from a tribal morality in which loyalty is offered only to one's own. Allah's love is attached to the reverent, not to a particular ethnicity or faction. A person who keeps faith with a non-Muslim treaty partner may be closer to the verse's ideal than someone who speaks constantly about religious loyalty while breaking promises to those under his protection. The question of verse 7 remains sharp: if the treaty is being honored, why would you dishonor it? The answer must be based on evidence, not resentment.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:8

> How ˹can they have a treaty˺? If they were to have the upper hand over you, they would have no respect for kinship or treaty. They only flatter you with their tongues, but their hearts are in denial, and most of them are rebellious.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Power Reveals Whether a Treaty Is Real**

Verse 8 answers the question raised in the previous verse by proposing a test: what would these people do if they gained the upper hand? A treaty that exists only while one side lacks the strength to break it is not secure. The verse does not praise weakness, and it does not condemn every strong party. It exposes a specific character: the party that uses polite words while waiting for the chance to disregard every obligation.

This test reaches beyond the original tribes. Many people appear honest when external pressure makes honesty easy. The deeper question is what they do when enforcement disappears. A promise honored only because betrayal would be costly reveals fear of consequence, not necessarily faithfulness. The verse asks the reader to look at a pattern of conduct under advantage, because power exposes the values that polite circumstances can conceal.

**Kinship and Treaty Cover the Natural and the Chosen**

The two bonds named in the verse cover different kinds of obligation. The first term can refer to kinship or an alliance that creates a bond of mutual protection. The second, dhimmah, is an undertaking that places responsibility upon a person or group. Together they indicate that the hostile party would disregard both inherited ties and formal promises. It would not protect a relative because of blood, nor a treaty partner because of a pledged duty.

The breadth of the charge matters. A person who breaks one promise may have failed in a particular matter. A party that discards every kind of bond has made disregard itself into a policy. Such a party cannot be trusted merely because it speaks the language of agreement. Trust is not built from a vocabulary of loyalty; it is built from conduct toward people whom one could safely betray.

**Mouth and Heart Are Put in Conflict**

The verse describes flattery with the tongue and denial in the heart. It does not teach that all pleasant speech is false, or that inner intention is irrelevant to a contract. Rather, it identifies deliberate speech that is used to conceal refusal. The tongue performs agreement while the heart has already rejected the obligation. This split is the signature of hypocrisy: language is made to reassure the other party while intention prepares to violate him.

The Qur'an often treats the heart as the center of settled purpose, not as a private organ that excuses public behavior. Words still matter because they create promises; what makes these words blameworthy is that they are used without the intention to honor them. The verse thus warns believers not to evaluate trust by charm alone. Pleasant speech can be a sign of good manners, but it can also be a tool of manipulation. Conduct over time reveals which one it is.

**“Most” Refuses an Unjust Generalization**

The verse concludes that most of them are rebellious, not that every individual without exception is identical. That small word matters. The chapter has already protected those who keep their treaties, and the qualification remains in force here. The Qur'an can make a severe judgment about a dominant pattern without erasing the people who do not share it. The exception is not a weakness in the accusation; it is evidence of careful judgment.

This restraint also guards against the believer's tendency to make an enemy morally uniform. Collective language can be necessary when a group acts through a common political structure, but it must not become a license to ignore individual conduct. Some members of a tribe may be guilty leaders, some reluctant followers, and some faithful treaty partners. The Qur'anic description remains tied to what the evidence shows. Even in a passage of confrontation, justice refuses to say more than the case supports.

**The Upper Hand Is a Moral Examination**

The phrase about prevailing supplies a general spiritual lesson. Every advantage is an examination of the person who receives it. A manager with power over an employee, a creditor over a debtor, an adult over a child, or a majority over a minority faces the same question: will strength be used to honor the relationship or to escape it? The verse's hostile parties fail because victory would expose the absence of kinship and treaty in their moral order.

Power also tests the believer. It is easy to read verse 8 as a description of someone else and miss the question directed inward. If a promise can be broken without consequence, would I still keep it? If a weaker party cannot answer me, do I still protect his claim? Allah's standard is not limited to agreements between states. The person who honors a warranty, repays a loan, or keeps a confidence when no one can force him is resisting the very pathology this verse names.

**A Just Response Requires Verification**

The warning does not tell the believers to become suspicious of every word or to answer possible hypocrisy with immediate violence. It tells them to recognize that a treaty has no moral security when one party has shown that it will discard it at the first opportunity. That conclusion should follow a record of behavior, not a feeling. Verse 7's command to remain true as long as the other side remains true still governs the faithful case.

For public leaders, this means distinguishing negotiation from deception and disagreement from treachery. For private believers, it means refusing both gullibility and cynicism. The believer neither worships appearances nor invents betrayal. He watches what happens when power changes hands. The verse's enduring test is simple: when someone has the upper hand, does he become more just because he fears Allah, or more treacherous because he fears no one else? That answer tells you whether the treaty is being honored in the heart as well as on the tongue.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:9

> They chose a fleeting gain over Allah’s revelations, hindering ˹others˺ from His Way. Evil indeed is what they have done!

**Expanded Commentary**

**Revelation Was Treated as a Commodity**

The verse portrays the hostile group as choosing a small worldly return in exchange for Allah's revelations. The image is a transaction, but its moral force lies in the terrible imbalance of the price. Revelation carries truth, guidance, and a person's final direction. The gain that replaces it is brief and limited. The verse does not say that the gain had no attraction. It says that the attraction was too small to justify what was surrendered.

The Qur'an often uses the language of buying and selling to describe moral decisions. People exchange something lasting for something immediate, or they give what cannot be retained in order to receive what cannot be lost. In 9:111, believers are described as selling their lives and wealth for Paradise. Verse 9 presents the counterfeit transaction: revelation is exchanged for influence, security, profit, or the comfort of remaining in charge. Both groups are trading. The difference is what each regards as valuable.

**A Fleeting Gain Can Be Politically Large**

The adjective fleeting does not mean that the worldly benefit was imaginary. Leaders can gain real wealth, prestige, control of shrines, or influence over trade. What makes the price small is the scale against which the Qur'an measures it. A few years of authority cannot compare with the truth of Allah or with the life to come. A political success can be large inside a human system and still be small in the final account.

That evaluation challenges the way power describes itself. A person who benefits from an unjust order rarely calls the benefit temporary. He names it stability, tradition, or necessary compromise. Revelation strips away the flattering vocabulary and asks what has actually been purchased. If a leader keeps a falsehood alive because it protects his office, the office is the price tag. If a community hides guidance because honesty would cost it a market, the market is the price tag. The Qur'an calls the exchange poor because the seller has misunderstood the worth of what he sold.

**Turning Away Also Turns Others Away**

The verse says that they hindered others from Allah's way. The verb can carry two movements: turning away oneself and causing others to turn away. The second makes the offense wider. A private person may reject a truth for his own reasons; a person with authority can organize rejection, shame those who accept guidance, close access to the message, or make obedience look socially impossible.

This distinction protects a necessary balance. The Qur'an does not appoint believers to force every inward conviction. It does hold accountable those who use their influence to prevent others from hearing or following the truth. Verse 6 illustrates the opposite duty: the seeker must be allowed to hear the Word and be escorted safely. Verse 9 describes a system that does the reverse, using power to keep people from the road. The wrongdoing is therefore not only disbelief but obstruction.

**The Market Metaphor Includes a Better Trade**

The same Qur'an that criticizes the bad sale invites a better one. It asks, Who will lend Allah a goodly loan? He will multiply it for him, and he will have a generous reward (57:11). It also announces a commerce that saves from painful punishment: faith in Allah and His Messenger and striving in His way (61:10–11). These passages are not separate decorations. They clarify verse 9 by showing that human life always involves preference and exchange. The question is whether the person trades truth for a short gain or trades temporary possessions for lasting nearness.

The good trade is not a bargain in which Allah needs what the believer owns. It is a merciful way of describing obedience in terms human beings understand. The bad trade is equally compassionate in its warning: the one who sells revelation for advantage is not a shrewd merchant. He is the seller who gives away the only asset that could have saved him.

**The Price Is Paid by the Whole Community**

When leaders trade away revelation, the loss does not remain private. Their followers inherit a distorted map. A population taught that betrayal is normal begins to regard trust as weakness. A child raised to mock faith may never be given a fair chance to understand it. A society that rewards obstruction eventually loses the ability to recognize truth even when it appears plainly. This is why the verse's second clause matters as much as the first. The sale becomes destructive when the seller uses his position to make others enter the same market.

The believer's duty is not merely to avoid personally selling divine guidance. He must also refuse to profit from a system that blocks it. That refusal can take the form of honest speech, protection for a vulnerable person, accurate teaching, or declining a reward tied to deception. Not every application is dramatic. The essence is the same: do not make another person's access to truth the collateral for your comfort.

**A Daily Audit of What Is Being Sold**

The verse invites a practical inventory. What benefit would make me soften a clear principle? Which approval would persuade me to call falsehood wisdom? What do I ask other people to surrender so that I can keep my position? These questions expose the small prices that govern large decisions. The answer may be money, but it may also be reputation, belonging, or the avoidance of an uncomfortable conversation.

The final judgment, Evil indeed is what they have done, is brief because the transaction has already explained itself. No ornate condemnation is needed. They knew what they were exchanging, and they chose a temporary gain over revelation and then invited others to lose the road as well. The believer who remembers this verse learns to distrust any bargain that requires truth to be treated as expendable. Some gains are not gains. They are receipts for what was sold.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:10

> They do not honour the bonds of kinship or treaties with the believers. It is they who are the transgressors.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Indictment Becomes Personal**

Verse 10 completes the preceding description by stating whom the hostile people refuse to honor: any believer with whom they have a bond of kinship or treaty. The wording moves from the community as a whole to the individual believer. No person is too weak, too distant, or too insignificant to deserve the protection created by family or covenant. The enemy's problem is not merely that it has broken a large political arrangement. It has made the believer himself disposable.

This personal focus sharpens the charge. Public betrayal often hides behind large words such as strategy, necessity, or the interest of the tribe. The verse brings the matter down to a person who was owed loyalty. A covenant is always experienced by particular human beings. When a leader breaks it, the injury is carried by families, travelers, merchants, and neighbors. The verse refuses to let an abstract explanation hide the people harmed by the breach.

**The Two Bonds Return**

Kinship and treaty repeat the paired terms from verse 8. One bond is received through family or alliance; the other is formed by a deliberate promise. The Qur'an treats both as morally serious. Blood relation does not make every action acceptable, but neither may it be dismissed whenever it becomes inconvenient. A treaty is not less binding because its partner is outside the believer's religious community. Natural and chosen bonds both create claims that must be respected.

The verse's order also has a wider lesson. Human beings often honor whichever bond best serves them and neglect the other. A person may defend relatives while breaking contracts with strangers, or speak of legal agreements while treating family members harshly. The Qur'an places both under the same moral examination. The believer's character is not divided into a private ethic for kin and a public ethic for outsiders. Allah sees the whole pattern.

**Transgression Means Crossing a Limit**

The final word identifies the perpetrators as transgressors. The Arabic root suggests passing beyond a boundary. That boundary may be a legal term, a promise, or the limit set by justice in conflict. Transgression is not simply strong action. It is action that refuses the line that should govern it. The Qur'an can authorize resistance to aggression while still condemning the person who exceeds the reason and limit of that resistance.

This is why the verse is connected to the rule, Whoever transgresses against you, then transgress against him in the same measure in which he transgressed against you (2:194). The instruction is not a celebration of revenge. It is a demand for proportion. The hostile group in verse 10 has crossed multiple lines at once: family protection, treaty obligation, and the protection owed to believers. The label transgressors describes a settled disregard for limits, not simply the fact that a conflict exists.

**The Believer Is Not Protected Only When Strong**

The phrase with the believers carries an important social meaning. In a tribal environment, protection often depended on the strength of the clan behind a person. A solitary believer or a weak family could become an easy target. Verse 10 denies the aggressor that calculation. The obligation applies to any believer, not only to the believer whose relatives or allies can retaliate.

That principle is reflected in the Prophetic warning about the protected non-Muslim, whose blood may not be violated. The ethical point is broader than one legal category: security is measured by the promise given, not by the victim's ability to defend himself. A community that protects only those who can punish betrayal has not learned the Qur'anic standard. The purpose of covenant is to create safety precisely where power is unequal.

**A General Rule for Public Trust**

The verse applies to institutions as well as tribes. A government that signs an agreement with a small community, a business that accepts a customer's trust, or an organization that promises confidentiality takes on a duty. The stronger party cannot later claim that the weaker party was foolish to rely on the promise. If the agreement was clear and the other party kept it, honoring the bond is a matter of justice before it is a matter of public relations.

The same rule governs family power. Parents must not use kinship as a license for abuse, and children must not use religious language to cancel every duty toward parents. Verse 10 does not turn every bond into an absolute command; it teaches that bonds have weight and cannot be discarded for convenience. When a higher command requires a boundary, the boundary must be explained honestly rather than disguised as though no bond existed.

**Why the Verse Says “They”**

The final statement, it is they who are the transgressors, separates the wrongful act from the identity of every person in the surrounding dispute. The pronoun points back to the people whose conduct has been described. They are not called transgressors because they belong to a race or because they live on one side of a frontier. They are identified by the way they treat bonds and believers. This is a pattern the Qur'an repeatedly uses: moral classification follows action.

For the reader, the verse is both a warning and a safeguard. Do not excuse systematic betrayal as clever politics. Do not answer it by inventing guilt in people who have not done the same. Watch how a person behaves when a promise protects someone weaker than himself. If he honors the bond, he has answered the verse's charge. If he crosses the limit because he can, the verse supplies its own verdict. Justice begins by naming the transgression accurately.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:11

> But if they repent, perform prayer, and pay alms-tax, then they are your brothers in faith. This is how We make the revelations clear for people of knowledge.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Repentance Changes the Social Category**

Verse 11 returns to the condition stated in verse 5, but it now explains the consequence in the language of belonging. Those who repent, establish prayer, and pay the alms-tax become brothers in faith. The verse does not describe a temporary ceasefire or a probationary status. It names a new relationship. The people who were addressed as participants in a hostile order are received into the community once they abandon that order and enter the obligations of Islam.

Repentance here is therefore more than regret. It is a turning expressed in belief and public practice. Prayer directs the person toward Allah, while alms-tax makes the new faith visible in responsibility toward other people. The two acts stand together throughout the Qur'an because worship cannot be reduced to private feeling. A person turns to Allah in prayer and proves that turn by releasing a portion of wealth for the needs of the community.

**Prayer and Alms Form a Public Threshold**

The pairing of prayer and alms-tax is repeated in many Qur'anic passages. It joins the vertical and horizontal sides of religion. Prayer orders the body and heart around Allah; alms-tax orders property around justice and mercy. A person who claims repentance while refusing both forms of commitment has not accepted the full change described by the verse. The requirements are not a test of ethnicity or lineage. They are public signs that the former allegiance has been replaced by submission to Allah.

This does not mean that every spiritual change is instantly perfect. It means that entry into the community is not based on a vague declaration that leaves the person's conduct untouched. The believer prays, gives, and belongs. The verse's clarity protects both sides: the new member knows what brotherhood entails, and the existing community knows what it is receiving. Faith is inward, but it is not invisible in every consequence.

**Brotherhood Is an Obligation, Not a Mood**

The word brothers in faith carries legal and moral weight. It does not merely say that believers should feel warmly toward one another. Brotherhood creates duties of protection, honesty, support, and restraint from harm. The Qur'an elsewhere says that believers are guardians of one another, encouraging right and restraining wrong (9:71). Verse 11 establishes the foundation for that mutual care: when a person joins the faith through repentance and its public duties, he is not placed at the edge of the community forever.

This is a striking answer to the logic of inherited enmity. A former opponent can become a brother, and a former relative in tribe can be joined to strangers by faith. The new relation does not deny the history of conflict; it refuses to make history the final definition of a person. The community must be able to remember what happened without making forgiveness impossible. That balance is part of the clarity promised at the end of the verse.

**The Qiblah Community and the Protection of Life**

Classical jurists drew a major principle from this verse: the people who face the qiblah and practice the public marks of Islam are treated as members of the protected Muslim community. The exact legal discussions became complex, especially when political divisions arose among Muslims, but the verse's central social effect is clear. A person who has entered the covenant of faith is not to be treated as an outsider merely because his previous life was opposed to the believers.

The Prophet's saying, Islam is built upon five, names the testimony, prayer, alms, fasting, and pilgrimage as the visible pillars of the religion (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 8; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 16). Verse 11 highlights prayer and alms because the immediate context concerns the public transition from hostile idolatry to the Muslim community. It does not reduce Islam to two acts; it shows how those acts signify the new shared order.

**Knowledge Sees the Sequence**

The closing statement says that Allah makes the revelations clear for people of knowledge. Knowledge here is not the ability to repeat isolated sentences. It is the ability to see relations among verses. The reader who remembers only the fighting command of verse 5 misses the protection of verse 4, the asylum of verse 6, and the brotherhood of verse 11. The reader who sees the sequence understands that the passage is regulating the movement from hostility to security and then to belonging.

The verse also suggests that knowledge should produce fair classification. The learned person does not make every case identical. He recognizes the treaty keeper, the asylum seeker, the repentant convert, and the continuing aggressor as distinct cases. Knowledge is shown by accurate judgment, not by the loudest certainty.

**Brotherhood Requires a Community That Can Receive**

For modern believers, verse 11 raises a question about the community's memory. Can people who have changed be accepted without being constantly reduced to their worst past? Security and accountability remain necessary, but repentance is not genuine if no new life is allowed to follow it. A community that calls a convert a brother in theory while treating him as permanently suspect has not embodied the verse's promise.

The verse also asks established believers to make their own prayer and alms real. Brotherhood is not a label placed on someone else after conversion. It is a shared discipline. People stand together in prayer, and they share material responsibility through alms. The revelation is clear for those who know because they read both parts at once: repentance turns a person toward Allah, and that turn opens into a community where faith is practiced together.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:12

> But if they break their oaths after making a pledge and attack your faith, then fight the champions of disbelief—who never honour their oaths—so perhaps they will desist.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Breach Can Become an Organized Threat**

Verse 12 addresses a more advanced stage of betrayal. The hostile party has not merely failed to keep a promise; it has broken oaths after making a binding pledge and has attacked the religion of the believers. The two features belong together in the verse. A political breach becomes especially dangerous when it is joined to a campaign aimed at weakening the community's faith and resolve.

The Arabic verb for breaking an oath can evoke the untying of a firmly twisted cord. That picture explains the moral force of the verse. An oath is a bond deliberately strengthened through words. To break it after confirming it is to pull apart something one had just claimed to secure. The addition of attacking the religion shows that the breach is not an accidental failure or a private dispute. It has become an active effort against the community's identity and public order.

**Why the Leaders Are Named**

The command directs attention to the champions or leaders of disbelief. This wording matters. It identifies the people who organize, finance, and direct hostility rather than treating every member of the opposing population as equally responsible. Qur'anic narratives often describe the leading figures of a people as the ones who turn rejection into a social program. Leadership is named because leadership has consequences.

Classical commentators differed over whether the verse's command is restricted to the leaders or extends to the forces acting under them. Their disagreement reflects two concerns. One is that an organized threat cannot be ended by ignoring its directors. The other is that accountability should not be expanded carelessly to those who did not plan the breach. The verse's focus at least establishes a priority: confront the people whose authority keeps the conflict alive, rather than treating every follower as though he authored the policy.

**“They Have No Oaths” Describes the Consequence**

The phrase that they have no oaths does not mean that the written words never existed. It means that their own conduct has stripped those words of protective force. A party cannot invoke an oath as a shield after deliberately destroying the oath's conditions. The legal consequence follows from the breach; it is not an arbitrary cancellation imposed upon a faithful partner.

This principle is familiar in ordinary contracts. A person who repudiates the central promise of an agreement may lose the benefit he expected from the other side's performance. The Qur'anic verse places that principle in a setting where the failed promise also exposes a community to danger. The point is not that every minor disagreement dissolves a treaty. The point is that a decisive breach, joined to hostility, changes the status of the relationship.

**Attack on Faith Is Not Mere Offense**

The phrase attack your faith can be misunderstood if it is reduced to personal hurt feelings. The verse is addressing an organized political situation. Its language concerns leaders who have already broken their oaths and are then attacking the religion. The broader Qur'anic record shows that the Prophet endured insults and accusations without turning every insult into a military case. The decisive issue is the use of speech and influence to generate hostility, incite violence, or destabilize the covenant community.

This is why jurists disagreed over cases involving treaty holders who insulted Islam or the Prophet. Some held that a grave insult joined to breach removed protection; Abū Ḥanīfah's reasoning, as reported in the commentaries, distinguished disbelief as a belief from public speech that threatened political peace. The disagreement shows that the legal tradition did not simply treat every offensive word as the same act. It asked what the speech did within the treaty and whether it formed part of an aggressive campaign.

**The Stated Aim Is Desistance**

The verse ends with perhaps they will desist. This purpose clause limits the command by naming the desired outcome. The goal is cessation of the breach and attack, not endless punishment. If the hostile leadership stops, the reason for fighting has been removed. The word perhaps does not express uncertainty about Allah's knowledge; it describes the community's action as aimed at producing a different behavior in the opponent.

That aim is important for judging conduct in war. Force that exists only to satisfy anger has moved beyond the verse. Force intended to stop a concrete aggression may be lawful within its limits. The end condition must remain visible throughout the conflict. A community that forgets why it began fighting will eventually treat the continuation of fighting as its own justification.

**Leadership, Accountability, and Restraint**

The verse offers a framework for public accountability today. When an institution violates an agreement and uses its platform to recruit hostility, identify the decision-makers, document the breach, and seek the cessation of the harmful policy. Do not punish people merely because they belong to the same population. Do not excuse the leaders because the machinery of harm is complex. Responsibility follows influence.

For believers, the verse also warns against using religious defense as a cover for personal revenge. The Prophet's endurance under insult, the requirement of an actual breach, and the command that the aggressors desist all restrain the believer. Defending faith may require firmness, but firmness is not measured by how much anger is displayed. It is measured by whether the response is truthful, proportionate, and directed toward ending harm. The verse names champions of disbelief so that zeal does not become a substitute for judgment. Its severity is therefore controlled by its reason and its stated end.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:13

> Will you not fight those who have broken their oaths, conspired to expel the Messenger ˹from Mecca˺, and attacked you first? Do you fear them? Allah is more deserving of your fear, if you are ˹true˺ believers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Supplies the Reasons**

Verse 13 asks whether the believers will fight a people and then lists three reasons: broken oaths, a plan to expel the Messenger, and first aggression. The list is the chapter's clearest explanation of the hostile case introduced earlier. The fighting is not left hanging on a vague charge of difference. The verse presents a record of actions and intentions that have made continued submission to the old treaty arrangement impossible.

The first reason points to the destruction of a pledged bond. The second concerns an attack on the Messenger's presence and leadership. The third establishes who initiated the hostilities. Together they describe a threat to both covenant and community. Even if exegetes differed over whether the attempted expulsion refers to the Hijrah plot, later efforts against the Prophet in Madinah, or both, the principle remains: an intention to remove the Messenger by force is itself part of the case.

**First Aggression Matters**

The phrase attacked you first places the community's response in a moral sequence. The believers are not being asked to pretend that all sides began at the same time. The attack on Banū Khuzāʿah by Banū Bakr, with Quraysh support according to the historical reports, is the immediate example connected with the broken Ḥudaybiyah order. Qur'an 22:39 gives the wider principle that permission to fight was given to those who were fought because they were wronged.

This does not mean that being attacked makes every later action permissible. The Qur'an's rule of proportionality still governs. It means that the original responsibility for opening the conflict must be identified accurately. A community that refuses to distinguish aggressor from defender may call its own retaliation unprovoked, while a community that invents first aggression may use victimhood as a weapon. Verse 13 insists on a factual account before a forceful response.

**Fear Is Reordered, Not Denied**

After listing the reasons, the verse asks, do you fear them? The question does not assume that danger feels unreal. Soldiers can fear an army, families can fear expulsion, and a small community can fear the consequences of confronting a stronger power. The command is to reorder fear, not to pretend that the human emotion does not exist. Allah is more deserving of fear because His judgment is final and because obedience to Him cannot be traded for temporary safety.

The same principle appears in the command, Do not fear them; fear Me (5:44), and in the reassurance that Satan frightens his allies, so do not fear them but fear Allah (3:175). These verses do not praise recklessness. They teach that fear must not become the authority that decides whether a duty is obeyed. A believer may feel afraid and still choose the right action. Faith is shown by which fear governs the choice.

**The Rhetorical Question Addresses Hesitation**

Will you not fight is an urging question. It addresses a community that has reasons to hesitate after repeated conflict, exhaustion, and uncertainty. The command does not ignore the cost of action. It answers the cost of inaction. If a treaty-breaking power can attack allies, expel leaders, and strike first without consequence, then the people under threat are not being protected by passivity. The verse confronts the belief that avoiding a decision is always the safer option.

The surrounding passage keeps this urgency from becoming license. Verse 4 commands faithfulness toward those who have kept their treaty. Verse 6 commands protection for the one seeking an audience. Verse 13 therefore concerns a defined group whose conduct has crossed the boundaries named in the text. The believer is urged to respond to actual aggression, not to replace moral analysis with general hostility.

**Fear of Allah Produces Discipline**

The final condition, if you are true believers, connects courage with faith. A person who fears Allah will not be paralyzed by human power, but neither will he be intoxicated by his own power. Divine fear places the believer under limits even while he confronts an enemy. It forbids treachery, cruelty to noncombatants, and the pursuit of vengeance beyond the stated purpose. Courage without taqwā can become another form of rebellion.

The Prophet's conduct at Ḥudaybiyah shows the balance. He accepted terms that many Companions found humiliating because the agreement served a larger good. When the treaty was later broken, he responded decisively, but the response did not erase the distinction between those who breached and those who remained faithful. Fear of Allah allowed patience when patience was right and firmness when firmness became necessary.

**A Test for Modern Moral Courage**

Most readers will not face the battlefield situation of this verse, but its structure still tests them. Whose displeasure prevents you from stating a fact? Whose power persuades you to call an injustice peace? Do you fear losing status more than you fear betraying a trust? These questions transfer the verse's moral logic without pretending that every personal dispute is a war.

The verse does not authorize believers to dramatize their private conflicts as sacred battles. It teaches them to identify facts, protect the vulnerable, and place final accountability before human intimidation. When the record shows broken promises and first aggression, courage may be required. When the record does not show them, fear of Allah should prevent an invented charge. The believer's answer to the question is therefore not loudness. It is a disciplined readiness to act when Allah's justice requires it. Such readiness begins with truthful fear, not with excitement.
Faithful resistance is therefore measured by evidence and restraint. The believer confronts aggression because justice requires protection, while remembering that fear of Allah forbids revenge from becoming the purpose.

---
