## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:14

> ˹So˺ fight them and Allah will punish them at your hands, put them to shame, help you overcome them, and soothe the hearts of the believers—

**Expanded Commentary**

**Human Hands Can Be Instruments of Divine Justice**

Verse 14 attributes several results to Allah while describing the believers' hands as the means through which those results occur. The balance is deliberate. The community must act, but it must not imagine that its action makes it the owner of history. Allah punishes through their hands, puts the aggressors to shame, grants victory, and heals the hearts of the believers. Human agency is real, yet it remains subordinate to divine judgment.

The same relation appears in 8:17: You did not kill them, but Allah killed them; you did not throw when you threw, but Allah threw. That verse does not deny that people struck or threw. It denies that their actions were independent of Allah's will and support. Verse 14 applies the same theology to a campaign. The believers are responsible for obeying the command and for the manner of their conduct; the final outcome belongs to Allah.

**Punishment Is Not a License for Vengeance**

The phrase at your hands can be misused if it is read as a promise that every violent act committed by people who claim divine support must be righteous. The Qur'an never gives human beings permission to turn personal anger into a divine sentence. The hands are instruments only when the command, the cause, and the limits are all sound. The preceding verses have identified treaty breach, aggression, and attempted expulsion. Verse 4 has excluded the faithful treaty partner, and verse 6 has protected the seeker of asylum.

Those conditions matter because justice is not created by attaching God's name to an action. A person who fights for pride, revenge, wealth, or domination cannot make the action sacred by calling it punishment. Divine attribution increases responsibility. If the hands are claimed as instruments of Allah, they must be clean from treachery and excess. The verse's theology should produce humility and restraint, not self-congratulation.

**Shame Exposes a Failed Moral Order**

The verse promises to put the hostile people to shame. This shame is public exposure of a power that imagined itself secure. Their treaty violations and aggression had been presented as strength; defeat reveals them as moral failure. The Qur'an often connects disgrace with the collapse of false claims. A person can preserve an impressive appearance while his conduct is hidden, but a public reversal can show the world what the appearance concealed.

The promise is not a command for believers to enjoy humiliation as a personal pleasure. It is a statement about the end of injustice. Shame belongs to the aggressor's false account of himself, not to the believer's appetite for revenge. The believer should want the threat ended and the truth revealed, while remembering that Allah can also turn the heart of the former enemy. Verse 15 will make that possibility explicit.

**Victory Belongs to the One Who Gives It**

The words help you overcome them protect the community from thinking that victory is a proof of its own superiority. An army may be better organized, larger, or more experienced, but none of those means guarantees the result. The Qur'an recalls at Ḥunayn that great numbers did not help the believers when they relied on them. The power that matters is Allah's help, received through lawful means.

This does not cancel preparation. A community must plan, equip itself, and obey its leaders. Reliance on Allah is not a refusal to use means. It is the refusal to worship means. When victory comes, the believer credits Allah without denying the work performed by human beings. When defeat comes, he examines the community's conduct without concluding that Allah has no power. Both outcomes remain within a wider wisdom.

**Healing the Hearts of the Wronged**

The verse's gentlest phrase is the promise to soothe the hearts of the believers. The immediate historical reference is connected by exegetes to Banū Khuzāʿah, who suffered in the attack that broke the Ḥudaybiyah arrangement. Their pain was not merely strategic. They had been betrayed, exposed, and left to carry the memory of those killed. The Qur'an recognizes that justice has an inward effect. It can relieve a wound created by public wrong.

That recognition does not glorify rage. It distinguishes between anger that signals an injustice and hatred that seeks endless harm. A community may need to restore safety and dignity before its members can release the anger. Private revenge often leaves the wound open because it is uncontrolled and cannot establish a trustworthy account. Justice carried out under a lawful order can begin to heal because it tells the injured that their suffering was seen and that the aggressor's power was not the final word.

**The Four Results Form a Whole**

Punishment, shame, victory, and healing address four dimensions of the conflict. Punishment answers the wrongdoing. Shame answers the public claim of the wrongdoer. Victory answers the community's material danger. Healing answers the wounded interior of the believers. The verse does not reduce war to one dimension. A military decision can be successful on the battlefield while leaving injustice unaddressed or trauma untreated. Allah's promised result is more complete.

For leaders, this means that ending a threat is not enough if the harmed people are abandoned afterward. For believers, it means that healing is not achieved by feeding rage. The community must seek a just outcome, care for those who suffered, and remain open to repentance among former enemies. Verse 14 joins external action to internal repair so that the response does not reproduce the injury it was meant to end.

**Responsible Action in the Present**

The modern application begins with refusing false claims of divine authorization. Before acting against wrongdoing, identify the actual harm, the lawful authority, the innocent people who may be affected, and the conditions for stopping. Then act with the humility of an instrument rather than the pride of an owner. The believer may work to expose corruption, protect victims, or dismantle an abusive system, but he must not confuse intensity with justice.

The verse promises that Allah can use human hands without making human beings gods. That is its enduring balance. Act when duty requires action; attribute the power and result to Allah; and seek, alongside victory, the healing of those who were wronged. A hand that claims to punish without caring for the wounded has not understood the whole verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:15

> removing rage from their hearts. And Allah pardons whoever He wills. For Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Justice Reaches the Interior**

Verse 15 completes the healing promised in the previous verse by naming the emotion to be removed: rage from the hearts. The Qur'an does not treat the injured believers as if they should feel nothing after betrayal and violence. It acknowledges that anger has entered them. The promise is not simply that the external enemy will be defeated, but that the believers will eventually be freed from the inner heat that the wrong produced.

The word for rage suggests a fierce, pressured anger rather than a passing irritation. Such anger can preserve memory of injustice, but it can also take over the person who carries it. By saying that Allah removes it, the verse places emotional healing within divine care. The believers are not told to justify the wrong, and they are not told to nurse the anger forever. Justice addresses the wrong; mercy can then release the heart from being governed by it.

**Anger Can Point to Justice Without Becoming Its Master**

A community that feels no anger at betrayal may have lost its sense of moral reality. The verse's promise to remove rage implies that the emotion arose in response to something that mattered. The wronged people need not call the attack harmless in order to become peaceful. At the same time, anger is a poor ruler. It exaggerates, seeks new targets, and makes the original injury a reason for fresh injury. Divine healing is not the suppression of moral concern. It is the restoration of proportion.

This distinction matters for the believer's personal life. A person can be right about a wrong and still be damaged by the way he continues to carry it. The Qur'an's prayer, Our Lord, forgive us and our brothers who preceded us in faith, and do not place in our hearts resentment toward those who believe (59:10), shows that the heart's purification is a separate need from the judgment of an act. One can name injustice accurately and still ask Allah to remove its poison.

**The Door of Pardon Opens After the Sentence**

The verse then turns from the rage of the believers to the possibility that Allah may pardon whoever He wills. The transition is important. Punishment of aggression and pardon of a repentant person are not opposites. A person may have been part of a hostile group and later abandon that position. Allah's pardon is not a denial of the harm committed. It is a new judgment made possible by repentance, changed conduct, and divine mercy.

The root of the word for pardon is related to the sūrah's central language of turning. Human beings turn back, and Allah turns toward them. This relationship is visible in the chapter's opening title, al-Tawbah, and in the repeated invitations to repent. Even after the verse has promised punishment and victory, it does not declare that every former enemy is beyond return. The door remains open because Allah's mercy is not limited by the believer's assumption about who can change.

**Knowledge and Wisdom Govern the Pardon**

Allah is named All-Knowing and All-Wise at the end. His knowledge means that no hidden motive is missed. A false conversion cannot deceive Him, and a sincere return cannot be overlooked. His wisdom means that pardon is not random indulgence. It belongs within an order in which repentance, justice, and the protection of the community are all considered.

These names also guide human judgment. Believers do not know every inward state, so they should not claim to possess Allah's final knowledge of people. They may evaluate conduct and protect the community, but they should leave ultimate classification to the One who knows what lies behind the act. Nor should mercy be granted in a way that places victims in renewed danger. Wisdom joins forgiveness to discernment. Knowledge prevents naivety; mercy prevents permanent hatred.

**A Community Must Let Healing Finish**

The promise to remove rage suggests that the community should not preserve anger as a political resource after the danger has passed. Leaders can keep people mobilized by repeating an old injury, and individuals can build identity around grievance. Verse 15 points toward another end. The harm must be addressed, but the heart should not be required to remain in an emergency forever.

This is not a command to force survivors into instant reconciliation. Healing cannot be ordered by a slogan. It is a gift Allah grants through truth, justice, safety, and time. The community can support that gift by honoring the injured, refusing denial, and preventing new harm. It can also support it by allowing those who genuinely repent to become different from what they were. A society that refuses both accountability and pardon traps everyone in the original wound.

**The Verse for a Person Carrying Old Anger**

For the individual believer, verse 15 offers a precise prayer. Do not ask only for the strength to hide anger. Ask Allah to remove its hold after the truth has been faced. A person may need to state what happened, seek restitution, create a boundary, or involve a just authority. None of these steps contradicts forgiveness. They can be the means through which the heart stops confusing surrender to rage with loyalty to the truth.

The verse also offers hope to the one who fears that his anger has become part of his identity. Allah can remove what the person cannot remove by willpower alone. He knows the wound and the wisdom of the cure. The final names are therefore not a distant conclusion. They are the assurance that justice is not blind and mercy is not careless. The God who sees the injury can bring the heart beyond the injury without asking it to call the injury good.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:16

> Do you ˹believers˺ think that you will be left without Allah proving who among you ˹truly˺ struggles ˹in His cause˺ and never takes trusted allies other than Allah, His Messenger, or the believers? And Allah is All-Aware of what you do.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Faith Is Not Left Untested**

The verse opens with a question that challenges a false expectation: do believers think they will be left alone? The question is not about abandonment by Allah. It is about being left without a trial that reveals what the claim of faith means in practice. A community may speak of loyalty, sacrifice, and reliance on Allah while circumstances remain easy. The test shows whether those words govern real choices.

The Qur'an states the same principle in 29:2–3: Do people think they will be left to say, We believe, and they will not be tested? Testing does not give Allah information He lacked. It brings hidden commitments into visible action. A person learns what he trusts when competing demands arrive, and a community learns its true priorities when comfort, family, money, or safety pull against obedience.

**Striving Is a Revealing Act**

The verse asks who among the believers truly strives in Allah's cause. Striving here belongs to the broad field of effort and sacrifice, though the immediate setting includes the obligations of defending the community. The point is not that every person must perform the same task. It is that faith cannot remain a claim detached from cost. Someone who believes that Allah's command matters will eventually spend time, property, courage, or status on that belief.

This is why the sūrah repeatedly joins wealth and life. Wealth shows what a person is willing to release; life or self shows what he is willing to risk. The balance is not measured by public drama alone. A scholar's labor, a caregiver's endurance, and a truthful person's loss of status may all be forms of striving when they are directed toward Allah. The test is whether the effort is real and whether its purpose is God's cause rather than the ego's display.

**The Inner Circle of Trust**

The verse also asks who takes no trusted allies apart from Allah, His Messenger, or the believers. The word for trusted ally can mean a close confidant, someone admitted into the inner counsel. The verse is not prohibiting ordinary kindness, fairness, trade, or peaceful cooperation with people outside the faith. It is examining the seat of deepest loyalty: whose judgment shapes the believer's identity and whose approval decides his obedience?

That distinction matters because a person can be surrounded by many people while reserving ultimate allegiance for Allah. The Messenger embodies the divine guidance in human history, and the believers are the community with whom that guidance is lived. A friend of another faith may be trustworthy and loved, yet the believer does not hand over the authority to redefine what Allah has made true. The verse concerns the government of the heart, not the elimination of social life.

**A Trial Makes Allegiance Visible**

The upcoming expedition to Tabūk gave this question a concrete setting. The journey was long, the weather severe, resources limited, and the Byzantine frontier created fear. These pressures separated people who merely approved of Islam from people willing to follow the Messenger at personal cost. The verse comes before the detailed accounts of those who stayed behind, the truthful who confessed, and the hypocrites who manufactured excuses. It is a preface to the sorting that events will perform.

A trial can reveal loyalty without making the trial itself good. Hunger, fear, and loss are not virtues merely because they are painful. Their value depends on the response they draw from a person. One believer becomes more honest, generous, and dependent on Allah; another becomes resentful or deceptive. The event is the same kind of instrument, but the character it reveals differs.

**Allah’s Knowledge Does Not Need the Test**

The closing statement, Allah is All-Aware of what you do, prevents a mistaken reading of the phrase proving. Allah does not conduct an examination because He is uncertain. The trial makes the hidden visible in history, before the community and before the person himself. It also establishes the basis on which reward, correction, and accountability can appear in the created world.

This is similar to a teacher who knows a student's ability but still gives an examination. The result does not inform the teacher of an unknown reality; it displays the student's work and gives the student a record of his condition. Divine knowledge remains complete before, during, and after the trial. Human responsibility remains real because the person chooses what he does under the pressure.

**How the Verse Prevents Complacency**

The verse is not meant to make believers search for persecution or invent tests of one another. It removes complacency and replaces it with honest readiness. A comfortable religious identity is not the same as a tested faith. The believer should ask which loyalties he protects when obedience becomes costly, which people he trusts with his conscience, and what kind of service he is willing to perform when no recognition is offered.

The answer will not be measured by slogans. Allah sees what is done. The community should therefore avoid judging hidden hearts while still taking public actions seriously. Someone may be weak yet sincere; another may be eloquent yet disloyal. Verse 16 places both the test and the final knowledge with Allah. The believer's task is to respond honestly when the test arrives, not to perform a religious identity for the crowd. The unseen loyalty becomes visible through the choices that pressure produces, especially when comfort and obedience point in different directions.
The test separates a sincere claim from a convenient association. Allah makes the hidden loyalty visible through choices, especially when obedience costs friendship, safety, or reputation.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:17

> It is not for the polytheists to maintain the mosques of Allah while they openly profess disbelief. Their deeds are void, and they will be in the Fire forever.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sacred Building Requires a Sacred Purpose**

The verse says that polytheists are not to maintain the mosques of Allah while openly professing disbelief. The verb for maintain can include building, repairing, financing, filling, and administering a place. The issue is therefore not only who may enter a mosque. It is who may claim custodianship over an institution whose defining purpose is worship of the One God.

A mosque is not sacred merely because it has walls, a roof, or a respected history. Its identity comes from the worship for which it exists. If a person publicly denies that foundation while claiming authority over the house, the contradiction becomes institutional. The verse addresses that contradiction. It does not deny that non-Muslims may encounter Muslims, ask questions, or receive protection. Verse 6 has already required an audience and safe escort. It distinguishes access to a place from control over its religious purpose.

**“Bearing Witness Against Themselves”**

The verse describes the polytheists as bearing witness against themselves through disbelief. Their public creed is evidence that they do not affirm the One whose house they seek to maintain. This does not mean that every act of charity or physical repair has no worldly benefit. A person may repair a building and make it safer. The verse concerns the religious status and ultimate meaning of the deed when the foundation of worship is rejected.

The Qur'an often says that deeds can come to nothing when their basis is disbelief or when they are directed to other than Allah. In 39:65, association is described as causing deeds to be nullified. The point is not that Allah fails to see the labor. It is that labor performed as a substitute for faith cannot create the spiritual standing the worker imagines. External service cannot repair an inward denial of the One to whom the service is supposed to be offered.

**The Historical Transition of the Sacred Mosque**

The immediate setting involved the end of pagan pilgrimage practices around the Sacred Mosque after the conquest of Makkah. The old custodial order had connected the sanctuary with tribal prestige, pilgrimage revenue, and inherited offices. Islam did not simply measure the holiness of those offices by the fact that they were old. It asked whether the institution served tawḥīd or protected a religious economy built around idols.

This transition did not require the destruction of every family who had held an office. The Prophet returned the key of the Kaʿbah to ʿUthmān ibn Ṭalḥah, according to the well-known reports, while redefining the purpose and authority of the sanctuary. The example clarifies verse 17. The issue is not personal humiliation of a former custodian. It is the transfer of religious legitimacy from inherited prestige to faithful service of Allah.

**Maintenance Is More Than Money**

The verse's language includes more than construction funds. A person maintains a mosque by shaping its teaching, deciding who is welcomed, preserving prayer, and protecting the institution from being used for another purpose. Someone may donate generously yet turn the mosque into a platform for factional influence. Another may sweep its floor, teach Qur'an, or keep its doors open with no public recognition. The verse asks what kind of maintenance is being performed and for whose sake.

This is why the next verse lists belief, prayer, alms-tax, and fear of Allah as qualifications for true maintenance. Verse 17 establishes the negative boundary; verse 18 explains the positive character. The two verses should be read together. A building cannot be judged only by its donor's name or its architectural beauty. Its maintenance must preserve the worship, justice, and God-consciousness that make it a mosque.

**Deeds and the Hereafter**

The statement that their deeds are void and that they will remain in the Fire is an ultimate judgment tied to open disbelief. Believers should not casually apply this verdict to people whose inward state they do not know. The verse addresses a public condition and leaves the final account to Allah. Its severity warns that religious service is not a substitute for worship, and that public management of sacred things cannot conceal a settled denial of their Lord.

This warning also applies to believers who treat institutional service as a shield from accountability. A Muslim committee member may maintain a building while betraying its trust. A donor may fund a mosque while using the gift to control the imam. The verse's broader logic asks whether the work truly serves Allah. Religious form does not automatically make a deed sound.

**The Mosque as a Measure of Community Identity**

For modern communities, verse 17 should be used as a criterion, not as a weapon against ordinary visitors. A mosque can welcome a seeker, protect a neighbor, and offer the truth without handing over its religious definition to someone who rejects it. Hospitality and custodianship are different responsibilities. The first may be extended widely; the second must preserve the purpose of the institution.

The verse finally turns the believer's attention from buildings to foundations. Who decides what is taught? What values govern the budget? Are the vulnerable protected, and is prayer central? A mosque maintained for reputation, faction, or profit may look impressive while losing its meaning. The house of Allah is maintained properly when its people confess the One to whom the house belongs and let that confession govern the institution's life. Without that confession, maintenance has preserved a shell but not the sanctuary's meaning. The building remains, but its central act of worship has been displaced.
The lesson is not to judge a sacred building by its walls alone. Worship becomes meaningful when the heart and the institution both acknowledge Allah's oneness and serve His guidance.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:18

> The mosques of Allah should only be maintained by those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, pay alms-tax, and fear none but Allah. It is right to hope that they will be among the ˹truly˺ guided.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Positive Standard for Custody**

Verse 18 answers the boundary established in verse 17 by describing who should maintain the mosques of Allah. Four qualities are named. The custodian believes in Allah and the Last Day, establishes prayer, pays alms-tax, and fears none but Allah. The list moves from conviction to practice, from worship to social responsibility, and from both to moral courage. A mosque's true maintenance requires all four dimensions.

Belief in Allah gives the institution its foundation. Belief in the Last Day prevents the custodian from treating short-term popularity as the final measure. Prayer keeps the building centered on worship rather than on administration. Alms-tax tests whether religious leadership recognizes the needs of people. Fear of Allah protects the institution when donors, officials, or crowds demand a compromise. The verse describes a whole character, not a single qualification.

**Faith and the Last Day Set the Direction**

The first two qualities name the horizon of the custodian's work. Belief in Allah means that the mosque belongs to Him and that its teaching cannot be remade according to private preference. Belief in the Last Day means that every decision will be judged beyond the institution's public reputation. Together they protect against two forms of corruption: turning religion into personal property and treating worldly success as proof of divine approval.

A mosque may be wealthy, crowded, and admired while its decisions are driven by fear of losing status. Verse 18 asks a deeper question. Are those responsible for the house acting before Allah's judgment? The Last Day makes hidden motives relevant. A small act of honest maintenance may be more valuable than a grand project designed to attract praise. The custodian serves under an account that cannot be managed by public relations.

**Prayer and Alms Keep Worship Whole**

Prayer and alms-tax repeat the pair from verse 11. Prayer establishes regular orientation toward Allah; alms-tax establishes a regular duty toward people. The mosque is not maintained by prayer if it becomes indifferent to poverty, and it is not maintained by charity if prayer is displaced by social prestige. The two practices prevent the institution from becoming either a private spiritual club or a secular charity wearing religious language.

The word establish suggests more than occasional performance. Prayer is given a stable place in the rhythm of the community. Likewise, alms-tax is a structured obligation, not only a spontaneous act of generosity when the donor feels inclined. A mosque that helps its people pray and fulfills its material responsibilities is alive in the sense intended by the verse. Its walls are maintained because its purpose is maintained.

**Fear None but Allah Protects the Message**

The final qualification is fear of none but Allah. It does not demand that a person feel no natural concern about danger or loss. It means that no human threat is allowed to overrule a known duty. Mosque leaders often depend on donors, congregants, institutions, or officials. Those relationships can be managed wisely without making their approval the source of truth.

This condition links the verse with 9:13, where the believers are told that Allah is more deserving of fear than the hostile army. The same fear-audit applies inside a mosque. A custodian who speaks firmly to enemies but changes the religion to keep a wealthy supporter has not met the verse's standard. Courage is not loudness. It is the freedom to do what Allah requires when another person can punish or reward you.

**“It Is Right to Hope” Is Humility**

The verse ends by saying that such people may be among the guided. The expression of hope is striking. Those who meet the listed qualities are not told to congratulate themselves as though guidance were a possession they had earned beyond question. The language keeps humility alive. They have taken the means of guidance, but they remain dependent on Allah to preserve and complete it.

Classical exegetes discussed the force of this expression, including the principle that Allah's promise is certain even when it is phrased as hope. Whether read as promise or gracious encouragement, the effect is the same: service in a mosque should produce gratitude rather than entitlement. The custodian does not say, I deserve to lead because I have built. He asks Allah to make the work a means of guidance.

**The Institution Must Reflect the People Named**

A community can test its institutions by the four qualities of this verse. Does its leadership confess Allah and answer to the Last Day? Are prayer and alms central rather than decorative? Can its teachers correct wealthy or powerful people when necessary? Do its budgets, hiring decisions, and public statements show fear of Allah more than fear of losing applause?

The questions apply to large mosques and small prayer rooms, schools, charities, and households. Maintenance includes cleaning, but it also includes protecting the moral purpose of the place. A mosque may need modern management, sound finances, and professional administration. None of those replaces the character named here. They become useful when they serve it.

**A Calling for Ordinary Servants**

Verse 18 does not limit the maintenance of Allah's mosques to famous scholars. The person who opens the doors, teaches a child, pays the light bill, feeds a traveler, or quietly gives to the poor can participate in this work. The standard is not celebrity. It is a combination of faith, worship, service, and courage.

For believers, that makes the verse both demanding and hopeful. The mosque is not maintained by a building committee alone. Every worshipper contributes to its atmosphere. The person who prays, gives, refuses to compromise truth, and remembers the Last Day is helping the house become what its name claims. Guidance is hoped for by such people because they are not merely preserving a structure. They are preserving a place where Allah is known.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:19

> Do you ˹pagans˺ consider providing the pilgrims with water and maintaining the Sacred Mosque as equal to believing in Allah and the Last Day and struggling in the cause of Allah? They are not equal in Allah’s sight. And Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Good Service Does Not Erase a False Foundation**

Verse 19 challenges a comparison between two kinds of religious honor. Providing water to pilgrims and maintaining the Sacred Mosque were real services with a long history. Believing in Allah and the Last Day and striving in His cause are also real actions, but they involve the whole direction of a person's life. The verse asks whether the two can be placed on the same level. Its answer is no.

The point is not that giving water is worthless. Pilgrims need water, and caring for sacred visitors can be generous and difficult work. The point is that an honorable service cannot substitute for the faith that gives the service its proper aim. A person may maintain the external form of a sanctuary while rejecting the One worshipped there. He may then claim that his office proves his superiority. The verse corrects that claim without denying the usefulness of the service itself.

**The Historical Dispute Behind the Comparison**

Early reports connect the verse with a dispute about which act deserved the highest merit. Al-ʿAbbās, who was associated with providing water to pilgrims, and ʿAlī, who had taken part in early struggles for Islam, are mentioned in one account. Another report describes a broader discussion among Muslims near the Prophet's pulpit. The details differ, and al-Qurṭubī allowed that a verse already revealed may have been applied to a later dispute. The common issue is clear: people were ranking visible religious offices against costly commitment.

That issue is not confined to the first community. Religious institutions often honor the donor, caretaker, or public official because the role is visible. The person who sacrifices reputation, safety, wealth, or comfort may receive less recognition because his service cannot be displayed as an office. Verse 19 provides a divine correction to the community's scale. It does not reject public service. It refuses to let public service hide the absence of faith and sacrifice.

**The Two Sides of the Higher Comparison**

The second side of the comparison contains belief in Allah, belief in the Last Day, and striving in Allah's cause. Belief gives the action a center, the Last Day gives it a final horizon, and striving gives it a cost. Together they describe a person who is not merely preserving a religious symbol but allowing the truth to reorder his life.

Striving does not have one form in every circumstance. In the original setting it included defending the community and spending for its survival. In other settings it may include seeking knowledge, resisting injustice, serving the poor, or giving up a profitable falsehood. What these forms share is expenditure. The person gives something that the ego would prefer to retain. That cost reveals whether faith is a decoration or a governing commitment.

**Allah’s Scale Is Not the Crowd’s Scale**

The verse says the acts are not equal in Allah's sight. Human beings see the office, the crowd, the building, and the public honor. Allah sees faith, intention, sacrifice, and what the act actually serves. This difference explains why a modest contribution can outweigh a large donation. It also explains why a magnificent religious project can be spiritually empty if it is built to protect status or idolatry.

The Qur'an's language of scales appears elsewhere: Are those who know equal to those who do not know? (39:9). Equality is not assumed merely because two acts look religious from outside. The divine scale considers proportion, foundation, and consequence. That knowledge should make believers humble. They can admire useful service without declaring the servant superior to the person who carried a heavier burden for Allah.

**Wrongdoing Includes Misplaced Value**

The final clause calls the mistaken group wrongdoers and says that Allah does not guide them. Wrongdoing in Qur'anic language includes placing a thing where it does not belong. Here the error is a misplacement of value. Sacred maintenance has been placed above faith and struggle, perhaps because it is safer, more prestigious, or more compatible with the old order. The problem is not that the service exists. It is that the service has been made the measure of righteousness while the foundation is denied.

The same mistake occurs when a person values religious appearance above truthfulness, public charity above private justice, or ritual prestige above obedience. The verse teaches believers to ask what their acts are being used to prove. If the act is meant to purchase exemption from faith, it cannot provide it. If it is offered within faith, it may become noble and may be part of the striving the verse praises.

**Service and Sacrifice Should Be Joined**

The balanced reading does not force a choice between caring for pilgrims and striving in Allah's cause. The problem is treating them as equal when one is being used to replace the other. A believer may provide water, maintain a mosque, and strive with wealth and self. The later verses praise believers who combine belief, emigration, and struggle. Service becomes stronger when it is joined to faith; faith becomes visible through service and sacrifice.

For modern communities, the verse calls for a better ranking system. Honor those who maintain buildings, but also honor those who teach, protect, give, and accept loss for truth. Do not let a donor's name or an inherited office become evidence of spiritual rank. Ask whether the institution's visible work is supported by belief in Allah and the Last Day. The divine scale is asking that question even when the community prefers easier measurements.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:20

> Those who have believed, emigrated, and strived in the cause of Allah with their wealth and their lives are greater in rank in the sight of Allah. It is they who will triumph.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Movements Define the Higher Rank**

Verse 20 names the people who stand above the merely respected religious office described in the previous verse. They believe, emigrate, and strive in Allah's cause with wealth and lives. The sequence describes a movement from conviction to separation and then to expenditure. Faith gives the direction. Emigration shows that the direction has become more important than inherited comfort. Striving shows that the new commitment continues to cost something after the move.

The verse does not present emigration as a romantic journey. For the first Muslims, leaving Makkah could mean abandoning property, family protection, trade, and the status provided by one's clan. The Muhājirūn went to Madinah because faith required a new social home. The action created a community in which the believer's loyalty was no longer governed by the old tribal order. Their movement was therefore geographical and moral at once.

**Belief Comes Before Expenditure**

The order begins with those who have believed. Wealth and life do not create the rank by themselves. A person can give money for prestige, fight for power, or migrate for profit. The verse connects the visible sacrifices to belief in Allah, so the sacrifice has a truthful purpose. This protects striving from becoming another form of ambition.

Belief also gives the believer a source of endurance. If the cause were only political, loss would eventually make the effort seem pointless. Faith in Allah and the Last Day allows a person to value obedience even when the worldly result is uncertain. That does not make planning unnecessary. It changes what counts as success. The believer can accept a difficult outcome without calling obedience a failure, because Allah's judgment extends beyond the immediate report.

**Emigration Is a Transfer of Loyalty**

The word emigrate recalls the Hijrah, which became the beginning of the Islamic calendar. The calendar's starting point is not a coronation or a victory. It is a community leaving danger and building a life around faith. This choice explains why emigration appears between belief and striving. It is the bridge by which inward conviction becomes a new public arrangement.

In later circumstances, the moral meaning of emigration can appear in different forms. A believer may leave a place where worship is impossible, abandon a corrupt livelihood, move away from an abusive influence, or change a social circle that keeps him from Allah. These applications should not erase the historical meaning of the Hijrah, but they show the principle's reach. Emigration means refusing to let inherited surroundings have the final authority over one's relation to Allah.

**Wealth and Life Are Both Spent**

The verse joins wealth and lives, preventing a narrow idea of sacrifice. Some people are willing to give money but not time, courage, or reputation. Others speak bravely while refusing material responsibility. The Qur'anic description includes both forms of expenditure. Wealth supports the community's needs; the self is placed at risk in obedience. Neither is a substitute for the other when both are possible and required.

The pairing also creates a safeguard against spiritual boasting. A person cannot claim that his financial gift excuses him from moral courage, nor that his courage excuses him from generosity. The believers at the center of the sūrah made their resources serve their convictions. That pattern continues in the Qur'an's description of the good trade: faith and striving with wealth and selves are mentioned together (61:10–11).

**Rank Is Assigned by Allah**

The verse says these people are greater in rank in the sight of Allah. Their rank is not established by public applause, lineage, or the office they hold. Allah sees the cost, intention, and faithfulness of the act. The phrase in His sight also reminds the believer that the highest status may be hidden in this life. Someone unknown to a crowd may be honored with Allah because he gave what the crowd never saw.

The Qur'an elsewhere speaks of degrees for those who strive with wealth and selves (4:95–96). Degrees do not mean that every believer without the same opportunity is worthless. They mean that Allah recognizes differences in effort, sacrifice, and circumstance. A person who gives much from much may not be equal to one who gives little from little, and a person who bears a costly duty may not be equal to one who chooses the easy path. The scale is exact and merciful because it knows what each person could actually give.

**Triumph Is More Than Winning a Campaign**

The verse calls this group triumphant. The root of triumph can carry the sense of escaping danger and arriving at a good outcome. In the Qur'anic frame, triumph may include victory in this world, but it cannot be confined to it. Some who emigrate lose property. Some who strive are killed. Yet the verse calls them successful because their lives have reached the purpose for which they were made.

This is not a way to rename every defeat as victory. It is a way to place worldly outcomes in a larger account. A believer can grieve loss and still know that obedience was not wasted. Conversely, a person can win public power and still be a loser if the victory required selling revelation. The preceding verses rejected religious status without faith; verse 20 identifies the triumph of those who gave faith a costly form.

**The Rank Remains Open Through Faithful Effort**

For modern believers, the verse is not a command to imitate the first Muhājirūn in circumstances that no longer exist. It is an invitation to ask what must be left, carried, and spent for Allah now. Perhaps it is a dishonest income, a group that mocks obedience, a comfort that keeps the believer from serving, or a fear of losing approval. The answer will differ, but the pattern remains: believe, move toward the community of faith, and spend what Allah has entrusted.

The verse places the final verdict with Allah. That keeps sacrifice from becoming a claim of superiority over others. The one who strives is still a servant hoping for acceptance. He does not despise the person whose duty is different, and he does not turn his own effort into a new idol. Triumph belongs to those whose wealth and lives have been directed to Allah and whose rank is received from Him alone.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:21

> Their Lord gives them good news of His mercy, pleasure, and Gardens with everlasting bliss,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Lord Himself Announces the Reward**

Verse 21 presents the reward of those who believe, emigrate, and strive, and it begins with an intimate action: their Lord gives them good news. The title Lord, rabb, carries the sense of the One who owns, nurtures, and brings a thing to completion. The people who spent their wealth and lives are not abandoned to guess whether the sacrifice mattered. Their Lord personally announces what awaits them.

Good news is not an ornament added after the command. It sustains the person who must act before seeing the result. A community asked to leave homes, bear hardship, and risk life needs more than a bare order. It needs a true account of what its obedience is moving toward. The verse supplies that account in three linked gifts: mercy, pleasure, and Gardens of lasting bliss. The order teaches that the reward is not merely a collection of delights. It is a relationship with Allah that gives every delight its meaning.

**Mercy Comes Before the Garden**

The first gift is mercy from Him. Mercy is the covering of failure, the acceptance that makes a servant fit for nearness, and the care by which Allah brings a person through the dangers of the journey. The believer's striving is never a price that forces Paradise from Allah. It is itself made possible by mercy, and its defects are received through mercy. The verse therefore prevents the worshipper from turning sacrifice into a claim of ownership.

A person may leave home sincerely and still carry fear, weakness, or an imperfect intention. Allah's mercy does not make obedience unnecessary; it makes obedience fruitful despite human limitation. The Qur'an repeatedly joins forgiveness and reward because a servant needs both. He needs the wrong covered and the good accepted. Without mercy, the believer would examine his effort and despair at what is missing. With mercy, he works seriously while depending on the generosity of the Lord who knows his effort better than he knows it himself.

**Divine Pleasure Is Greater Than Possession**

The second gift is pleasure, riḍwān. This word points to Allah's approval and contentment with the servant. It is more personal than a reward placed before a person. A person can receive a gift from someone while remaining distant from the giver. Riḍwān means that the giver's relationship with the servant has itself become a source of joy.

The Qur'an later says that Allah's pleasure is greater than the Gardens and their pleasures (9:72). Verse 21 introduces that hierarchy here. The believer wants Paradise because it is Allah's promised home, but the highest desire is not the landscape without the Lord. The heart that has learned to sacrifice for Allah wants to be received by Him. The Garden is blissful because Allah's pleasure surrounds it. Without that pleasure, abundance would still leave the deepest hunger untouched.

**Naʿīm Names a Life Without Corrosion**

The final gift is Gardens with everlasting bliss. The word naʿīm suggests ease, flourishing, and enjoyment free from the strain that marks earthly pleasure. Worldly joy can be real, but it is exposed to loss, guilt, interruption, boredom, illness, and death. The Qur'anic promise does not merely increase the intensity of pleasure. It removes the defects that make created pleasure unstable.

The plural Gardens suggests abundance and variety, but the verse's emphasis rests on lasting quality rather than on a catalogue of objects. A gift that ends cannot provide complete rest to a heart that knows it will end. Everlasting bliss answers that weakness. The emigrant who left a house, the striver who spent wealth, and the believer who accepted danger are promised a dwelling where no faithful sacrifice becomes a source of regret.

**Good News Makes Sacrifice Intelligible**

The verse also shows how revelation speaks to human motivation. Some people object to reward as though a believer should act without hope of benefit. The Qur'an does not share that assumption. It knows that human beings are moved by love, fear, and hope, and it purifies those motives rather than pretending they do not exist. Mercy answers the fear of rejection. Pleasure answers the desire to be loved by Allah. Gardens answer the longing for secure joy.

Mature faith does not despise these gifts. It learns to love the Giver through the gifts. A child may first desire a reward because it is pleasant; a mature servant desires it because it is from Allah and because it represents His promise. The order of verse 21 allows that growth. The believer receives mercy, seeks divine pleasure, and rejoices in the Garden as the home prepared by the Lord.

**A Reward That Reverses the World’s Valuation**

The people of verse 20 were not necessarily honored by the world when they made their sacrifices. Emigration can look like loss, and striving can look like waste to people who measure life by property and safety. Verse 21 reverses that judgment. What appeared to be abandonment was movement toward mercy. What appeared to be social defeat was a path to divine pleasure. What appeared to be the loss of home was preparation for a lasting home.

For believers today, the verse gives a way to understand costly obedience without romanticizing pain. Hardship is not automatically proof of righteousness. The action must be connected to belief and Allah's cause. But when a person loses a worldly benefit because he refuses what Allah forbids, the loss is not the final measure. The Lord who sees the sacrifice gives good news that the world could not give: mercy, pleasure, and a bliss that does not expire.
The believer should therefore welcome the transformation of fear into hope. Allah's mercy does not erase the seriousness of the journey; it gives the traveler strength to continue toward Him.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:22

> to stay there for ever and ever. Surely with Allah is a great reward.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Eternity Completes the Promise**

Verse 22 completes the previous promise by adding the quality that earthly reward cannot provide: permanence. The believers will stay in the Gardens for ever and ever. The repeated expression closes the door on the anxiety that follows every worldly pleasure. A person may possess something beautiful and still fear its loss. The Garden promised here is not held on a temporary lease. It is a home whose enjoyment does not move toward an ending.

The Qur'an often pairs Paradise with words of abiding. This repetition is not merely poetic emphasis. It answers a human problem. Created beings are aware that time takes everything from them. Even a long life is short against the horizon of death. The promise of eternal residence changes the meaning of the reward. It is not a large share of a temporary world; it is a different order of existence in which the faithful are no longer threatened by departure.

**The Great Reward Is Kept With Allah**

The final clause says that with Allah is a great reward. The preposition gives the promise a secure location. The reward is not stored in a place where another power can seize it, nor is it dependent on the changing judgment of people. It is with Allah, the One who promises, preserves, and delivers. The believer's confidence rests on the character of the Giver before it rests on the description of the gift.

Greatness here also resists calculation. A person can count wealth, land, or years, but he cannot measure the reward that Allah has prepared. The Qur'an uses the same phrase in other passages, including 4:95 and 48:29, after describing faith and sacrifice. The recurring formula teaches that the reward's scale matches the Lord's generosity, not the narrow scale by which people assess a believer's worldly standing.

**The Promise Is Not a Wage Extracted From God**

The language of reward can mislead if it is treated as though Allah were a debtor forced to pay a servant who has made an equal exchange. The previous verse began with mercy, and mercy remains the ground of the promise. The believer works because Allah commands and hopes because Allah has promised. No creature can place Allah under compulsion. Even the ability to obey is a gift.

This does not make the promise uncertain. Allah's generosity is free, and His promise is true. The believer's humility and confidence therefore belong together. He does not boast that he purchased Paradise by his own strength, but he also does not doubt the word of the One who promised it. The verse's ending joins both ideas: the reward is great, and it is with Allah.

**Forever Changes the Meaning of Loss**

Emigration had involved real losses. Some believers left property, family protection, familiar streets, and a place in the old social order. Striving could involve injury or death. The promise of everlasting residence does not tell the believer that these losses were unreal. It tells him that they are not the end of the account. A person may surrender a house for Allah and receive a home whose security cannot be broken.

That contrast appears throughout the Qur'an. The enjoyment of this life is described as little compared with the Hereafter (9:38), not because created blessings have no value, but because their duration is limited. Verse 22 gives the missing term in the calculation. When the reward is everlasting, a temporary sacrifice can be understood without being trivialized. The believer may grieve what was lost while still knowing that Allah's replacement is not merely equal. It is beyond the reach of loss.

**The Promise Guards Against Empty Heroism**

The context also prevents a false glorification of sacrifice. People can suffer for nationalism, pride, revenge, or the desire to be remembered. Verse 22 does not promise eternal reward to every person who undergoes hardship. It follows the description of those who believed, emigrated, and strove in Allah's cause. The reward is tied to faith and purpose. Pain alone is not a path to Paradise.

This distinction protects the believer from two errors. He does not avoid every cost because comfort is good, and he does not seek pain as proof of spiritual worth. He asks what Allah requires, then accepts the cost attached to obedience. If hardship comes for another reason, he turns it into patience and trust. If an apparent sacrifice is driven by ego, he cannot cover that intention with religious language.

**A Great Reward Produces Steady Work**

The believer who trusts this verse does not become passive while waiting for the Garden. Eternal reward makes present obedience meaningful. Each prayer, act of generosity, truthful word, and difficult restraint is part of a life moving toward Allah's promised home. The reward is not an excuse to neglect the world. It is the reason to use the world without being owned by it.

The verse offers particular strength when results are hidden. A teacher may not see the fruit of years of work, a parent may not see the effect of patient guidance, and a person who refuses corruption may lose status without immediate vindication. Allah does not lose the account. The reward remains with Him. What is unseen by people is not absent from the divine store. Everlasting residence and a great reward turn unnoticed faithfulness into a secure investment. The servant may not see the return today, but Allah's keeping is more reliable than every worldly account. A delayed reward is not a lost reward when the promise rests with the One who never forgets. This certainty steadies people whose obedience seems to produce no immediate advantage. They continue because the ledger is held by Allah, not by public memory, and because the Garden's permanence makes every faithful step meaningful even when the step is hidden from everyone else.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:23

> O believers! Do not take your parents and siblings as trusted allies if they choose disbelief over belief. And whoever of you does so, they are the ˹true˺ wrongdoers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Faith Can Conflict With Family Loyalty**

The verse addresses believers with a command that touches the deepest human bonds: parents and siblings. These are not casual friendships that can be set aside without pain. Family provides identity, affection, shelter, and the sense of belonging that shapes a person's earliest life. The command therefore does not pretend that choosing faith over a hostile family preference is easy. It states that no created relationship may occupy the place of ultimate loyalty when it asks the believer to prefer disbelief over belief.

The forbidden role is expressed through trusted alliance, not through ordinary kindness. The believer is not told to hate a parent or abandon every duty toward a sibling. The issue is who receives the authority of walāyah: who is trusted to direct the believer's deepest commitments and defend a way of life opposed to Allah. Family love remains real, but it cannot become the court that overrules revelation.

**The Qur'an Distinguishes Kindness From Allegiance**

Another passage sets the distinction clearly. If parents strive to make a child associate partners with Allah, the child must not obey them in that matter, yet must keep their company in the world with kindness (31:15). This verse and 31:15 belong together. Disobedience to a parent in a matter of faith does not authorize cruelty, insult, or neglect. The believer refuses the false demand while preserving whatever kindness remains lawful.

This balance is harder than either extreme. Some people use family loyalty to excuse disobedience; others use religious loyalty to excuse harshness. The Qur'an allows neither. Parents retain rights of respect and care, but they do not have the right to command disbelief. Siblings may be loved and helped, but their approval cannot become the believer's inner qiblah. Faith sets a limit on obedience without turning the heart into stone.

**Trusted Alliance Means More Than Affection**

Walāyah includes nearness, protection, and reliance. It can describe the person whose counsel is admitted into the inner circle and whose values shape collective action. The verse does not prohibit a believer from working fairly with non-Muslim relatives or receiving their advice in worldly matters. It prohibits making their rejection of belief the basis of a governing alliance against faith.

That difference helps prevent the verse from being misapplied. A Muslim may have a non-Muslim parent, spouse, colleague, or neighbor. Justice, gratitude, and good treatment remain required. What the believer cannot do is make disbelief the standard by which he chooses truth, or join a campaign that seeks to uproot belief because the campaign is led by relatives. The question is not whether affection exists. The question is whether affection has been promoted into obedience against Allah.

**The Historical Pressure Was Real**

The first believers faced families who could pressure them to stay in Makkah, reject the Messenger, or return to the old religious order. Migration could mean separation from parents and brothers who controlled protection and property. A person who obeyed the family might preserve immediate safety while losing the community of faith. A person who followed Allah might be called disloyal by the people who had taught him loyalty.

The verse speaks into that pressure without denying its emotional weight. It gives the believer a higher reference so that the cost of obedience cannot be decided by the family group alone. This does not turn every disagreement with relatives into a test of faith. The condition is specific: they choose disbelief over belief and the believer takes them as the trusted allies who govern his loyalty. The text addresses a conflict of ultimate commitments, not normal family difference.

**Wrongdoing Is Misplaced Loyalty**

The verse calls those who make the forbidden alliance wrongdoers. Qur'anic wrongdoing often carries the idea of putting something where it does not belong. Family belongs in the sphere of love, care, and lawful respect. Allah belongs in the position of ultimate worship and obedience. When family approval is placed above Allah, the order is reversed. The person may feel loyal, but the loyalty has become unjust because it has displaced the rightful claim.

This helps the believer diagnose subtle compromises. A person may not be asked to deny the shahādah openly. He may simply be expected to hide prayer, accept a forbidden income, approve a false belief, or remain silent when family status depends on injustice. Each case requires wisdom, but the principle remains. The fear of disappointing relatives cannot decide what Allah has made clear.

**Honoring Parents Within the Boundary**

For modern believers, the verse should lead to careful practice rather than dramatic estrangement. Serve aging parents, speak with gentleness, keep family ties, and accept ordinary differences with patience. At the same time, refuse requests that require disbelief, injustice, or abandonment of an established duty. Explain the refusal without contempt. A boundary stated with kindness is closer to the Qur'anic balance than a public performance of hostility.

The believer may also need support from the believing community when family pressure becomes dangerous. Emigration in the broad moral sense can mean moving away from an influence that makes obedience impossible, while still preserving lawful ties. The verse does not promise that every family will approve. It promises that the loss of human approval cannot make a believer truly wronged when he protects the place of faith. The pain of separation is real, but it is not proof that the higher loyalty was mistaken. The believer may grieve the family conflict while refusing to make grief the judge of revelation.
The command protects the community from a loyalty that quietly asks faith to surrender. Believers can remain just to relatives while refusing to let family pressure decide whom they obey.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:24

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “If your parents and children and siblings and spouses and extended family and the wealth you have acquired and the trade you fear will decline and the homes you cherish—˹if all these˺ are more beloved to you than Allah and His Messenger and struggling in His Way, then wait until Allah brings about His Will. Allah does not guide the rebellious people.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Inventories the Heart’s Strongest Loves**

Verse 24 expands the family warning into a complete audit of attachment. It names parents, children, siblings, spouses, extended family, acquired wealth, trade, and beloved homes. The list moves through the whole human world: origin, future, companionship, clan, property, livelihood, and shelter. Nothing important is left outside it. The verse asks whether these goods are more beloved than Allah, His Messenger, and striving in His way.

The question is not whether believers may love families, wealth, commerce, or homes. The Qur'an itself recognizes these as blessings and duties. The issue is ranking. Love becomes rebellious when it is placed above Allah and then used to justify disobedience. A believer can love a parent deeply and still refuse the parent's command to abandon faith. He can cherish a house and still leave it when remaining would require betrayal. The verse measures not the presence of affection but the order of affection.

**A Complete Biography in One List**

The sequence has a psychological force. Parents represent the past from which a person came. Children represent the future he hopes to secure. Siblings and spouses represent intimate companionship. Extended family represents collective identity. Wealth records past labor. Trade represents future income. Homes embody safety, memory, and taste. The list therefore reaches every place where a person can feel anchored.

The test is most visible when two goods do not actually conflict. Anyone can claim that Allah is more beloved while family and faith are comfortable together. The ranking appears when obedience costs approval, money, a contract, an inheritance, or a familiar home. The verse does not ask the believer to manufacture such conflicts. It tells him what the answer must be when life brings them. The heart must be arranged before the crisis arrives, because a sudden choice reveals an order that has been forming for years.

**Allah and the Messenger Are the Fixed Center**

The counterweight to the eight loves is Allah, His Messenger, and striving in His way. Allah is the source and owner of every good listed. The Messenger teaches how to love Allah in concrete life. Striving proves that the love has become action rather than sentiment. The three belong together. Love of Allah without following the Messenger becomes a private claim with no revealed shape; following without sacrifice becomes a theory that has never been tested.

The Prophet taught that faith is not complete until he is more beloved than one's father, child, and all people (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 15; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 44). That teaching does not compete with filial kindness. It sets the Messenger's guidance above every human preference. The believer loves family more truly when he loves them within the truth, not when he helps them destroy their souls in order to preserve peace.

**“Wait” Is a Warning, Not a Timetable**

The command to wait until Allah brings His Will is deliberately open. It does not name one punishment or one historical event. The people whose ranking is wrong are told that divine action will expose the result of their choice. In the immediate setting, the command may refer to the coming victory and the events that would separate sincere believers from those held back by attachment. Its open wording also keeps the warning alive for later generations.

Waiting here is not peaceful patience. It is the pause of someone who has been warned and refuses to change. The verse gives no comfort to the person who says that family, business, or property will eventually decide the matter for him. Allah's command will arrive through revelation, circumstance, death, or judgment. The question is whether the believer reorders his loves before the command forces the issue.

**The Verse Does Not Condemn Created Love**

A careless reading could make religion sound hostile to family and home. The Qur'an does not teach that. It commands kindness to parents, protects marriage, praises charity toward relatives, and recognizes wealth as a trust. The danger is not love itself but love detached from the Giver. When a person treats a created good as an absolute, he demands from it the security only Allah can provide.

The cure is not emotional emptiness. It is proper order. Love the parent as a creature entrusted to you by Allah. Love the spouse as a companion in obedience. Use wealth as a means of service. Keep a home as a place from which worship grows. If one of these goods asks to become a rival to Allah, the believer must refuse the rivalry. That refusal may hurt, but it preserves the good from becoming an idol.

**A Practical Audit of Preference**

The verse can be used as a daily examination. Which relationship would I protect by concealing a truth? Which payment would make me accept an unlawful condition? Which career or house would I refuse to leave even if staying meant abandoning a clear duty? The answers do not automatically settle every legal detail, but they expose where the heart expects salvation from something other than Allah.

The final warning, Allah does not guide the rebellious people, identifies the danger as persistent refusal, not a moment of fear. A believer may hesitate, grieve, and need counsel. Rebellion is the settled decision to let created loves outrank the Creator. Verse 24 is severe because it is merciful: it names the objects that most often capture the heart before they become masters. The person who reorders them under Allah's guidance does not lose love. He learns where love belongs.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:25

> Indeed Allah has given you ˹believers˺ victory on many battlefields, even at the Battle of Ḥunain when you took pride in your great numbers, but they proved of no advantage to you. The earth, despite its vastness, seemed to close in on you, then you turned back in retreat.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Victory Can Become a Test of the Victor**

Verse 25 recalls Ḥunain not simply as a battle report but as a correction to the believers' confidence. Allah had already granted victory on many battlefields. The word many establishes a history of help. Then the verse singles out Ḥunain, where the believers were impressed by their great number. The problem was not having a large army. The problem was allowing the number to become the source of confidence.

The Muslim force after the conquest of Makkah was larger than any force the community had previously fielded. New Muslims from Makkah joined the veterans of Madinah, and the size of the army created a feeling that defeat was no longer possible. The valley of Ḥunain exposed that assumption quickly. A numerical advantage can be useful, but it cannot replace dependence on Allah. When a gift becomes an object of trust, the gift itself becomes a trial.

**The Valley Made Inner Overconfidence Visible**

The verse says the numbers did not avail the believers. This does not mean numbers have no military value. It means that material means have no independent power. The army still needed formation, courage, knowledge of terrain, and obedience. Yet none of these could guarantee victory apart from Allah. The opening of the battle showed what happens when a force relies on size without sufficient readiness and composure.

The earth then seemed to close in despite its vastness. The physical valley had not literally become smaller, but retreating soldiers experienced a loss of room. Narrow ground, surprise, noise, and confusion turned breadth into constriction. The image also describes panic. Fear can make a person feel trapped inside a world that has not changed. Qur'an 8:45 links steadfastness and remembrance with strength in battle; Ḥunain showed the opposite when confidence broke.

**The Qur’an Preserves the Community’s Failure**

The verse speaks to the believers in the second person and does not hide their retreat. A weaker religious history might record only the eventual victory, protecting the community's honor by removing its moment of collapse. Revelation does the opposite. It preserves the failure because a community that remembers only success will mistake success for entitlement.

This honesty is a form of mercy. The believers can study what happened without turning the defeat into despair. The retreat did not prove that Allah had abandoned them forever. It proved that their reliance needed correction. The Qur'an's willingness to name the community's error also gives later believers permission to examine their own failures truthfully. A mistake admitted before Allah can become instruction. A mistake concealed to preserve reputation becomes a repeated foundation.

**Ḥunain Followed a Major Success**

The timing intensifies the lesson. The conquest of Makkah had appeared to confirm the community's strength and the success of its mission. That success was real, but it created a new danger: people might confuse the victory Allah granted with an automatic guarantee attached to their numbers. Ḥunain came soon afterward and showed that the community could be shaken even after a dramatic triumph.

Success can make a person less careful than hardship does. In hardship, dependence is obvious. In abundance, the means look self-sufficient. The verse places the many earlier victories beside Ḥunain so that the believers can identify the pattern. Allah had been the giver in both conditions. The difference was the community's awareness. The event corrected an attitude before the next great demands of the chapter, including the difficult march toward Tabūk.

**The Meaning of “You Turned Back”**

The final clause is brief: then you turned your backs in retreat. It does not soften the image with excuses. Some believers lost formation, fled from the first attack, or were swept along by the panic of the larger mass. The Prophet remained present, and the later verse will describe the restoration of calm, but verse 25 is concerned with the moment when the army's confidence failed.

This separation of verses matters. A later recovery does not erase an earlier mistake, and an earlier mistake does not cancel the possibility of recovery. The believer needs both truths. Denial prevents repentance; despair prevents return. Verse 25 supplies the first truth so that verse 26 can supply the second. Honest memory is the bridge between them.

**The Modern Number We Trust**

The battle's spiritual application reaches beyond armies. A business may trust its capital, a charity its membership, a leader his audience, or a person his education and connections. None of these is wrong to possess. The danger begins when the number becomes the explanation for success and the reason to stop seeking Allah's help. The community can be large and still be fragile if its people assume that size makes error impossible.

The remedy is not to reject planning or strength. It is to place means in their proper position. Prepare well, count resources accurately, and remember who can make them useful or useless. Ḥunain teaches the believer to say after success, Allah granted this, rather than, We were too large to fail. The first sentence produces gratitude and vigilance. The second creates the conditions for a fall. Gratitude keeps a victory in the hands of its Giver, where it can remain a blessing rather than becoming evidence for the ego. Remembering weakness after success is a protection against repeating the valley's lesson. The army must carry the memory of its dependence into its next success. Otherwise gratitude fades, and the same gift that once helped the believers begins to persuade them that they no longer need help.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:26

> Then Allah sent down His reassurance upon His Messenger and the believers, and sent down forces you could not see, and punished those who disbelieved. Such was the reward of the disbelievers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Reassurance Restored the Scattered Army**

Verse 26 describes the reversal of Ḥunain by beginning with reassurance from Allah. The Arabic term sakīnah is related to stillness and settling. It names a calm that descends into a heart when fear has made it unstable. The verse places this reassurance upon the Messenger and the believers. The Prophet was not excluded from the need for divine support, and the ordinary believers were not left outside the gift.

The sequence matters. The army's first need was not a new number but recovered composure. Panic had made a large force ineffective. Reassurance restored the ability to hear, gather, and act. Reports about the battle describe the Prophet calling the believers back while those around him helped spread the call, but the verse gives the deepest explanation: Allah settled His Messenger and the believers. Human effort became useful again because the heart was no longer ruled by confusion.

**The Unseen Forces Belong to Allah**

The verse also speaks of forces the believers could not see. Classical exegetes commonly understood this as angels or other unseen forms of divine support, while discussing how such support operated. Some emphasized assistance in combat; others stressed the strengthening of courage and the casting of firmness into hearts. The text does not require the reader to reduce the unseen to one mechanical picture. It insists that visible causes do not exhaust Allah's means.

The believers still had to regroup and fight. Unseen support did not turn them into spectators. That balance corrects two errors. One error says that because Allah helps, human action is unnecessary. The other says that only visible strength counts and that the unseen is irrelevant. The Qur'anic account holds both together. The army acts with its bodies, while Allah controls the wider field of causes, confidence, fear, and result.

**The Punishment Was a Consequence, Not a Trophy**

The verse says Allah punished those who disbelieved and calls this the reward of the disbelievers. The word reward is used ironically for a result earned by persistent opposition. The enemy's defeat is not presented as an occasion for the believers to become cruel. It is a consequence within a moral order. Their aggression met resistance, and the false confidence that had driven them was broken.

The phrase also keeps the event under Allah's judgment rather than tribal revenge. A believer may rejoice that a danger has ended, but he must not imagine that every defeated person is personally beyond mercy. The next verse immediately speaks of Allah's turning in grace to whoever He wills. The account of punishment is therefore held beside the possibility of pardon. This is one of the chapter's recurring disciplines: justice and mercy are not allowed to erase each other.

**The Messenger’s Steadfastness Is Part of the Sign**

The verse gives reassurance first to the Messenger. His presence at the point of retreat became a visible center around which the scattered units could gather. This historical detail, preserved in reports about Ḥunain, should not be turned into a heroic substitute for the verse's theology. The Prophet's courage mattered, but his courage itself was supported by Allah's sakīnah.

That relationship is important for leaders. A leader may need to remain steady when a group panics, call people back, and absorb the first shock without spreading fear. Yet he should not imagine that his personal strength is enough. The leader also needs to receive calm from Allah and to direct the group back toward obedience rather than toward admiration of himself. Reassurance is a divine gift that may travel through a human voice without belonging to that voice.

**Visible Defeat Does Not End the Story**

Verse 25 ended with retreat. Verse 26 shows that retreat was not the final state. The believers could return to the field after recognizing their dependence. This pattern is valuable for anyone who has failed under pressure. A collapse can reveal pride, but it need not define the rest of the life. Repentance, remembrance, and renewed action can restore what panic interrupted.

The verse does not erase the consequences of the initial retreat. It places recovery after correction. The believers are not rescued so that they can keep their old confidence. They are reassured so that they can fight without worshipping their numbers. Allah's help is not an endorsement of the attitude that caused the crisis. It is a mercy that gives the community another chance to act rightly.

**Trust the Source of Calm**

Modern believers face their own moments when the world appears to narrow. The remedy is not denial of the danger. It is to seek the sakīnah that allows clear action within the danger. Prayer, remembrance, truthful counsel, and disciplined cooperation can become means through which Allah settles a frightened community. None of these guarantees an outcome chosen by the ego. They restore the ability to obey.

The unseen forces remain Allah's alone. That means the believer can use visible means without believing that visibility is the measure of power. A small group may endure, a large group may fail, and a frightened heart may become firm. The verse's final lesson is simple: when the community returns to Allah, the means can be changed from instruments of pride into instruments of service. Calm does not remove responsibility; it makes responsible action possible again. The believer receives reassurance in order to return to duty, not to remain still admiring the miracle. Recovery is complete only when calm becomes obedience, courage becomes service, and the people who were scattered can again accept discipline without imagining that their own strength saved them.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:27

> Then afterwards Allah will turn in grace to whoever He wills. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.
