The verse finally teaches that faith and truthfulness cannot be separated. The hypocrites' excuse fails because it denies what they knowingly did. The believer may be corrected, but he does not call black white to preserve his standing. He accepts the cost of truth because Allah's judgment is more important than the audience's relief.

A community should reward this honesty. It should allow a person who repents to return, while still protecting people from repeated harm. Pardon is not the same as pretending that no wrong occurred. Justice records the wrong, repentance changes the person, and Allah decides the final account with perfect knowledge.

This is why the command against excuses is followed by the possibility of pardon. Allah does not ask the servant to produce a perfect past before returning. He asks him to stop arguing with the truth. The person who repents may need to accept that some listeners will remember the offense, but he does not use their memory as a reason to remain false. He repairs what he can, learns reverence, and lets consistent conduct speak. The community protects the faith by refusing mockery and protects repentance by allowing the sincere return. Both firmness and mercy are required because the same act can be treated as a joke by the proud and as a wound by the one who reveres Allah.

The verse also warns leaders not to confuse punishment with repair. A person who has crossed a serious boundary may need limits, public clarification, or protection of the community. Those measures should serve justice, not the leader's anger. The possibility that one group is pardoned means that the door of sincere return must remain visible. The possibility that another is punished means that pardon cannot be granted merely because an apology is convenient. Allah knows the difference between a changed heart and a new performance. Human beings look for evidence of change while leaving the final judgment to Him.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:67

> The hypocrites, both men and women, are all alike: they encourage what is evil, forbid what is good, and withhold ˹what is in˺ their hands. They neglected Allah, so He neglected them. Surely the hypocrites are the rebellious.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Men and Women Share the Same Moral Pattern**

The verse places hypocritical men and women together. Their gender differs, but their moral pattern is the same: they encourage evil, resist good, and close their hands. This is a statement of equal accountability. Neither men nor women are given a special exemption from the duties of faith or the consequences of betrayal.

Equality here is not sameness of every social role. It is equality before moral judgment. A man cannot blame women for corruption while practicing it himself, and a woman cannot treat hypocrisy as a defect that belongs only to male leaders. Each person is responsible for the influence he or she exercises. The verse gives both a warning and a dignity: both can be agents of harm, and both are called to return to Allah.

**Hypocrisy Reverses the Moral Order**

The hypocrites encourage what is evil and forbid what is good. The reversal is deliberate. They do not merely fail to promote righteousness; they use their influence to make wrongdoing attractive and obedience embarrassing. A community becomes unsafe when its informal leaders praise corruption while mocking restraint.

This inversion often begins with language. Evil is renamed freedom, greed is called ambition, and sincere advice is called weakness. Good is made to look backward, severe, or impractical. The believer must therefore examine the words used to describe conduct, not only the conduct itself. A polished label cannot change the moral reality of an act.

**Closed Hands Reveal Closed Allegiance**

Withholding what is in their hands includes reluctance to spend in Allah's cause and refusal to offer support when support is needed. Their hands are closed because their hearts have already decided that resources belong only to the self. They may speak about community and loyalty, but their possessions reveal what they will actually protect.

Giving does not require waste or neglect of dependents. A believer keeps lawful responsibilities and still leaves a path for generosity. The hypocrite's problem is not careful budgeting; it is a settled refusal to recognize any claim beyond personal security. His hand opens for appetite and closes for duty. That pattern turns wealth into a wall between him and the people Allah placed around him.

**Forgetting Allah Produces Divine Neglect**

“They neglected Allah, so He neglected them” (9:67) is not a claim that Allah forgets in the human sense. It describes a reciprocal judgment: they abandoned remembrance, guidance, and obedience, so Allah left them without the special care and direction they had rejected. The language makes the moral consequence vivid.

A person can forget Allah while still speaking religious words. Forgetfulness appears when prayer is postponed without concern, commands are treated as obstacles, and gratitude disappears from the use of blessings. Divine neglect is therefore not arbitrary abandonment. It is the painful result of choosing to live as though Allah's guidance were irrelevant. The servant should seek refuge from this state by remembering Allah before the heart becomes comfortable without Him.

**The Community Needs Active Goodness**

The verse implies that neutrality is not always neutral. When evil is promoted and good is silenced, merely avoiding the worst act may leave the social order in the hands of those who promote it. Believers are called to encourage good and resist wrong with knowledge, wisdom, and attention to ability.

This duty must not become a license for shouting at everyone. Good counsel considers timing, evidence, and likely benefit. It protects the vulnerable and avoids creating a greater harm. The hypocrites' inversion teaches the opposite: their speech serves what corrupts. The believer's speech should make truth easier to practice and sin harder to celebrate.

**Rebellion Is a Habit of Refusing Guidance**

The verse ends by calling the hypocrites rebellious. Rebellion is not only a dramatic revolt. It can be the repeated refusal to let Allah's guidance correct one's preferences. A person may rebel through influence, silence, hoarding, or the constant creation of excuses.

The cure is active remembrance. Open the hand, support good, accept correction, and restore prayer. A person cannot repair hypocrisy by changing one public phrase while keeping the same moral pattern. He must let Allah reorder his influence and resources. The community then becomes a place where men and women protect one another from evil, make goodness honorable, and keep their allegiance visible through conduct.

A person who wants to know where he stands can examine three things: what he praises, what he opposes, and what he is willing to release. These reveal allegiance more reliably than a public label. Does he make righteousness welcome, or does he make it awkward? Does he resist harm when resistance costs a relationship? Does he treat his resources as trusts or as walls? The answers do not replace repentance, but they show where repentance must begin. Allah's neglect is not an excuse for despair. The servant can return to remembrance, reopen the hand, and support good until the moral order that hypocrisy reversed is restored in his own conduct.

The verse also guards against a false neutrality that treats good and evil as equal options. Believers are not called to dominate every conversation, but they are called to make moral truth visible. They teach children what justice means, support people who resist exploitation, and refuse to fund the practices they condemn. Their hands open where help is due, and their speech directs attention toward Allah rather than toward themselves. In this way remembrance becomes social as well as private. The heart that remembers Allah will eventually show that remembrance through what it supports, interrupts, and protects.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:68

> Allah has promised the hypocrites, both men and women, and the disbelievers an everlasting stay in the Fire of Hell—it is sufficient for them. Allah has condemned them, and they will suffer a never-ending punishment.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Warning Includes Men and Women Together**

The verse repeats the pairing of hypocritical men and women before naming the punishment. The repetition makes accountability unmistakably universal. Hypocrisy is not a defect tied to one sex, class, or public position. Anyone who hides rejection behind belonging can fall under the warning, and anyone who returns sincerely can seek Allah's mercy before the condition becomes fixed.

The verse also places hypocrites beside disbelievers because hypocrisy is not a harmless middle position. The hypocrite wants the benefits of faith while inwardly resisting its truth. His double life damages the community and protects the disbelief from correction. The external label cannot rescue the heart from the meaning of its choices.

**Hell Is Described as Sufficient**

The statement that Hell is sufficient for them means that it contains the full consequence of their rebellion. No further punishment is needed to make the judgment complete. The phrase also exposes the poverty of what they sought in the world. They wanted safety, status, and protection through deception, yet the shelter they built cannot withstand the final account.

A believer should not speak of Hell with casual excitement. The description is meant to awaken fear and prevent the path, not to make people enjoy the thought of another person's suffering. Serious warning and compassion can coexist. The servant fears the punishment for himself, hopes for repentance in others, and refuses to soften Allah's clear threat into harmless symbolism.

**Condemnation Means Distance From Mercy**

Allah's condemnation is often explained as being distanced from Him. This gives the punishment a spiritual depth. The hypocrites spent life distancing themselves from guidance while wishing to remain close to its social benefits. The final judgment makes their chosen distance complete.

The heart can taste a smaller form of this distance in the world. Prayer becomes empty, repentance feels impossible, and good advice produces irritation rather than hope. These signs should be treated as warnings, not as proof that Allah has closed every door. The door of repentance remains open while life remains, but the servant must stop defending the distance and turn back toward Allah's mercy.

**Everlasting Punishment Follows a Settled End**

The never-ending punishment must be understood with the distinction between settled disbelief and a sinful believer who repents or dies with faith. Allah does not confuse a passing weakness with a final rejection. The verse addresses those whose disbelief and hypocrisy have become their enduring state.

This distinction protects justice and encourages repentance. A sinner must not say, “My weakness is harmless because I still claim faith.” Nor should he despair as though one failure has made return impossible. The right response to the warning is immediate reform. Delay is dangerous because habits can become identities and identities can become the condition in which a person meets Allah.

**The Promise Is a Counterpoint to False Promises**

The hypocrites made promises to people and broke them. This verse gives a different kind of promise: Allah's promise is certain, not a performance shaped by audience pressure. The promised punishment is as reliable as the promises of mercy and reward made to sincere believers.

This certainty teaches the believer to distrust temporary security purchased through deception. A person may escape a complaint, preserve a position, or silence a critic, but none of those victories changes the divine account. Allah's promise makes hidden choices weighty. The servant should therefore prefer a truthful loss now to a false success that carries him toward lasting punishment.

**Fear Should Produce Protection, Not Cruelty**

A community that takes this verse seriously will protect itself from hypocrisy through truth, fair accountability, and opportunities for repentance. It will not treat every mistake as proof of hidden disbelief. It will not allow repeated harm to continue merely because naming it feels harsh.

The believer keeps both sides of the warning. He is firm about the consequence of settled rebellion and gentle toward the person who is genuinely returning. Allah's condemnation is not an excuse for human arrogance. It is a reason to abandon the traits that lead to it: deception, contempt, closed hands, and the neglect of Allah. The safest response is to seek mercy before the heart becomes comfortable without it.

The promise of punishment also gives weight to the community's work of warning. A believer should not turn the threat into a slogan used against every opponent, because careless declarations imitate the arrogance of the hypocrites. He should explain the path plainly and invite the person away from it. If rejection continues, he leaves the final judgment to Allah while protecting people from harm. The possibility of repentance remains a reason for patience before death, and the certainty of judgment remains a reason not to normalize rebellion. The servant asks Allah for mercy because he knows that no one is safe through his own image or ancestry.

The severity of the promise should produce urgency in teaching and tenderness in repentance. A believer warns a person away from the path without claiming that his own safety is guaranteed by a label. He asks Allah to protect him from the same disease and gives people a way to return while they are alive. If the person refuses and continues to harm, boundaries become necessary. Firmness then serves mercy by preventing one rebellious voice from drawing others into the same distance from Allah. The punishment is everlasting, but the warning arrives while change is still possible.

The warning is therefore a call to return before the distance becomes final.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:69

> ˹You hypocrites are˺ like those ˹disbelievers˺ before you. They were far superior to you in might and more abundant in wealth and children. They enjoyed their share in this life. You have enjoyed your share, just as they did. And you have engaged in idle talk, just as they did. Their deeds have become void in this world and the Hereafter. And it is they who are the ˹true˺ losers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Power Does Not Change the Moral Pattern**

The hypocrites are compared with earlier peoples who possessed greater might, wealth, and children. The comparison removes their excuse that their present behavior is justified by circumstance. The earlier people had more visible strength, yet strength did not save them from the consequences of rejecting guidance. The hypocrites have less power and still repeat the same moral pattern.

This is a warning against measuring truth by the scale of worldly success. A large army, prosperous family, or influential network can make a community feel secure. None of these proves that Allah approves its direction. The question is what power serves. If strength protects justice and gratitude, it can be a blessing. If it protects arrogance and idle talk, it becomes evidence against its owner.

**Enjoyment Is Not the Same as Gratitude**

The earlier people enjoyed their share, and the hypocrites enjoyed theirs. The verse does not condemn every lawful pleasure. It condemns enjoyment detached from remembrance, responsibility, and accountability. A person may receive food, family, comfort, and status, then act as though receiving them has released him from the duty to thank the Giver.

Enjoyment becomes dangerous when it teaches the heart that the world is all there is. The person begins to defend the lifestyle that produced the forgetfulness. He does not ask whether the wealth is lawful, whether the family is being guided, or whether the comfort is making him generous. He asks only how to keep the share growing. Gratitude measures the effect of a blessing; appetite measures only its size.

**Idle Talk Connects the Generations**

The verse repeats that the hypocrites engaged in idle talk as earlier peoples did. Speech is part of historical continuity. Communities inherit not only buildings and wealth but habits of mockery, gossip, distraction, and the use of public language to avoid truth. A generation can imitate a previous generation's errors while imagining that its technology makes it wiser.

Idle talk is not every light conversation. It is speech that consumes attention without serving truth, mercy, knowledge, or wholesome rest. It can be loud or polished, traditional or modern. The believer asks what his words produce. If they make evil attractive, truth embarrassing, and time meaningless, their entertainment is carrying a serious moral cost.

**Imitation Can Be a Hidden Form of Defeat**

Earlier commentators connected this verse to the danger of following previous communities step by step. Imitation is not wrong when it concerns useful knowledge or sound practice. The danger is copying a community's moral failures because those failures appear successful. People see wealth and imitate the habits that accompanied it, forgetting that the visible result may conceal a judgment.

The believer studies history to gain discernment, not to collect costumes. He asks why a people rose, what corrupted them, and which choices destroyed their trust. The same error can wear a new uniform. A community that laughs at the failures of the past while repeating their pride has learned only the names, not the lesson.

**Deeds Can Become Void Through Misplaced Purpose**

The verse says their deeds became void in this world and the Hereafter. This does not mean that every worldly benefit vanished immediately. Their roads, wealth, and achievements may remain for a time. It means that the deeds lost their moral value because they were not directed toward Allah and did not protect the people from the consequences of disbelief.

A deed can also be emptied by hypocrisy when its outward form is preserved but its purpose is rejected. The believer therefore asks what his work is building in the soul. Public success cannot replace sincerity, and social benefit cannot be used to declare rebellion righteous. Allah alone gives lasting worth to an action.

**The Real Loss Is a False Calculation**

The final judgment calls them the true losers. They may have thought the believers were losing by sacrificing comfort, while they themselves were winning through possession and influence. The verse reverses the calculation. Loss is not measured by how much a person spent in the world; it is measured by what he carried into the final account.

This perspective does not make worldly planning pointless. It tells the servant what planning is for. Build, earn, raise children, and enjoy lawful provision, but do not trade faith for the means. The best share is the one that leads to gratitude. The worst share is the one that makes a person repeat an old error while calling the repetition progress.

A society can repeat an old error while believing itself original. It may mock earlier peoples for worshipping power while worshipping influence, or condemn their vain speech while filling its own hours with distraction. The names change, but the moral structure remains. A believer studies prosperity with caution, asks what it is doing to worship, and refuses to copy the habits of a successful corrupt culture. He can learn useful skills from any people without inheriting their contempt for guidance. The final loss belongs to the person who has spent the temporary share so completely that nothing remains for the meeting with Allah.

The wise person therefore receives the pleasures of this life as a loan and uses them as preparation for the next. He does not reject family, wealth, strength, or recreation, but he asks what each one is making him. If comfort makes him generous and prayerful, he thanks Allah. If it makes him contemptuous of the needy or careless with time, he treats the comfort as a warning. The earlier nations are not mentioned to entertain the listener; they are mirrors. The mirror is useful only when a person is willing to see his own face in the pattern and change before the ending arrives.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:70

> Have they not received the stories of those ˹destroyed˺ before them: the people of Noah, ’Âd, and Thamûd, the people of Abraham, the residents of Midian, and the overturned cities ˹of Lot˺? Their messengers came to them with clear proofs. Allah would have never wronged them, but it was they who wronged themselves.

**Expanded Commentary**

**History Is a Moral Witness**

The verse asks whether the hypocrites have received the accounts of earlier peoples. These stories are not offered as decoration or as a list of ancient names. They are evidence that communities can possess strength, culture, and time yet still destroy themselves by refusing clear guidance. History becomes a witness against the claim that consequences are too distant to matter.

The named peoples include Noah's community, ʿĀd, Thamūd, Abraham's people, Midian, and the overturned cities commonly associated with Lot. The range of stories prevents the listener from reducing judgment to one kind of sin or one region. Different societies received messengers, faced different tests, and shared the choice to reject clear proof. Their variety makes the warning harder to dismiss as someone else's unique problem.

**Clear Proof Makes Responsibility Concrete**

The messengers brought clear proofs. The communities were not destroyed because Allah wanted to trap them in ignorance. They received signs, instruction, and a chance to respond. A clear proof does not force every person to believe, but it removes the excuse that no guidance was available.

This principle applies to the believer's life. He should seek knowledge and ask for evidence, but when the truth becomes clear he cannot remain permanently neutral. Understanding creates responsibility. The Qur'an also teaches that Allah does not burden a soul beyond its capacity, so clarity must be considered alongside ability and circumstance. The verse condemns people who had the means to recognize and obey, then chose injustice instead.

**The Overturned Cities Carry a Double Image**

The expression “overturned cities” points to a destruction that changed the order of a place. Some interpreters connect it specifically with the cities of Lot; others see the phrase as broader. Both readings preserve the central lesson: a society can be turned from apparent stability to ruin when its moral corruption is defended as normal.

The image also warns against trusting permanence. A city appears solid because houses, roads, and institutions remain in place. Yet social order depends on choices that can be reversed. When injustice becomes a custom and warnings are mocked, the outside may still look strong while the inside is collapsing. The believer asks not only whether a society functions, but whether it functions justly.

**Allah Does Not Wrong the Wrongdoer**

The closing principle is explicit: Allah did not wrong them; they wronged themselves. This rejects the complaint that punishment is arbitrary. Allah sent messengers, presented proof, and judged according to what people chose. Their destruction was not an injustice inflicted on innocent communities.

The statement also describes sin as self-harm. A person may imagine that disobedience injures only another person or violates only an external rule. In reality, the wrong reshapes his own heart, relationships, and future. Allah's command protects the servant, so rejecting it damages the one who rejects it even when he thinks he is gaining freedom.

**Receiving Stories Is Not the Same as Learning**

The hypocrites may have heard these stories many times, yet the question asks whether the accounts have truly reached them. Information reaches the ears without reaching the conscience. A person can recite the names of destroyed peoples and still imitate their pride, mockery, and refusal of guidance.

Learning occurs when history changes a choice. The believer asks which habit in the story resembles his own, which warning he has been postponing, and what repair must begin now. He does not use the destruction of earlier people to feel superior. He uses it to fear becoming another example for those who come after him.

**Mercy and Warning Stand Together**

The messengers came with proof before judgment. This sequence shows mercy. Allah gave the people a chance to recognize truth, and the warning gave them a chance to change. Mercy does not mean withholding every consequence forever. It means that the consequence is preceded by guidance and that the door of repentance remains open before the final end.

A believer should carry this balance into teaching and reform. He explains clearly, warns honestly, and leaves room for return. He does not assume that a person's past makes change impossible. He also does not call endless delay mercy when delay is only allowing harm to spread. The stories become guidance when they produce humility, justice, and a prompt return to Allah.

The list of peoples also teaches that no community is protected by ancestry. Being connected to a prophet, a land, or a respected history does not replace present obedience. Each generation receives the record and must answer for its own response. The stories are not tools for boasting that one's group is better than the destroyed. They are warnings that clear proof can be resisted by people who still possess wealth, intelligence, and social confidence. The believer receives the account with humility: Allah's justice is perfect, the evidence is real, and the choice to continue in wrong belongs to the person who makes it.

A clear proof also creates a duty to teach with clarity. The believer should not hide behind complex language when the moral issue is plain, and he should not turn a complex issue into a false certainty merely to win an argument. The messengers brought proof suited to their people, and the people were still responsible for how they received it. Modern readers receive the accounts through scripture and reflection; they should allow those accounts to challenge inherited pride. Allah's justice means that no person is judged by another person's response. Each soul receives warning, ability, and the chance to return.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:71

> The believers, both men and women, are guardians of one another. They encourage good and forbid evil, establish prayer and pay alms-tax, and obey Allah and His Messenger. It is they who will be shown Allah’s mercy. Surely Allah is Almighty, All-Wise.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Believers Protect One Another**

The verse describes believing men and women as guardians, allies, and responsible friends to one another. Faith is not presented as a private arrangement between an individual and Allah. It creates bonds of care, counsel, protection, and shared duty. A believer watches over another person's dignity, safety, and faith without claiming ownership of the other person.

The pairing of men and women is important. Both belong to the moral community, both carry obligations, and both can help preserve what is good. Guardianship does not mean that every person has the same role in every setting. It means that no sex is excluded from spiritual agency. A woman can advise, protect, teach, and resist wrong; a man can do the same. The community's health depends on the virtue of both.

**Goodness Needs Encouragement**

Believers encourage good rather than waiting for goodness to appear without support. Encouragement includes teaching, making lawful obedience easier, praising honest effort, and arranging institutions that help people keep their duties. A person may know what is right and still need companionship, resources, or a clear example before he can act on it.

This duty should not become a search for control. Encouraging good is different from forcing everyone into one personality or treating a personal preference as divine law. The believer explains what Allah has made clear and leaves room for lawful differences. He uses wisdom because the goal is obedience, not the satisfaction of correcting people.

**Wrong Must Be Resisted With Wisdom**

Forbidding evil requires knowledge of what is wrong, the ability to speak, and attention to the likely result. A person who condemns an act without understanding it may create confusion. A person who confronts a wrong in a way that causes greater harm may have served anger rather than reform.

The duty still requires courage. Silence can become cooperation when evil is praised and the vulnerable have no protection. The believer chooses a form suited to capacity: counsel privately, report abuse through a safe process, withdraw support from corruption, or speak publicly when public harm demands it. Guardianship means refusing to abandon people to those who exploit them.

**Prayer and Alms Give the Bond a Structure**

The verse joins social responsibility to prayer and alms-tax. The community cannot be sustained by activism without worship, and worship cannot be defended while the needy are ignored. Prayer turns the believers toward Allah repeatedly. Alms-tax turns their resources toward lawful social care. The two practices prevent guardianship from becoming either empty idealism or mere administration.

Establishing prayer means more than performing isolated movements. It protects the times, conditions, and meaning of the prayer so that remembrance shapes conduct. Paying alms-tax means more than occasional generosity. It accepts an obligation in wealth and recognizes that the poor have a claim. A believer who guards both inner worship and outward justice becomes a more reliable guardian of others.

**Obedience Joins the Whole Program**

The final practical quality is obedience to Allah and His Messenger. Without this center, people may encourage what they personally call good and forbid what threatens their power. Obedience supplies the measure by which communal authority is judged. Leaders are not allowed to redefine good as whatever preserves their position, and followers are not allowed to call rebellion virtue merely because it feels independent.

The Messenger's guidance also keeps social concern connected to mercy. Correction must serve truth, and truth must be carried with justice. A community that quotes duty while practicing humiliation has lost the balance of the verse. Obedience gives its guardianship a purpose beyond faction and reputation.

**Mercy Is Promised to Those Who Practice Care**

Allah's mercy is promised to the people who join mutual guardianship, moral action, prayer, alms, and obedience. Mercy is not earned as a payment that humans can demand. It is a promised grace reached through the path Allah has described. The believer acts because Allah commands, then hopes because Allah is merciful.

The closing names Allah as Almighty and Wise. His power makes mercy effective, and His wisdom makes its distribution just. Believers can therefore work without imagining that their care alone saves the community. They protect one another, but Allah is the true Protector. Their task is to become instruments of His mercy through knowledge, courage, worship, and fair treatment.

Mutual guardianship also means that believers do not leave moral work to famous people. A small act of protection, a private correction, or a fair refusal to repeat gossip can preserve a community. The duty is shared according to knowledge and ability. Someone who lacks authority may still withhold support from wrong and direct a vulnerable person to help. Someone with authority bears a greater burden to create safe ways for truth to be spoken. In every case, prayer keeps action from becoming self-righteousness, and obedience keeps compassion from becoming mere sentiment. Allah's mercy reaches through the people who make goodness possible for one another.

The promise of mercy also gives moral work its right spirit. Believers do not correct others because they enjoy superiority. They correct because they want good to prevail and harm to stop, while knowing that Allah can correct them through the person they advise. Men and women should cooperate without competing over who owns religious authority. Knowledge creates responsibility, and authority creates accountability. Prayer keeps the reformer dependent on Allah; alms-tax keeps him attentive to material need; obedience keeps him within the limits of the message. Mutual guardianship becomes mercy in practice when it is carried with humility.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:72

> Allah has promised the believers, both men and women, Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there forever, and splendid homes in the Gardens of Eternity, and—above all— the pleasure of Allah. That is ˹truly˺ the ultimate triumph.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Promise Includes Men and Women Equally**

The promise is addressed to believing men and women together. The reward is not divided by a lesser spiritual status assigned to one sex. Both receive Allah's promise of the Garden because both were called to faith, obedience, sacrifice, and guardianship. This is the other side of the moral equality stated in the preceding verse: shared responsibility is joined to shared hope.

The inclusion also corrects a narrow view of religious reward. A person's final worth is not measured by social visibility, wealth, or the authority attached to a public role. Allah sees faith and action in every servant. The woman whose obedience is hidden and the man whose service is unseen are both included in the promise when they believe and act sincerely. The Garden is not a prize reserved for public fame.

**The Gardens Answer Human Longing Without Reducing It**

The imagery of gardens with flowing rivers speaks to life, beauty, refreshment, and permanence. Water that flows beneath the gardens suggests abundance without the fear that the source will dry up. The homes are splendid because the reward includes belonging, safety, and a dwelling worthy of rest. These descriptions address the body and the imagination in language people can understand.

The promise should not be reduced to material appetite. The Garden is a created reward from Allah, and its beauty is part of His generosity. The believer is allowed to hope for its pleasures without being ashamed of that hope. Yet the images also point beyond the limits of worldly pleasure: no earthly garden removes death, loss, or fear. The promised Garden does.

**Eternity Changes the Meaning of Home**

The verse names Gardens of Eternity. A worldly home can be beautiful and still be temporary. Families separate, buildings decay, and a person may lose the place he worked years to build. The eternal dwelling answers this instability. It offers residence without eviction, joy without the shadow of departure, and belonging without the fear that time will take it away.

This hope can make the believer less enslaved to status in the present world. He cares for his home and family, but he does not treat worldly possession as the proof that he has finally arrived. He can live simply without feeling spiritually defeated and can use wealth without making it his identity. The eternal home places every temporary home in perspective.

**Allah's Pleasure Is Greater Than the Garden**

The verse gives the highest place to Allah's pleasure. This does not make the Garden insignificant. It tells the believer that created rewards are not the summit of the relationship with the Creator. The greatest success is to be received by Allah with contentment and to know that He is pleased with the servant.

This ranking also purifies intention in worship. If a person acts only for pleasure, he may abandon obedience when the reward is delayed. If he seeks Allah's pleasure, he remains faithful even when worldly results are hidden. The Garden is a gift; Allah's pleasure is the sign that the servant has reached the purpose for which the gift exists.

**Hope Is a Moral Force in the Present**

The promise of the Hereafter is not an escape from worldly responsibility. It strengthens the believer to practice responsibility without demanding immediate payment. He can give wealth, protect another person, or endure loneliness because he trusts that Allah's reward is larger than the present cost.

Hope also protects against envy. Another believer's success does not reduce one's portion in Allah's bounty. The Garden is not a scarce resource fought over by human rivalry. This allows people to rejoice in one another's obedience and to support one another toward the same end. The community described in the previous verse becomes possible when its members hope together rather than compete for the only visible reward.

**The True Triumph Has a Clear Measure**

The verse calls this the ultimate triumph. Triumph is not defined as winning every public argument or possessing the largest home in this world. It is to reach the promised Garden, remain there, and receive Allah's pleasure. That measure gives present victories their proper place. A worldly win that costs faith is loss; a worldly loss endured in obedience may be part of triumph.

The believer still seeks justice and lawful success. He simply refuses to call them ultimate. He asks Allah for a life that leads to the final home, a heart that seeks divine pleasure above applause, and actions that make the promise believable in the present. The greatest hope is therefore also a discipline: live now as a servant who wants to meet Allah pleased with him.

The promise also teaches the believer how to use present homes. A family home should train gratitude, a workplace should be governed by honesty, and a public institution should protect dignity. These earthly places cannot become Paradise, but they can reflect a servant's hope for Paradise. He builds without worshipping what he builds, enjoys without forgetting the Giver, and gives without imagining that temporary comfort is the final triumph. Allah's pleasure remains greater because every created delight is valuable only when it is received under His approval. The heart that seeks that approval is already being trained for the eternal dwelling.

The highest reward also protects the believer from using worship as a bargain. He hopes for the Garden because Allah promised it, but he obeys Allah because Allah is worthy of obedience. This is why a servant can remain faithful when no worldly benefit appears. The promise of contentment is not a reason to despise the created world; it is a reason to place the created world beneath its Creator. Every lawful pleasure becomes safer when it leads to gratitude, and every sacrifice becomes lighter when it is offered for the One whose pleasure is greater than the reward itself.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:73

> O Prophet! Struggle against the disbelievers and the hypocrites, and be firm with them. Hell will be their home. What an evil destination!

**Expanded Commentary**

**Striving Takes More Than One Form**

The command to strive against disbelievers and hypocrites is broad. It does not prescribe one method for every person or every circumstance. Exegetes have described force against open aggression, persuasion and proof against hypocrisy, and other forms of effort suited to the situation. The word points to serious exertion in defense of truth, not to a single unthinking response.

This breadth prevents two distortions. One reduces striving to physical conflict and ignores teaching, evidence, patience, and moral resistance. The other turns “struggle” into a soft word for doing nothing when people are being harmed. The believer asks what form protects faith and people, what authority is legitimate, and what response is proportionate. Firmness without wisdom becomes cruelty; wisdom without firmness becomes surrender.

**Firmness Does Not Mean Temper Without Control**

The command to be firm with them concerns moral clarity and resistance to manipulation. A leader should not allow repeated oaths, mockery, or threats to decide the community's direction. Firmness may mean enforcing a boundary, refusing a false claim, exposing a plot, or standing against aggression. It does not mean losing self-control or treating every opponent as equally dangerous.

The Prophet's firmness was governed by justice. He heard claims, protected the community, and distinguished people by evidence. A believer who shouts at everyone has not necessarily become firm; he may simply be ruled by anger. Firmness is the ability to hold the line when pressure rises while still speaking truthfully and avoiding excess.

**Open Disbelief and Hidden Hypocrisy Need Discernment**

The verse joins disbelievers and hypocrites but does not erase the difference between them. Open opposition can be identified by declared rejection or aggression. Hypocrisy hides behind belonging and therefore damages trust from within. The response may differ: open conflict can require defense, while hypocrisy may require investigation, counsel, restricted trust, and exposure of harmful conduct.

This distinction protects the innocent from collective punishment. Not every non-Muslim is an enemy, and not every struggling Muslim is a hypocrite. The command concerns those who oppose and harm the truth, not people who live under a peaceful agreement or ask sincere questions. The believer must keep moral categories connected to actual conduct rather than using them as labels for disliked people.

**The Inner Struggle Also Requires Firmness**

The command has an inward application without replacing its public meaning. A servant may harbor hypocrisy, fear, or love of status within himself. He must strive against those traits through repentance, truthfulness, prayer, and the refusal to protect a false image. The enemy inside often speaks in the voice of comfort, so the servant needs the same firmness he demands from an external opponent.

This inward struggle is not self-hatred. It is the disciplined removal of what blocks obedience. The believer identifies the lie, limits the opportunity for it, and asks Allah for help. He does not make peace with a defect merely because it has been present for years. Firmness begins when the servant stops negotiating with what he knows is corrupt.

**Hell Defines the Stakes Without Permitting Vengeance**

The verse warns that Hell is the home of those who persist in this opposition. The warning gives the command urgency, but it does not make human beings owners of the final judgment. Believers can resist aggression and expose hypocrisy while still hoping that people repent. They do not punish an enemy simply to enjoy pain or claim knowledge of the unseen.

Hell is mentioned to show that opposition is not a game with no lasting meaning. A person may avoid worldly consequences, win an argument, or preserve a reputation. None of that changes the final destination if the heart remains opposed to Allah. The believer's firmness should therefore be directed toward rescue and justice, not toward personal revenge.

**A Leader Must Match Means to Reality**

The command is addressed to the Prophet, whose leadership carried public authority. Ordinary individuals cannot turn a general verse into permission for private violence or coercion. They must act within lawful authority, agreements, and the limits of their responsibility. Much of the striving required in ordinary life is truthful speech, protection of the vulnerable, education, and refusal to cooperate with corruption.

The verse calls for courage with discipline. A community that knows its purpose can choose the necessary means without imitating the disorder of its opponents. It becomes firm because Allah is Almighty and Wise: powerful enough to protect the truth, wise enough to require that its servants protect it justly.

Firmness also requires knowledge of limits. A person cannot claim to struggle for Allah while violating agreements, harming innocents, or acting without lawful authority. The command belongs within the complete moral law, which protects life, honors trust, and forbids transgression. The inward struggle against hypocrisy may be constant, while public action must be measured by ability and circumstance. A believer may oppose falsehood through teaching, evidence, refusal, service, or lawful defense. He does not choose the most dramatic method simply because it looks brave. Allah is Wise, so the servant seeks a wise form of courage.

The believer should also resist the temptation to make firmness a personal identity. A person may enjoy conflict and call that enjoyment courage. The command directs effort toward Allah's cause, not toward the ego's need to dominate. Before acting, he checks intention, authority, evidence, and likely consequence. After acting, he accepts correction if the means were wrong. This discipline keeps striving connected to worship. The final warning about Hell makes the work serious, while Allah's wisdom determines the form that seriousness must take in each real situation.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:74

> They swear by Allah that they never said anything ˹blasphemous˺, while they did in fact utter a blasphemy, lost faith after accepting Islam, and plotted what they could not carry out. It is only through resentment that they pay Allah and His Messenger back for enriching them out of His bounty! If they repent, it will be better for them. But if they turn away, Allah will torment them with a painful punishment in this world and the Hereafter, and they will have no one on earth to protect or help them.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Denial Cannot Erase a Spoken Wrong**

The verse describes people who swear that they did not say what they in fact said. Their denial is not a search for the truth; it is an attempt to control the record. They want the benefits of innocence without accepting the responsibility of speech. A false oath may deceive listeners, but it does not change the event before Allah.

This teaches the believer to treat speech as morally real. A careless word can wound, mislead, or expose a hidden contempt. If challenged, the honest response is to clarify what was meant and admit what was wrong. Reconstructing the story to protect reputation adds a second sin to the first. Truthful confession may lower a person's standing before people, but false denial places him under the judgment of the One who heard the original words.

**Blasphemy Is Not a Casual Category**

The verse calls their speech a word of disbelief because it attacked the foundations of faith. This seriousness should not be used to label every awkward phrase or ignorant question as blasphemy. The passage concerns deliberate words joined to mockery, denial, and hypocrisy. Legal and theological judgment requires knowledge, evidence, and proper authority.

At the same time, fear of careless labelling must not make believers careless with sacred things. Allah and His Messenger are not objects for contempt. If a person has crossed that line, the path is repentance, retraction, and renewed reverence. A community can distinguish a confused question from deliberate insult while still refusing to normalize insult as entertainment.

**Plots Reveal Resentment Beneath the Claim**

The verse mentions ambitions they could not achieve and plans they failed to carry out. Their denial therefore sits beside a deeper pattern: they were not merely embarrassed by a sentence. They resented the Prophet and the believers, and they had imagined gaining power over them. Hidden ambition gives the speech its direction.

A person may perform loyalty while waiting for an opportunity to reverse the moral order. This is why communities must assess not only words but also repeated choices, alliances, and the consequences someone seeks. The aim is not to invade private thought. It is to protect people from conduct that clearly turns disagreement into a plan to harm.

**Gratitude Can Be Replaced by Revenge**

The verse says they repaid Allah and His Messenger with resentment for having enriched them from Allah's bounty. A gift should have produced gratitude, but entitlement transformed it into a grievance. When the beneficiaries did not receive everything they wanted, they treated previous kindness as an insult and sought revenge against the source of their provision.

This is a severe form of ingratitude. It warns donors not to make recipients dependent on personal favor, and it warns recipients not to convert help into a claim of ownership over the giver. Provision belongs to Allah even when it arrives through a human hand. The grateful person remembers the gift without becoming servile; the resentful person remembers it only as evidence that he deserves more.

**Repentance Remains Better**

After naming the wrong, the verse opens a path: if they repent, it will be better for them. Repentance is not a decorative conclusion. It requires abandoning the contempt, admitting the lie, ending the plot, and returning to faith. If others were harmed, repair belongs to the return.

The promise of betterment shows that revelation does not expose people merely to destroy them. Exposure can be a final warning before the heart hardens beyond willingness to change. The believer should therefore repent quickly when corrected. He should not wait until a defense has become part of his identity. A lowered head before Allah is better than a protected image before people.

**Turning Away Removes False Supports**

If they turn away, the verse warns of punishment in this world and the Hereafter and says they will have no protector or helper on earth. The loss of helpers exposes the emptiness of the alliances they trusted. Human power can conceal a wrong for a time, but it cannot defend a person against Allah's judgment.

The warning does not mean that every hardship proves punishment. It says that continued rebellion has consequences in both realms. The believer seeks protection through repentance, truthful companionship, and obedience. He refuses to repay generosity with resentment and refuses to defend a lie because the truth is embarrassing. Allah's mercy is near to the one who returns, while no human alliance can rescue the person who insists on turning away.

The believer should also be cautious about repeating detailed reports of a public insult unless the evidence is sound and the detail serves a real purpose. The verse itself gives enough principle: false oaths, contempt, failed plotting, resentment, and an open path to repentance. That pattern can be applied without turning history into rumor. When a person returns, the community should not keep reopening the wound to display its own righteousness. When he refuses to return, the community should not allow sympathy for his public image to expose others to his harm. Allah's knowledge joins justice to mercy more perfectly than any human reaction.

The door of repentance remains open, but the liar must stop protecting the lie. He confesses what can be repaired, restores rights, and lets changed conduct prove that the oath of faith is real.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:75

> And there are some who had made a vow to Allah: “If He gives us from His bounty, we will surely spend in charity and be of the righteous.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Vow Sounds Righteous Before the Test Arrives**

The people described make a promise to Allah: if bounty reaches them, they will give charity and become righteous. Their words identify a condition that should not need to be there. Worship is presented as a payment for receiving wealth, as though the servant will begin obeying only after Allah proves that He can satisfy the servant's request.

A person may ask Allah for provision and promise to use it well. The danger lies in making the promise a bargain that excuses present disobedience. Righteousness is not reserved for the day of abundance. A poor person can be grateful, honest, and generous with time. If a vow is made, it increases responsibility; it does not make obedience optional until the condition arrives.

**Bounty Tests the Promise More Than Poverty Does**

Before wealth arrives, the vow costs little. It allows the speaker to imagine himself generous without releasing anything. Abundance removes the shelter of imagination. Once the money is in hand, the person must decide whether his earlier words were worship or fantasy.

This is why the servant should be careful with spiritual promises. He should not make dramatic declarations while asking for what he wants. A simple intention followed by steady obedience is safer than a grand pledge made under emotion. Allah already knows the servant's need and does not require a performance of future righteousness before granting provision.

**Charity Is a Response to Grace, Not a Price**

Charity should follow gratitude, but it cannot be used to purchase Allah's bounty. Allah gives before the servant earns the right to boast, and the gift remains Allah's property after it enters the servant's hand. Giving therefore acknowledges grace; it does not create a debt that forces Allah to give more.

The vow's language reveals a transactional heart. The servant imagines that wealth will change him automatically. Yet wealth magnifies what is already present. If there is gratitude, it gives gratitude wider reach. If there is greed, it gives greed more material. The believer asks for bounty while asking even more for the character that can carry bounty safely.

**Righteousness Is Larger Than One Donation**

The vow joins charity to being among the righteous. This is correct in direction but narrow if righteousness is reduced to money. The righteous pray, tell the truth, fulfill trusts, protect the vulnerable, and use wealth well. Charity is one sign of that life, not a substitute for it.

A person who gives while cheating, lying, or harming dependents cannot use the gift to declare himself righteous. Nor should a person with little money imagine that he is excluded from righteousness. He can give a kind word, useful knowledge, labor, or patient care. Allah sees the sacrifice in relation to what the servant possesses.

**A Conditional Promise Reveals a Conditional Allegiance**

The phrase “if He gives us” can reveal a hidden complaint: the speaker will serve only after Allah serves him in the exact way requested. True faith accepts that Allah's bounty may arrive as money, opportunity, restraint, or guidance. The servant may ask for a specific good, but he does not make obedience depend on receiving it.

This is especially important when prayer seems unanswered. A believer may grieve and continue to ask, yet he does not abandon worship because the desired outcome has not appeared. If he has made a vow, he fulfills it according to the lawful conditions and does not treat disappointment as permission to break faith. Allah's wisdom is not measured by the timing of one request.

**The Promise Should Become Action Before the Wealth**

The safest response to this verse is to begin now. Give something within present ability, practice gratitude before increase, and decide how future wealth will serve Allah without waiting for wealth to arrive. Then the promise is no longer a speech made to secure a blessing. It becomes a habit already being formed.

When bounty comes, the servant can receive it without surprise at his own generosity because generosity has been trained in smaller matters. He pays obligations, gives charity, and keeps enough to fulfill responsibilities. The vow then becomes a reminder of gratitude rather than a bargain. Allah gives from His bounty, and the servant answers by becoming more righteous, not merely more comfortable.

A servant should therefore make his vows modest and his obedience immediate. Instead of promising that future wealth will make him righteous, he can begin with the present blessing of breath, time, and ability. He can set aside a small amount, serve a neighbor, and keep a duty that no audience sees. These acts reveal whether the desire for righteousness is real. If Allah later expands his provision, the expansion increases an existing habit rather than creating a new identity. The vow then becomes a reminder to remain grateful, not a bargain that makes generosity depend on the amount of comfort received.

A vow made in hardship should therefore be treated as a responsibility, not as a bargaining chip. The servant does not say that Allah owes him prosperity because he promised to be generous. He says that if prosperity arrives, he will be grateful enough to release part of it. Even then, the vow does not make him righteous by itself. Righteousness is a continuing relation to Allah, expressed in truth, prayer, justice, and care. The best preparation for future bounty is present obedience, because the heart that is trained before increase is less likely to become a prisoner of increase.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:76

> But when He gave them out of His bounty, they withheld it and turned away indifferently.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gift Exposed the Promise**

The promised condition arrived: Allah gave them from His bounty. The event should have released the charity they had pledged. Instead, they became miserly and turned away. Their problem was therefore not inability. It was the change that occurred when imagined wealth became actual possession. The gift exposed whether their earlier vow had been sincere.

This pattern appears whenever a person speaks generously before receiving power, money, or recognition. Before the gift, generosity costs no possession. After the gift, the person feels that releasing any part of it threatens the identity he has built around ownership. The verse teaches the servant to judge his own promises by what he does when the opportunity to fulfill them arrives.

**Miserliness Is More Than Keeping Money**

Withholding includes refusing the financial obligation, but the moral defect can spread wider. A person may hold back time, knowledge, forgiveness, or protection even when Allah has given him the ability to share. The closed hand is an outward sign of a heart that treats bounty as a private wall.

Careful spending is not miserliness. A servant must provide for dependents, avoid waste, and plan for real needs. Miserliness begins when fear of loss makes a person deny a rightful claim or forget gratitude. The wealthy person can be generous without extravagance, and the poor person can be miserly with a small amount. The measure is willingness to fulfill Allah's claim.

**Turning Away Shows a Change in Allegiance**

The verse adds that they turned away indifferently. They did not merely forget one payment in confusion. They moved away from the promise and from the moral relationship it represented. Indifference is dangerous because it makes betrayal feel ordinary. The person no longer feels troubled by the gap between his words and actions.

A believer should treat such a gap as urgent. If he promised a charitable act and now hesitates, he should review the promise, the need, and the lawful conditions rather than waiting for the feeling to return. Obedience often precedes emotional ease. The heart learns generosity by giving, not by waiting until every fear has disappeared.

**Provision Is a Test of Gratitude**

Bounty tests what poverty can conceal. A person with little may sincerely wish to give more, while a person with much can refuse a small obligation. Neither condition automatically proves virtue or failure. Allah tests the servant through what is in his hand and what he believes he may keep.

Gratitude translates into use. The grateful owner pays debts, feeds dependents, supports the needy, and recognizes that his wealth came through means he did not create alone. He does not need to distribute everything to prove gratitude, but he must not pretend that possession erased the Giver's claim. Every increase asks a question about direction.

**The Promise Was Never a Contract Against Allah**

Their behavior also shows a misunderstanding of vows. They treated their promise as a way to persuade Allah to grant wealth, then acted as though receiving wealth cancelled the spiritual purpose of the promise. But a vow binds the servant to truthfulness; it does not bind Allah to deliver the requested outcome.

A person should therefore avoid making promises in moments of desire. If he has made one, he should fulfill it sincerely rather than renegotiate after the blessing arrives. Allah knows whether the vow was an act of devotion or a strategy for obtaining a benefit. The safest charity is given because Allah deserves it, not because the donor wants to control the next gift.

**Indifference Can Be Reversed by Immediate Action**

The verse is a warning, but it also tells the servant where to begin repair. Open the hand before the excuse grows. Give what is due, acknowledge the failure, and rebuild trust through repeated action. If someone was harmed by the withholding, repair the harm directly.

A person who has become indifferent may not feel worthy of generosity. He should still return. Allah's bounty is not permission to remain selfish, and a past failure is not permission to stop correcting the future. The gift revealed the defect, but the same gift can become a means of repentance when it is finally placed where Allah commands.

The heart that is afraid of losing wealth should remember that every possession already rests within Allah's power. Hoarding cannot make the gift permanent. It can only deprive the owner of the chance to turn it into lasting good. Responsible planning is wise, but planning must include the claims that Allah placed on wealth. A person who gives may still become poor, yet he has not lost the meaning of what he gave. A person who withholds may remain rich, yet the richness can become a witness that he preferred possession to gratitude. Immediate action is the best protection against the indifference described here.

The servant can protect himself by deciding in advance what generosity will look like. He can set a fair portion aside, consult people who understand need, and refuse to let sudden increase rewrite his obligations. Such planning is not distrust of Allah. It is a way to keep gratitude concrete when desire becomes loud. If he has already failed, he should not wait for a dramatic feeling of remorse. He can begin with the payment he withheld and the apology he postponed. The hand learns openness through use, and Allah can turn a gift that exposed greed into a means of healing it.

Gratitude must become action before the heart learns to call withholding wisdom.

The hand opens when gratitude stops being only a feeling.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:77

> So He caused hypocrisy to plague their hearts until the Day they will meet Him, for breaking their promise to Allah and for their lies.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Broken Promise Can Become a Heart Disease**

The verse describes hypocrisy as a plague that remains in the heart until the meeting with Allah. The image is strong because hypocrisy spreads through the moral system. One broken promise is not merely an isolated failure; when it is defended by lies and followed by indifference, it trains the heart to live in contradiction.

The servant should not confuse a sin followed by repentance with a permanent disease. The danger lies in refusing to admit the sin, repeating it, and allowing the falsehood to become part of one's identity. A person may begin by hiding one failure and end by needing an entire public story to protect the hiding. The heart then spends more effort preserving the image than seeking Allah's forgiveness.

**Divine Requital and Human Choice Meet**

Many exegetes understand the subject of the punishment to be Allah, while another reading emphasizes that their own miserliness produced the hypocrisy. These readings are not enemies. Allah judges and allows the consequence, while the people choose the conduct that makes the consequence fitting. Divine judgment does not cancel human responsibility.

This balance protects the believer from two errors. He cannot say that his hypocrisy was forced on him and therefore blame no one. He also cannot imagine that his self-repair alone controls the final result apart from Allah's mercy. He must repent, tell the truth, and ask Allah to heal the heart. Guidance is sought through action and received as grace.

**The Promise and the Lie Stand Together**

The verse names two causes: breaking the promise to Allah and lying. The breach is not only financial. It is a refusal to let one's word bind one's conduct. The lie then protects that refusal. Together they create a person whose statements point one way while his choices travel another.

Truthfulness is therefore more than accurate information. It is agreement between speech and intention. A servant may change a plan for a valid reason, but he explains the change honestly and accepts the consequence. He does not make a promise in order to gain a benefit and then treat the promise as disposable once the benefit arrives. Allah hears the words at both ends of the bargain.

**A Plague Grows Through Repetition**

A physical plague spreads by repeated contact; a moral plague spreads by repeated habits. Each lie makes another lie easier. Each withheld obligation teaches the hand to close more quickly. Each uncorrected promise makes future promises less meaningful. The person may still speak the language of faith, but the language has become a cover rather than a guide.

The cure must therefore interrupt repetition. Stop making claims that are not intended, fulfill a neglected obligation, admit what has been concealed, and accept a smaller reputation for a greater truth. The servant may need trustworthy help because hidden habits are difficult to see alone. Humility is part of treatment; the person who insists that no disease exists cannot seek healing.

**Until the Meeting With Allah Means Time Is Not Endless**

The phrase until the Day they meet Him gives the warning a final horizon. The disease may remain throughout life if the person refuses repentance, but life itself is the period in which repair is still possible. The servant should not postpone return because the heart still feels divided. Delay gives the plague more time to become normal.

Meeting Allah means that the hidden state will no longer be hidden. The public story will have no power to edit the account. This should produce both fear and hope. Fear moves the servant to correct the lie now; hope tells him that correction is still meaningful before death closes the record.

**Repentance Restores Agreement Between Word and Deed**

Repentance begins when the servant stops bargaining with the truth. He names the broken promise, returns what he withheld, apologizes where necessary, and makes future commitments carefully. He does not demand immediate trust from people he deceived. He earns trust through consistent action while asking Allah for forgiveness.

The heart can change direction. A plague is not a permanent name for every patient. But healing requires obedience that contradicts the old habit. The servant must let his hand open, his speech become accurate, and his private worship become more real than his public claim. Allah knows the unseen, and His mercy welcomes the person who returns before the meeting arrives.

The image of a plague also teaches that hypocrisy harms relationships. People stop trusting promises, then stop offering honest counsel, and finally protect themselves through suspicion. The liar has damaged not only his own heart but the social fabric around him. Repair therefore includes patient consistency. He must keep small promises, accept verification, and avoid demanding instant confidence from those he deceived. Allah can heal what repeated falsehood damaged, but healing follows truth. The servant should not despair at the seriousness of the disease; he should fear the greater danger of becoming proud of the disease and calling its symptoms wisdom.

A promise should therefore be made only after the servant has considered the cost and the ability to fulfill it. He should say less and do more. If circumstances change, he reports the change rather than disappearing behind silence. This habit protects the heart from the first stages of hypocrisy. It also protects other people from building plans on words that were never secure. Allah's knowledge of the hidden makes every promise weighty, even when no document records it. Truthful conduct keeps the heart flexible; lies harden it until the person experiences hypocrisy as normal.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:78

> Do they not know that Allah ˹fully˺ knows their ˹evil˺ thoughts and secret talks, and that Allah is the Knower of all unseen?

**Expanded Commentary**

**Private Speech Is Still Within Allah's Knowledge**

The verse asks whether the hypocrites know that Allah knows their thoughts, secret conversations, and all unseen things. Their public oaths are not the full record. The private discussion in which they mock, plan, or encourage one another is also known. No locked room, whispered message, or hidden motive lies outside Allah's awareness.

This knowledge is a warning to the dishonest and a comfort to the sincere. The hypocrite cannot protect himself by controlling the public version of events. The sincere person who is falsely accused can trust that Allah knows what happened even when no human witness is available. Divine knowledge is not selective attention; it includes the words people conceal and the intentions beneath the words they reveal.

**The Unseen Includes What Has Not Yet Appeared**

The Qur'anic idea of the unseen includes realities beyond present sense, future events, and hidden aspects of existing things. Allah's knowledge is therefore not limited to what has already become public. He knows what a private plan will produce, what a concealed resentment is preparing, and what a person's heart would choose if circumstances changed.

This does not mean that human beings possess the unseen. They must not claim to know another person's final state or invent hidden motives without evidence. The verse belongs to Allah's knowledge. The believer acts on what is clear and leaves the unseen judgment to Him. That humility prevents suspicion from becoming a religious virtue.

**Secret Conversation Can Be Worship or Corruption**

A private conversation is not automatically blameworthy. People need counsel, comfort, planning, and confidential care. The moral question is what the privacy serves. A secret used to protect a vulnerable person can be mercy. A secret used to mock the Prophet, spread falsehood, or coordinate harm becomes evidence of corruption.

The source tradition contrasts the hypocrites' private talk with the private remembrance of virtuous people. This contrast is important. Privacy removes the pressure of an audience, so it reveals what a person values when applause is unavailable. The believer should cultivate hidden worship, honest prayer, and confidential acts of care. The private self should not be a place where public faith goes to die.

**Knowing Allah Sees Changes the Use of Silence**

The servant may choose silence for wisdom, but he cannot use silence to create a realm beyond accountability. A person who says nothing publicly may still be responsible for the plan he supports privately. Conversely, a person who lacks the power to speak publicly may still be faithful through private refusal, prayer, and truthful counsel.

Awareness of Allah's sight makes privacy cleaner. It prevents the believer from using anonymity as permission to insult, deceive, or exploit. It also gives him courage to do good when nobody will praise it. The hidden act has an audience: Allah, who knows the secret before it becomes an action.

**Thoughts Need Discipline Before They Become Speech**

The verse mentions thoughts as well as conversations. Not every passing thought is a chosen belief, but cherished thoughts shape speech and action. Envy may appear uninvited; the servant becomes responsible when he feeds it, justifies it, and turns it into a plan against another person.

The believer disciplines the inner life through remembrance, truthful self-examination, and refusal to rehearse contempt. He asks Allah to replace suspicion with evidence and resentment with justice. This work may never be visible to people, but Allah sees it. Hidden obedience is as real as hidden corruption, and the unseen Judge knows which one the heart is choosing.

**Divine Knowledge Makes Repentance Urgent**

Because Allah already knows, repentance does not require informing Him of a secret. It requires ending the secret's power over the servant. The person stops the harmful conversation, admits the lie to the people affected, and returns to obedience. He does not wait for public exposure before changing.

The verse's question is therefore an invitation to wakefulness. Do they not know? The believer answers with conduct. He lives as someone whose messages, private rooms, and unspoken motives are within Allah's knowledge. That awareness produces neither despair nor performance. It produces sincerity: worship when unseen, truth when inconvenient, and humility before the One who knows everything hidden.

The knowledge of the unseen also prevents the servant from worshipping secrecy. Privacy can protect sincerity, but secrecy cannot make an unlawful act safe. A hidden kindness may be more valuable because it is free from applause; a hidden insult may be more dangerous because it is rehearsed without correction. The believer gives his private life to Allah through prayer, restraint, and honest thought. He does not demand that people know every struggle, but he also does not use their ignorance as permission to continue a wrong. Allah knows the secret, and that knowledge is enough to make the secret morally real.

The believer can use this awareness to build a private life that is better than his public image. He prays when nobody is watching, refuses a hidden wrong when it would be easy to commit, and speaks well of people who cannot reward him. These acts are not escapes from society. They are the roots of trustworthy public conduct. A person whose private speech is worship is less likely to use public religion as a mask. Allah knows every unseen thing, so the servant asks that what Allah knows of him be better than what people suppose.

No private room is beyond the sight of the One who knows the unseen.

That awareness makes hidden worship meaningful and hidden wrongdoing frightening.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:79

> ˹There are˺ those who slander ˹some of˺ the believers for donating liberally and mock others for giving only the little they can afford. Allah will throw their mockery back at them, and they will suffer a painful punishment.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mockery Attacks Both Abundance and Scarcity**

The mockers criticize two opposite donors. They slander the person who gives generously and ridicule the person who can afford only a little. Their target is not the amount itself. Their target is the act of giving, because generosity threatens the selfish order they prefer. If a person gives much, they accuse him of showing off; if he gives little, they call the gift worthless.

This double attack reveals a standard designed to condemn everyone. It cannot be satisfied because its purpose is not evaluation but humiliation. The believer should recognize this tactic in public life. A malicious critic may use any result as proof against the person he already wants to discredit. The answer is not to stop doing good. It is to keep the intention directed to Allah and to use sound criteria when evaluating the work.

**A Large Gift Is Not Automatically Proud**

A generous donation can come from wealth, gratitude, and a sincere desire to relieve need. It can also be mixed with a desire for recognition. Human beings should not claim certainty about the unseen motive merely because the amount is large. They can protect the fund from vanity and still honor the possibility of sincere sacrifice.

The donor should examine himself. Does he prefer that people know, or would he still give if his name were hidden? If public acknowledgment serves accountability, it may be lawful; if it becomes the purpose, the gift is in danger. The correct response to suspicion is neither self-display nor paralysis. Give responsibly, purify the motive, and leave the final acceptance to Allah.

**A Small Gift Can Carry a Large Sacrifice**

The person who gives little may be giving a large share of what he owns. Amount alone cannot measure sacrifice. A small contribution from limited means may represent a night's labor, a family's careful budget, or the choice to share food that was already scarce. Allah knows the proportion between the gift and the giver's capacity.

This does not make every small donation automatically superior. It teaches the community not to despise modest giving. A poor person should not be shamed into giving beyond ability, and a wealthy person should not use a small public gift to avoid a larger obligation. Each servant asks what Allah has placed in his hand and gives with sincerity and wisdom.

**Mockery Destroys the Social Purpose of Charity**

Charity joins people across differences in wealth. Mockery breaks that bond by making the wealthy afraid to give publicly and the poor ashamed to give at all. The mocker wants both groups isolated: one anxious about being accused, the other convinced that his effort has no value. The result is less generosity and more resentment.

A healthy community protects donors from vanity without humiliating them and protects modest donors from contempt without romanticizing poverty. It teaches accurate accounting, privacy where appropriate, and gratitude for every sincere contribution. Correction should improve the giving, not make giving itself socially dangerous.

**Allah Returns the Mockery to Its Source**

The statement that Allah throws their mockery back at them describes a moral reversal. The people who thought they were superior become the ones exposed as foolish. Their laughter cannot diminish the gift, but it does diminish their own character. They reveal that they prefer contempt to service.

The believer should not imitate this reversal by mocking the mockers for pleasure. He can expose the wrong and protect the giver without feeding the same spirit. Allah's judgment is enough. The servant's task is to keep his own heart free from the desire to humiliate those who humiliate others.

**Sincerity Is Not Measured by the Crowd**

The verse frees the donor from public judgment. People may misunderstand a generous act or overlook a modest one, but Allah sees the means, the intention, and the need. This allows the believer to give without building his worship around approval.

He should also receive criticism intelligently. If a donation is wasteful or misdirected, he should correct it. If the criticism is merely slander, he should not surrender the good act to the slanderer's standard. The final measure is neither the largest amount nor the loudest laugh. It is whether the servant gave what he could, where Allah allowed, with a heart that sought Allah rather than an audience.
