The believer values this discomfort because it is safer than the comfort of a lie. He also tries to become such a companion for others, offering counsel without humiliation and honesty without cruelty.

**Truthfulness Is a Form of Protection**

A truthful companion protects the servant from the chain of one lie leading to another. If a person has failed, the truthful friend helps him admit it early. If a rumor arrives, the truthful friend asks for evidence. If a project is corrupt, the truthful friend refuses to bless it merely because many people benefit from it.

This protection extends to institutions. Leaders who surround themselves with people who only praise them become vulnerable to hypocrisy. Leaders who welcome honest review can correct errors before they grow. The community should not confuse loyalty with silence. A truthful ally remains loyal to Allah's approval even when that requires disagreeing with a human leader.

**Taqwā Makes Truth a Daily Practice**

Mindfulness of Allah makes truthfulness more than a social reputation. The servant tells the truth when nobody can reward him and when a lie would be easier. He fulfills a promise after the emotion that produced it has faded. He corrects a public statement when he discovers it was wrong.

These acts train the heart. Taqwā grows through small decisions that place Allah above convenience. Company supports the training, but the final choice remains personal. A person cannot blame friends forever for his lies, nor can he become truthful simply by standing near good people. He must respond to their example.

**The Command Builds a Trustworthy Community**

A community of truthful people is safer for the weak, the repentant, and the leader who needs honest counsel. Its members do not weaponize every admission, and they do not reward performance over character. They can disagree without inventing motives because they share a commitment to truth.

The verse is brief because its demand is wide. Fear Allah inwardly, then choose companions whose lives help that fear become conduct. Be truthful in speech, intention, promises, and spending. When the community is built on such people, repentance can be received, institutions can be examined, and mercy can operate without being manipulated.

The truthful also protect one another from exaggeration. They do not conceal a wrong to preserve friendship, and they do not magnify a wrong to destroy friendship. Their loyalty is to Allah, so they can advise a companion privately and defend him from an unfair accusation at the same time. This balance makes a community safe for repentance. People know that honesty will not be rewarded with gossip and that lies will not be protected by friendship. Taqwā becomes visible in the quality of the relationships believers build around truth.

The command to be with the truthful also has an inward dimension. The servant should keep company with his own honest conscience by refusing to rename greed as ambition, resentment as justice, or cowardice as patience. He brings these feelings before Allah without pretending they are already pure. Truthful friends can help because they hear the words he uses, but the heart must eventually stop negotiating with what it knows. When inner speech becomes honest, outward speech becomes less manipulative, and repentance can proceed without the constant work of protecting an image.

Such companions help the believer distinguish a true failure from a passing difficulty. They do not call him a hypocrite because he is tired, nor do they call him sincere merely because he speaks beautifully. They watch whether he returns to Allah, repairs harm, and keeps faith when no praise is available. Their honesty is a mercy because it gives the servant a chance to correct himself before a hidden weakness becomes a settled character. A truthful community is therefore one of the practical gifts of taqwā.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:120

> It was not ˹proper˺ for the people of Medina and the nomadic Arabs around them to avoid marching with the Messenger of Allah or to prefer their own lives above his. That is because whenever they suffer from thirst, fatigue, or hunger in the cause of Allah; or tread on a territory, unnerving the disbelievers; or inflict any loss on an enemy—it is written to their credit as a good deed. Surely Allah never discounts the reward of the good-doers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Messenger's Mission Changes the Meaning of Sacrifice**

The verse says that the people of Medina and the nomadic Arabs around them should not prefer their lives over the Messenger's when following him is required. The command belongs to the Prophet's public leadership and to the conditions of that community. It is not a general permission for private individuals to abandon lawful authority or to force others into danger.

The principle is that a community cannot claim loyalty while treating its messenger's mission as someone else's burden. The believers share the cost according to ability. They do not place the Prophet in danger while preserving every personal comfort. In later settings, the same principle appears as readiness to support truth and legitimate communal responsibility rather than as blind obedience to every political leader.

**Hardship Itself Can Become Reward**

The verse lists thirst, fatigue, and hunger. These are not achievements people would seek for their own sake. They become rewardable when endured in Allah's cause and joined to sincere action. The servant does not need every step to produce a visible success before Allah records it.

This gives meaning to unavoidable discomfort. Travel, long work, care for others, and the effort to preserve justice may exhaust the body. If the servant accepts the hardship for Allah while using wise means to reduce it, the hardship is not wasted. The reward lies not in pain alone but in the obedience, patience, and purpose carried through the pain.

**Movement Can Frighten Wrongdoing**

The verse includes stepping into territory that unnerves the disbelievers. This describes the moral effect of the believers' presence. They do not need to boast or commit injustice to weaken false power. Their organized obedience, courage, and refusal to submit can make wrongdoing uncertain.

The principle applies beyond battle. A truthful witness can unsettle a corrupt institution. A group that refuses exploitation can make an unjust practice harder to sustain. A teacher who gives people sound knowledge can weaken the power of rumor. The believer seeks this effect through lawful means. He does not create fear in the innocent and call it victory; he makes falsehood less secure by making truth more visible.

**Loss Inflicted on an Enemy Is Not the Whole Reward**

The verse counts even the loss inflicted on an enemy as a good deed when it occurs in Allah's cause. This does not make every harm to an opponent righteous. The cause, authority, conduct, and target matter. The reward is connected to resisting wrongdoing within the moral limits Allah has set.

The believer therefore avoids hatred as a goal. He may resist an aggressor, protect a community, and accept that an enemy's power is reduced. He does not rejoice in cruelty or harm people who are not responsible. Good deeds are not produced by an unlawful method simply because the speaker uses religious language.

**The Record Includes Small and Large Steps**

The repeated idea that each hardship and step is written teaches that Allah's record is attentive. A long campaign and a single exhausting walk may both be significant when they express sincere service. The servant does not know which act will carry the greatest weight, so he should not despise small contributions.

This also comforts people whose work is hidden. The person who prepares food, repairs equipment, drives others, or stays with dependents may not appear in the public story. Allah records the cost that allowed others to serve. No step is lost when it is taken for Him, even if people never learn that the step occurred.

**Allah Does Not Discount Goodness**

The verse ends by assuring that Allah never discounts the reward of good-doers. Human accounting can overlook hours, intentions, and indirect benefits. Allah does not. He knows the thirst, the fatigue, the fear, and the reason the servant continued.

This assurance should produce both endurance and humility. Endurance, because sacrifice has a guaranteed witness. Humility, because the reward is Allah's generosity rather than a wage the servant can demand. The believer serves without asking whether the act will become famous. Allah records every good step, and His record is more secure than any public history.

The verse therefore gives dignity to every part of a difficult effort. The visible march, the hidden preparation, the care for those who remain, and the endurance of heat all belong to the record when they serve Allah. A servant should not wait for a heroic role before offering what he has. He can reduce the burden of another person, refuse to prefer comfort, and take one lawful step into difficulty. Allah does not discount the reward because the step was small or unseen. He records the good-doer with a generosity greater than the servant's own account of what was achieved.

The assurance is especially important when a servant compares his contribution with that of others. One person may endure public danger while another quietly keeps a family supplied. One may travel while another gives wealth or skill. Allah does not use human visibility as the measure of reward. He records the act, the hardship, and the intention together. The believer therefore contributes without envy and receives another person's sacrifice without dismissing his own. Every good-doer is seen by the Lord who never loses a single step.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:121

> And whenever they make a donation, small or large, or cross a valley ˹in Allah’s cause˺—it is written to their credit, so that Allah may grant them the best reward for what they used to do.

**Expanded Commentary**

**No Contribution Is Too Small for Allah's Record**

The verse follows the earlier mention of hardship by naming donation and movement. A believer may spend much or little, cross a long valley or take a short step, and every sincere act is written. Human eyes notice scale, but Allah notices obedience. The small gift may represent a larger sacrifice for the person who gives it, while a large gift may cost the wealthy little. Allah's record holds both the visible amount and the hidden effort.

This gives dignity to people whose resources are limited. A person cannot say that because he lacks wealth he has no share in service. He can give a skill, a meal, a ride, or a portion of time. The verse does not turn small service into a performance. It assures the servant that sincere usefulness is never beneath Allah's attention.

Crossing a valley is an image of movement through uneven ground. It involves distance, fatigue, and uncertainty about what lies ahead. The believer's service is not merely an intention kept safely in the heart. He places his body and resources on a path where obedience costs something.

The image also includes indirect service. A person who prepares the route, supports the traveler, or makes the crossing safer shares in the effort when the work is for Allah. Communities often depend on such unseen labor. The record does not erase the contribution because the person was not at the front of the procession.

**Written Deeds Give Meaning to Hidden Work**

The act is written to their credit. This language assures believers that no sincere effort disappears into the confusion of events. A donation may be spent and forgotten by people. A journey may leave no public monument. Allah preserves the deed, its intention, its difficulty, and its benefit.

The record also creates accountability. A person should not assume that an action is harmless merely because it is private. Allah writes the sincere good and the concealed corruption. The believer therefore cleans his motive before giving and asks whether the journey serves obedience rather than reputation.

**The Best Reward Exceeds the Act**

Allah grants the best reward for what they used to do. The reward is not a narrow wage equal to the deed. Allah can reward the best aspect of the servant's work, overlook the weakness attached to it, and give more than the servant could claim. This is generosity, not a denial that deeds must be sincere.

The promise protects the servant from calculating whether his effort is worth the cost. He gives what he can and trusts Allah to complete the value. The best reward may appear in the Hereafter, but it can also include a purified heart, a protected community, or a benefit that continues after the giver has forgotten the act.

The pairing of small and large donations prevents public wealth from becoming the only measure of virtue. A wealthy person must give generously, but he cannot purchase superiority through a large gift if he seeks praise. A poor person may give little and be more sincere because the little represents real deprivation.

This does not encourage people to use limited means as an excuse for withholding what they can give. Each person asks what Allah has placed within reach. The amount is judged with circumstances, obligations, and intention. The verse gives a floor of hope, not a ceiling that excuses indifference.

Crossing a valley can symbolize the ordinary movement required by a faithful life. Traveling to learn, visiting the sick, carrying supplies, and going to reconcile people may all be steps written as good when they are undertaken for Allah. Worship is not restricted to moments of stillness. It includes purposeful movement through the world.

The believer therefore stops despising the practical work around religious service. Administration, transport, preparation, and care can be part of the same reward when they are lawful and sincere. Allah sees the distance others overlook and records the effort that made the visible good possible.

The verse encourages continuity. A servant need not wait for a heroic occasion. He gives regularly, moves when needed, and leaves the accounting to Allah. Small acts repeated with sincerity may form a life whose value is greater than one dramatic gesture.

The final promise also makes service humble. The servant does not demand that people repay him because Allah has already promised the best reward. He accepts gratitude without depending on it and continues when no one notices. What people forget remains written with Allah, and His generosity is better than public recognition.

The believer should also remember that Allah rewards the best of what people do, not only the most visible part. A person may have weaknesses in his record and still possess a sincere act Allah magnifies. This is not a reason to tolerate sin; it is a reason to keep serving while repairing the weakness. The written record gives hope to the exhausted and responsibility to the comfortable. Both know that the Lord sees the full journey, including the steps that made later goodness possible.

**Every Step Can Be Worship**

The practical movement of believers also teaches that worship includes preparation and follow-through. A person who gives a donation may need to travel, arrange its delivery, and check that it reaches a sound purpose. A person who crosses a valley may need to return and report what was learned. Allah records these connected steps rather than only the moment that attracts attention. The servant works carefully, keeps the trust intact, and leaves the final reward to Allah. What looks like ordinary logistics can become part of a life spent in His cause.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:122

> ˹However,˺ it is not necessary for the believers to march forth all at once. Only a party from each group should march forth, leaving the rest to gain religious knowledge then enlighten their people when they return to them, so that they ˹too˺ may beware ˹of evil˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Community Needs More Than One Kind of Service**

The verse corrects the idea that every believer must be physically present in every campaign. Some march, while others remain to gain religious knowledge and teach their people. Both roles serve the community. Courage in the field and patient learning in the gathering are complementary responsibilities rather than competing claims to virtue.

This balance prevents a community from losing its teachers whenever a crisis appears. It also prevents scholars from treating study as a refuge from every communal duty. The division of labor is deliberate: some protect the community's immediate safety, while others protect its understanding and long-term direction.

The verse presents learning as a responsibility that must be carried by a group. Not everyone can master every field, but every community needs people who devote themselves to sound knowledge. These people learn deeply, distinguish revelation from custom, and return to explain what they have understood.

This is not permission for a small class to monopolize religion. The knowledgeable teach, and the wider community listens, asks, and acts. Learning becomes a trust rather than a badge. A scholar who refuses to communicate has not completed the responsibility described here, and a listener who refuses to learn cannot shift all responsibility to the scholar.

**Teaching Must Return to the People**

Those who gain understanding enlighten their people when they return. Knowledge is not complete when it remains in notes, private conversation, or personal admiration. It must reach the people whose choices can be improved by it. Teaching translates understanding into guidance that families and communities can use.

The return also keeps the learner connected to ordinary life. A person who studies only among experts may forget the questions and pressures faced by workers, parents, and new believers. Coming back requires him to explain clearly, listen carefully, and make the knowledge relevant without weakening its truth.

**Warning Includes Hope and Caution**

The purpose of teaching is that people may beware. Warning alerts them to evil and its consequences, but it also gives them the opportunity to choose safety. A teacher should not use fear to display authority. He explains what harms faith, character, and society, then shows the path of repentance and good action.

The warning must be proportionate and evidence-based. Not every disagreement is a danger, and not every unfamiliar practice is evil. The knowledgeable person distinguishes a clear prohibition from a matter of legitimate debate. Caution protects the community when it is joined to mercy and understanding.

A healthy community allows people to develop different gifts. Some are suited to travel and defense, some to teaching, some to healing, administration, agriculture, or care. The verse gives religious learning a central place among these gifts because every other service needs moral direction.

Specialization does not create hierarchy of human worth. The one who teaches is not automatically better than the one who carries supplies. Each role is judged by sincerity and benefit. The community honors the different burdens and makes cooperation possible instead of forcing every person into the same public form of service.

**Learning Requires Movement and Patience**

The verse can also imply leaving familiar surroundings to gain understanding. Serious learning may require travel, time, and the humility to sit with a teacher. A person who wants immediate answers may collect slogans without acquiring wisdom. The learner accepts a season of study so that future teaching will not be built on guesswork.

At the same time, study cannot become an endless postponement of action. The learner takes what he knows and teaches it responsibly while continuing to grow. Knowledge is a trust carried in stages. The community benefits when its scholars remain learners and its learners become useful teachers.

The verse ends with collective awareness. People who understand religion help their people notice the paths that lead to harm before those paths become normal. They explain obligations, expose manipulation, and make repentance accessible. A watchful community is not a suspicious community; it is one that sees clearly and responds wisely.

The division of labor therefore becomes an act of mercy. Some go forth, some learn, and all benefit when each role is honored. The community remains strong when it can defend its people today while educating them for tomorrow.

The division of roles also teaches leaders not to praise only the visible sacrifice. A community that honors the traveler but neglects the teacher will eventually lose the knowledge that tells it where to travel. A community that praises study but refuses to protect its people will lose the freedom in which study can flourish. The verse calls for mutual respect. Those who gain understanding return as servants, not as a separate elite, and those who march recognize that their sacrifice is strengthened by people who preserve guidance.

The teacher's return also demands humility about the limits of his knowledge. He distinguishes what is certain from what is debated and does not turn personal preference into a divine command. He listens to the people he serves because their questions reveal where explanation is needed. In this way, learning becomes a cycle: the community sends people to understand, the learners return to teach, and the people's real needs deepen the next stage of study. The whole body grows when knowledge travels back to the people.

The result should be a people who can recognize evil before it spreads and good before it is forgotten.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:123

> O believers! Fight the disbelievers around you and let them find firmness in you. And know that Allah is with those mindful ˹of Him˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command Addresses an Organized Community**

The verse speaks to believers as a community under the Messenger's leadership. Its command to fight is not a license for private violence or for harming neighbors because of identity. It concerns an organized conflict in which authority, cause, and moral limits matter. The believer reads the command alongside the prohibition of transgression and the duty to protect those not engaged in fighting.

This context is essential because zeal without discipline can turn a revealed command into a pretext for chaos. Legitimate defense and resistance to aggression require knowledge of who is responsible, what means are lawful, and whether the action serves justice. A person cannot appoint himself judge, commander, and executioner and then claim that the verse approves his anger.

The wording about those around the believers teaches strategic responsibility. A community should not chase distant glory while ignoring danger at its own door. It protects neighbors, families, and institutions from immediate aggression before making grand claims about remote problems.

The principle applies to moral reform as well. A person should confront the clear fault nearest to his own responsibility before performing outrage about the faults of strangers. He begins with his household, conduct, and community. Nearness creates a duty of attention, not permission to treat every nearby person as an enemy.

**Firmness Is Not Cruelty**

The believers are told to let opponents find firmness in them. Firmness means that aggression will not be rewarded and that the community will not collapse through fear. It does not mean uncontrolled rage, torture, humiliation, or delight in suffering. The surrounding teachings of the Quran place firmness inside justice and restraint.

A firm person can speak calmly and still refuse an unjust demand. A community can be merciful to the vulnerable while being unyielding toward an aggressor. The aim of firmness is deterrence and protection. When the enemy recognizes that wrongdoing will meet a lawful boundary, the need for further conflict may be reduced.

**Inner Faults Must Be Faced First**

The spiritual reading of the verse turns the command inward without erasing its public setting. The believer must fight the disbelief, arrogance, greed, and cowardice within himself. If he wants to correct others while protecting his own corruption, his firmness is likely to become hypocrisy.

This inward struggle does not mean tolerating external oppression. It means that public resistance must grow from a heart disciplined by worship. A person who has learned to restrain his own anger is more capable of using force only when necessary. A person who cannot govern himself should fear being entrusted with power over others.

**Taqwā Governs Strength**

The verse ends by saying that Allah is with those mindful of Him. Divine support is connected to taqwā, not to numbers, confidence, or loud declarations. Mindfulness governs the use of strength before, during, and after conflict. It asks whether the cause is just, whether the means are lawful, and whether the heart is seeking Allah or revenge.

Taqwā also gives courage. The believer does not depend solely on equipment or public approval. He uses means carefully while knowing that Allah controls outcomes. This reliance prevents both cowardice and arrogance: cowardice because Allah is with the faithful, and arrogance because victory remains Allah's gift.

A community that responds to every disagreement as warfare will become unjust and exhausted. The verse does not erase distinctions between debate, competition, treaty, and armed aggression. Firmness is measured by the situation. Sometimes it means defense, sometimes a clear legal boundary, and sometimes patient dialogue that prevents conflict.

Proportion protects the innocent and preserves moral credibility. It also keeps a community from being manipulated by people who need a permanent enemy to maintain influence. Leaders must explain the threat, the objective, and the limits. Believers obey lawful direction while asking whether the action remains within Allah's justice.

The purpose of firmness is not endless battle. If an aggressor knows that the community can protect itself, negotiation may become more honest. A weak community may be pressured into agreements that serve only the powerful. Strength, governed by taqwā, can create the conditions for peace by making exploitation less profitable.

The believer therefore asks for the kind of strength that protects and then knows when to stop. Allah's companionship is not proven by the amount of harm inflicted. It is shown by courage that remains obedient, restraint that remains principled, and a willingness to accept peace when justice allows it.

Firmness must therefore be accountable. After danger passes, the community reviews whether its response protected the innocent and served a just purpose. It does not glorify every act performed under the banner of defense. Taqwā requires truth about mistakes as well as courage before an aggressor. A believer can honor those who protected people while still condemning excess. Allah's companionship is sought through justice, and justice includes the willingness to correct one's own side.

The mention of nearness also warns against exporting conflict as a way to escape local reform. A believer may speak passionately about distant injustice while ignoring a neighbor's rights or his own dishonesty. Taqwā brings responsibility home. It asks the servant to protect those immediately under his care, correct his own anger, and support lawful justice before seeking grand recognition. When local conduct is sound, broader resistance has a more trustworthy foundation.

Strength is therefore a trust, not a license to become harsh in every disagreement. It serves justice, protects the vulnerable, and remains answerable to Allah when the conflict ends.

When strength is used in this disciplined way, it protects rather than corrupts the community. The believer asks Allah for courage to resist aggression and humility to stop when the duty is complete. He does not confuse victory with permission to transgress.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:124

> Whenever a sûrah is revealed, some of them ask ˹mockingly˺, “Which of you has this increased in faith?” As for the believers, it has increased them in faith and they rejoice.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Revelation Reveals Different Hearts**

The verse contrasts the mockery of hypocrites with the joy of believers. Revelation does not act like a neutral object producing the same response in every person. It meets hearts that are open, resistant, proud, fearful, or sincere. The words remain true, but the listener's condition shapes whether the truth becomes guidance or an occasion for ridicule.

This contrast calls the believer to examine the heart before blaming the message. If a passage unsettles him, he asks what attachment it is exposing. Discomfort can be a doorway to repentance. The hypocrite instead turns the discomfort into a joke and gathers others around the joke so that nobody has to face what the revelation demands.

The believers' faith increases when a new sūrah is revealed. Faith is not treated as a fixed possession that cannot deepen. Understanding, certainty, love, humility, and obedience can all grow through sincere engagement with Allah's words.

Increase requires more than hearing sounds. The believer listens, reflects, asks how the command applies, and acts upon what he understands. A person may know many passages while remaining unchanged if knowledge never reaches conduct. The increase is visible when revelation produces trust, patience, worship, and moral courage.

**Rejoicing Is a Sign of Recognition**

The believers rejoice because the revelation confirms and nourishes them. Their joy is not shallow excitement. It is the relief of receiving guidance from the One who knows what the heart needs. A difficult command may still bring joy because it gives the servant a clear path out of confusion.

This joy can coexist with fear. The believer may tremble at a warning and still rejoice that Allah warned him before the consequence arrived. He may feel the burden of an obligation and rejoice that Allah entrusted him with a way to serve. Joy is love recognizing the wisdom of the One who speaks.

**Mockery Protects Resistance**

The question asked by the hypocrites is not a sincere inquiry about faith. It is a way of making obedience look foolish. Mockery creates social pressure: if one person admits that the revelation changed him, others may accuse him of weakness. The group protects its pride by laughing before the message can be considered.

The believer should be careful with humor about sacred matters. Lightness can conceal an unwillingness to submit. A person does not need to pretend that every emotional reaction is profound, but he should never use ridicule to avoid a clear moral demand. Honest questions seek understanding; mocking questions seek permission to remain unchanged.

**Purity Affects Reception**

The Quran can remove the hardened crust around a heart when it is received with humility. The believer approaches the text as a servant, not as a judge looking for material to confirm his superiority. He asks what Allah is teaching and where he must change.

This does not eliminate the need for scholarship. Careful interpretation, language, context, and reliable teaching protect the believer from making the verse say whatever he already prefers. Humility and knowledge work together. A pure heart does not reject disciplined study, and disciplined study does not excuse a proud heart.

Every newly revealed sūrah gave the early community another opportunity to grow. The believer's joy therefore includes gratitude for timely guidance. Allah did not leave the community to invent its direction under pressure. He corrected, warned, consoled, and clarified as circumstances unfolded.

The modern believer receives the completed Quran but still encounters its guidance anew. A familiar verse can increase faith when a changed life makes its meaning more immediate. The servant returns to revelation in grief, responsibility, temptation, and hope. The text has not changed; the heart has become ready to hear another layer of its address.

The verse does not present the hypocrites as victims of a message that arbitrarily harmed them. Their hearts were already inclined to resistance, and their response deepened that inclination. Believers, by contrast, welcomed guidance and became more faithful. Each response trains the next response.

The servant therefore prays for a heart that rejoices in truth even when truth corrects him. He does not demand that revelation flatter him. He asks Allah to make the Quran a source of increase, then proves the request through listening, obedience, and gratitude.

A new encounter with the Quran can also increase faith by changing how the believer sees other people. He becomes slower to mock the weak, more willing to forgive, and more alert to the consequences of his choices. If reading produces only arguments and superiority, he asks whether he has received the words or merely collected them. Rejoicing in revelation means being grateful for correction. The servant welcomes a passage that removes an illusion, even when losing the illusion is uncomfortable.

The believer can make this response practical by setting aside time for recitation, reflection, and one concrete act of obedience. A passage about charity may lead to a gift; a passage about forgiveness may lead to an apology; a warning about hypocrisy may lead to a truthful disclosure. These responses are signs that faith has increased. The heart rejoices because revelation has not remained distant words. It has entered the choices by which the servant meets Allah.

A revelation that increases faith makes the servant more teachable, more grateful, and more ready to obey. That is the joy the verse praises.

A believer may therefore read the same sūrah many times and find that a different warning or promise reaches him each time. He thanks Allah for the renewed opening and responds with a deed that confirms his joy. Revelation is living guidance for the servant who approaches it with humility.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:125

> But as for those with sickness in their hearts, it has increased them only in wickedness upon their wickedness, and they die as disbelievers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sickness Is Moral as Well as Intellectual**

The verse speaks of sickness in the heart. This includes doubt and false belief, but it also includes greed, jealousy, guile, and the desire to resist truth. The heart in Quranic language is the center of understanding, intention, and moral choice. A person can possess information while his heart remains sick because he uses knowledge to avoid surrender.

Sickness does not always announce itself through open rejection. It may appear as resentment when another person is praised, discomfort when justice costs money, or a habit of hiding motives behind religious language. The believer treats these signs as a call to healing rather than as proof that he is beyond mercy.

The sūrah increases wickedness in those whose hearts are sick, but the revelation is not the cause of their sickness. It reveals and provokes what was already present. A mirror does not create a blemish by showing it. The Quran's clarity makes resistance visible and gives the person an opportunity to repent.

This distinction protects believers from blaming the message when someone reacts badly. It also prevents them from congratulating themselves too quickly. If a passage produces anger in them, they ask whether it has exposed a hidden attachment. Revelation can become a medicine only when the patient allows the diagnosis to be true.

**Wickedness Can Accumulate**

The phrase “wickedness upon wickedness” describes a process. A person doubts, then protects the doubt with mockery. He envies, then invents a reason to discredit the envied person. He lies, then adds another lie to preserve the first. Each refusal makes the next refusal easier.

This is why small moral compromises deserve attention. The heart is being trained by repetition. A servant interrupts the accumulation through confession, apology, and a deliberate act of obedience. He does not wait until the chain is so long that he can no longer remember where it began.

**Faith and Hypocrisy Are Not Mere Labels**

The verse describes a settled direction rather than giving ordinary believers permission to label their opponents. Human beings can observe speech and conduct, but Allah knows the final state of the heart. The warning should produce self-examination before it produces accusation.

A person may recover from doubt through honest study. A jealous heart may learn gratitude. A deceitful person may become truthful through repentance and accountability. The possibility of healing remains open during life. The danger lies in treating sickness as an identity to defend rather than an illness to bring before Allah.

The verse says they die as disbelievers. Death ends the period in which repentance and change can be chosen. This makes the present moment weighty. The servant should not assume that tomorrow will provide a softer heart or a better opportunity to obey.

The warning is not meant to drive the believer into panic over every passing thought. It calls him to respond while response is possible. He seeks forgiveness, learns what he does not know, and takes practical steps away from the habit that is hardening him. A living heart can still turn.

**Healing Requires Humility**

A sick heart often resists treatment because it wants to remain the authority. The person may accept advice about others while refusing correction about himself. Humility begins when he admits that the verse could be addressing him. He asks Allah to show him the difference between a question that seeks truth and a question designed to delay obedience.

Healing also involves companionship. Truthful people can expose rationalizations, while sincere worship softens the heart. The servant avoids settings that reward cynicism and seeks teachers who combine knowledge with mercy. Allah's revelation wounds the false self so that the real person can return to life.

Although the ending is severe, the fact that the warning is heard is itself an opening. Allah informs the community how corruption grows so that it can be interrupted. The believer does not read the verse as a sentence already written against him. He reads it as a summons to remove the disease before it becomes his final description.

The response is simple but demanding: believe, repent, tell the truth, repair harm, and receive revelation with humility. Allah can replace accumulated defilement with purification when the servant turns before death closes the door.

A heart that recognizes illness should not hide behind the claim that everyone is flawed. General weakness is true, but it does not excuse a chosen refusal to heal. The servant identifies the particular disease: envy, distrust, greed, or love of deception. He seeks the opposite virtue through practice. Gratitude answers envy, generosity answers greed, truthful companionship answers guile, and study with humility answers doubt. Allah's warning becomes a map toward treatment when the servant accepts its diagnosis.

The medicine must be taken repeatedly because the heart can relapse. A person who overcame jealousy yesterday may feel it again today. He returns to gratitude instead of declaring himself cured. A community that removed one dishonest practice keeps its records open because new temptations arise. This vigilance is not obsessive self-hatred. It is the healthy caution of someone who knows that Allah's words reveal hidden disease and that continued humility keeps the wound from becoming a source of pride.

The servant keeps returning to Allah until the final moment, because healing remains possible while life remains.

The warning therefore leaves the door open without making the danger small. The servant seeks help, accepts correction, and replaces the diseased habit with its opposite virtue. He knows that Allah can heal him, but he also knows that healing requires him to stop defending the wound.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:126

> Do they not see that they are tried once or twice every year? Yet they neither repent nor do they learn a lesson.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Trials Are Opportunities to Wake Up**

The verse points to recurring trials and asks why the people do not repent or take heed. Hardship is not automatically proof that a person is being punished, but it is always an opportunity to examine direction. Illness, hunger, conflict, exposure of secret dealings, and other disruptions can reveal what comfort allowed the heart to ignore.

The hypocrites experience the trial and then return to their old habits. Their problem is not a lack of signs. It is a refusal to let signs change them. The believer asks what a hardship is teaching, even while seeking relief from it and refusing to romanticize pain.

The trials occur once or twice every year. Repeated warning creates repeated chances. A person who ignores one lesson may claim confusion, but a person who ignores the same lesson again and again is choosing a pattern. He sees the consequences and still protects the behavior that produced them.

This applies to private life and public institutions. A family may experience the same conflict after every dishonest conversation. An organization may face the same crisis after every failure of oversight. The wise response is not merely to survive the latest event. It is to identify the habit that keeps recreating it.

**Repentance Is a Change in Direction**

Repentance is more than feeling bad during a trial. It means returning to Allah through confession, abandonment of the wrong, and repair. If greed caused a dispute, the repentant person changes the financial practice. If arrogance caused refusal of advice, he invites honest counsel. If neglect of worship caused spiritual emptiness, he protects the neglected obligation.

The verse joins repentance with taking heed because emotion without learning fades. A person may cry in difficulty and repeat the same behavior when ease returns. Real repentance carries the lesson forward into the next decision.

**Trials Can Expose Hidden Alliances**

A crisis reveals what a person truly trusts. Some trust Allah and use lawful means; others trust reputation, wealth, or a political connection. When those supports fail, the heart's dependence becomes visible. The believer treats this exposure as useful knowledge, even when it is painful.

He asks whether he has made comfort a condition for obedience. Does he pray only when life is orderly? Does he speak truth only when it costs nothing? Does he care for others only when praised? Trials can answer these questions. The answer is not meant to produce despair but to show where reliance must be rebuilt.

Taking heed means preparing differently after the trial. A community that suffered for lack of supplies plans better. A person who was deceived learns to verify without becoming paranoid. A leader whose secrecy caused harm creates transparent procedures. Learning is practical because the Quranic warning is intended to protect future choices.

The believer also prepares spiritually. He stores habits of prayer and generosity before the next hardship comes. A heart trained in ease is more able to remain steady in difficulty. Preparation is not a denial of reliance on Allah. It is one of the means through which reliance becomes responsible action.

**Mercy Appears in the Chance to Notice**

Repeated trials can be understood as mercy because Allah gives the person more than one opportunity to wake up. The warning arrives before the final outcome. A person who sees the pattern can still turn, and a community can still repair its institutions.

This mercy should not be abused. More chances do not guarantee an endless supply of time. The servant does not ask for another crisis before obeying. He takes the present sign seriously, thanks Allah for exposing the weakness, and begins the correction while the heart can still respond.

“Do they not see?” asks whether the problem is vision rather than evidence. The trials are visible, but the hypocrites refuse to interpret them. The believer trains himself to notice moral meaning without pretending to know the exact divine reason for every event.

He says, “What can I learn?” rather than, “Whom can I blame?” That question turns disruption into guidance. It leads to repentance, wiser preparation, and greater compassion for others who are being tested. A trial becomes a sign of mercy when it returns the servant to Allah.

The believer also learns to distinguish a trial from a convenient excuse. A hardship may explain why an obligation was difficult, but it does not automatically erase every responsibility. He asks what was within his capacity and what correction is now possible. This honest accounting avoids both self-condemnation and self-deception. The trial becomes beneficial when it produces a clearer plan, a softer heart toward others, and a stronger habit of turning to Allah before the next difficulty arrives.

Taking heed also includes compassion for people whose trials differ from one's own. The believer does not use another person's illness or loss to announce a simple verdict about his faith. He asks how to help, what justice requires, and what lesson applies to himself. Allah may be teaching the whole community through one person's difficulty. When people respond with mercy and honest reflection, the trial produces more than survival. It produces a community less proud, better prepared, and more attentive to the Lord.

The lesson is received when the servant turns the next choice into evidence that the previous warning was not wasted.

A trial that produces this kind of attention has become a teacher. The servant does not claim to know every hidden reason for the event, but he knows enough to repent, prepare, and become more compassionate. He has allowed the warning to serve its purpose.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:127

> Whenever a sûrah is revealed, they look at one another, ˹saying,˺ “Is anyone watching you?” Then they slip away. ˹It is˺ Allah ˹Who˺ has turned their hearts away because they are a people who do not comprehend.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Their First Concern Is Exposure**

When revelation is recited, the hypocrites look at one another and ask whether anyone is watching. Their concern is not what Allah is saying but whether their reaction has been noticed. This is the psychology of a person who manages appearances while resisting truth.

The question can also be understood as mockery among themselves. Either way, the group is united by avoidance. They monitor the audience instead of examining the message. The believer reverses this priority: he is more concerned with Allah seeing his heart than with people seeing his face.

They leave when the revelation demands attention. Physical departure represents an inward refusal. A person may remain seated while mentally leaving, changing the subject, checking who agrees, or finding a reason to delay obedience. The body can be present and the heart absent.

The verse does not condemn every need to leave a gathering. People have legitimate duties, health needs, and responsibilities. The issue is deliberate escape from guidance because it threatens a preferred identity. The believer distinguishes practical departure from the habit of fleeing correction.

**Public Religion Can Hide Private Avoidance**

The hypocrites are close enough to hear the sūrah, yet proximity does not benefit them. A person can attend religious gatherings, use sacred vocabulary, and still organize his life around avoiding surrender. External association is valuable only when it leads to inward truth.

This warning applies to institutions as well. An organization may display scripture while discouraging anyone from applying it to finances, leadership, or treatment of the weak. The believer asks whether revelation is allowed to examine the community or is kept as decoration. A gathering becomes sound when people can hear correction without immediately searching for an exit.

**Turning Away Can Become a Consequence**

The verse says Allah has turned their hearts away because they do not comprehend. This is not arbitrary misguidance. Their repeated avoidance has formed a condition in which they no longer understand the message as guidance. Refusal of truth can damage the capacity to receive truth.

The servant should therefore take small acts of avoidance seriously. He prays for understanding, stays with a difficult passage, and asks a sincere teacher rather than rushing to mock. Comprehension is a gift, but the heart must not keep closing the door through pride.

The glance between them shows how companions can make avoidance easier. One person wants to leave, another gives the signal, and the group confirms that no one needs to face the revelation. Communities can normalize this through jokes, changing the subject, or praising the person who refuses correction.

Truthful companionship does the opposite. A sincere friend asks what the passage requires and helps the group discuss it without humiliation. He does not expose private weakness for entertainment, but he does not provide a hiding place for public wrongdoing. The quality of the group shapes whether hearing becomes guidance or evasion.

**Comprehension Includes Moral Response**

To comprehend revelation is not merely to understand vocabulary. It is to recognize its authority and allow it to change conduct. A clever person may explain a verse while remaining untouched by it. The believer seeks understanding that produces humility, worship, and justice.

This is why study and obedience belong together. The servant asks questions, checks interpretation, and then acts on what is clear. If he uses complexity to avoid every demand, knowledge has become another form of escape. Allah's guidance is understood most fully when it is lived.

The verse exposes the moment when people choose to slip away, but it also lets hearers choose differently. They can remain, listen, and admit that the sūrah has found them. They can return after leaving and receive the correction they first avoided.

The believer asks Allah not to turn his heart away. He resists the need to know who is watching and remembers that Allah already sees. When the message is difficult, he stays with it long enough for fear and pride to become repentance.

A community can make staying easier by creating a culture in which correction is not a public ambush. People need room to ask questions, admit confusion, and seek explanation. When every mistake is mocked, hypocrites learn to hide and sincere learners stop speaking. When everything is excused, no one takes guidance seriously. The balanced community keeps the revelation central, protects dignity, and expects a response. It helps the heart remain present long enough for understanding to become obedience.

The believer can also notice the first movement toward escape. He feels the urge to check whether others agree before deciding whether a command is true. He laughs at a serious point to prevent anyone from asking about it. He delays an obligation until the emotional pressure has passed. Naming these habits helps him interrupt them. He turns his face back toward the message, asks Allah for comprehension, and accepts that a heart may need repeated returns before it becomes steady.

To stay with revelation is to let Allah's speech remain more important than the audience's approval. That persistence can turn a moment of avoidance into the beginning of comprehension and repentance.

The community can help by making sincere attention honorable. It protects people from mockery while still asking them to respond to the truth. In such a gathering, remaining present is an act of courage, and comprehension can grow into repentance, worship, and a durable change of character.

He remains present before Allah.

with humility.

Attention becomes worship when the servant stays with revelation long enough to let it correct him. He seeks understanding without performance and accepts that humility is the beginning of comprehension.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:128

> There certainly has come to you a messenger from among yourselves. He is concerned by your suffering, anxious for your well-being, and gracious and merciful to the believers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Messenger Is Near to the Human Audience**

The verse reminds the people that a messenger has come from among themselves. He speaks their language, knows their social world, and shares the conditions of human life. Guidance is not delivered by a distant abstraction. It arrives through a person whose example can be seen and whose concern can be recognized.

This nearness is a blessing, not a reason to take the messenger lightly. Familiarity can make people forget the extraordinary gift of receiving revelation through one of their own. The believer honors both dimensions: the Messenger is human and therefore an accessible model, and he is commissioned by Allah and therefore entitled to obedience.

The Messenger is concerned by what the people suffer. His mission is not indifferent to their pain. He wants them protected from disbelief, injustice, confusion, and the Fire. His concern may lead him to warn them, correct them, or require sacrifice, but the severity of a warning does not cancel its mercy.

This gives a standard for religious leadership. A teacher should know the real burdens people carry and offer guidance that addresses them. He should not enjoy exposing weakness or use fear to enlarge his authority. Firmness can be compassionate when it is motivated by a desire to save rather than to dominate.

**Anxiety for Well-Being Is Active Care**

The Messenger is anxious for their well-being. This is more than a pleasant feeling. It produces teaching, patience, prayer, counsel, and persistent invitation. He does not abandon people after their first refusal. He continues to make the path clear while leaving their choice in their hands.

Believers imitate this care by becoming useful to one another. They notice hunger, loneliness, ignorance, and danger. They offer practical help and truthful advice rather than merely expressing concern. Compassion becomes credible when it spends time and accepts inconvenience.

**Mercy Does Not Mean Approval of Wrong**

The Messenger's mercy includes warning against what destroys. A person who approves every desire may appear kind but can be abandoning the sufferer to harm. The Prophet's graciousness was joined to clear revelation, so his mercy never required him to call falsehood true.

This balance is important for families and communities. Correction should be delivered with dignity, but it should not be withheld whenever a person might feel uncomfortable. The believer asks whether his speech serves healing. He avoids humiliating the wrongdoer, yet he does not make the wrong safe by remaining silent.

The verse concludes by describing the Messenger as gracious and merciful to the believers. This special care is not tribal favoritism. It reflects the bond of faith and the responsibility of guiding those who accept the message. He teaches them how to worship, disagree, repent, and support one another.

Believers should receive one another with a similar mercy. They do not treat every weakness as proof of hypocrisy. They help the repentant return and make room for people who are still learning. Mercy strengthens faith when it is joined to truth, because it gives the servant courage to admit where he needs repair.

**Human Closeness Makes the Example Practical**

A heavenly command may seem beyond imitation, but the Messenger embodies revelation in human choices. He works with people, faces rejection, carries grief, and responds to conflict. His example shows that mercy can exist under pressure and that concern can remain principled.

The believer studies that example not to turn admiration into sentiment but to acquire a manner. He speaks with patience, protects dignity, and continues to guide without coercion. He remembers that people often need repeated explanation and that a harsh response may close the door he hoped to open.

The arrival of such a messenger is a gift. The people are not left to invent a path through suffering. They receive a guide who wants their good more deeply than their own short-term preferences may allow. Gratitude therefore means listening, obeying, and preserving the mercy of the message in one's treatment of others.

The believer asks Allah to make him a source of relief rather than another burden. He follows the Messenger by combining concern with truth, firmness with gentleness, and knowledge with service. Such a life honors the messenger who came from among the people for their good.

The Messenger's concern also guards against a distorted picture of leadership as control. He wants people to choose guidance, not merely to submit outwardly under pressure. His mercy leaves space for persuasion, patience, and repeated counsel. Those who follow him should examine whether their own religious influence makes people more truthful and hopeful or merely more afraid of them. The best imitation is not a claim to possess his rank. It is a willingness to carry another person's burden without using that burden to gain power.

Mercy in leadership also includes knowing when to let people bear the consequences of their choices. The Messenger's concern did not force belief or remove every hardship caused by rejection. He offered guidance and remained truthful, while the audience remained responsible before Allah. A compassionate teacher therefore does not enable dependency or protect a person from every result. He gives support that leads toward maturity, repentance, and service. Concern becomes wise when it respects both the person's dignity and the person's accountability.

Such care is not sentimental weakness. It is the disciplined mercy of someone who wants people to reach safety and is willing to guide them patiently toward it.

The servant who follows this example does not measure mercy by how pleasant every word sounds. He measures it by whether people are treated with dignity and guided toward what saves them. He remains patient, truthful, and ready to bear the cost of another person's recovery.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:129

> But if they turn away, then say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Allah is sufficient for me. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him. In Him I put my trust. And He is the Lord of the Mighty Throne.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Rejection Does Not Cancel the Mission**

The verse addresses the moment when people turn away after warning and mercy have been offered. The Prophet is not commanded to collapse into despair or to compromise the message in order to keep every listener. He is told to declare that Allah is sufficient. The mission remains grounded in Allah even when people refuse it.

This does not mean that the servant stops caring. The previous verse established deep concern for people's suffering. Sufficiency means that the Prophet's worth, courage, and final success do not depend on the audience's immediate acceptance. He can continue inviting without making acceptance the condition of peace.

The declaration that there is no god worthy of worship except Allah restores the center of the heart. Rejection by people can tempt a leader to seek approval from another audience, another power, or his own reputation. Tawḥīd releases him from these substitutes.

The statement is not merely theoretical. If Allah alone is worshipped, then fear, hope, obedience, and ultimate dependence belong to Him alone. The servant can use lawful means and value human support without giving created beings the place of the Creator. This makes resilience possible when human support disappears.

**Trust Is Responsible Reliance**

The Prophet says he trusts Allah. Tawakkul does not mean abandoning effort or refusing counsel. The Messenger warned, taught, planned, and endured. Trust means that after using the means, he leaves the outcome to Allah and remains obedient whatever the outcome may be.

A believer who trusts Allah still seeks treatment, earns lawfully, protects dependents, and addresses injustice. He does not call passivity reliance. Nor does he treat planning as a rival to faith. Trust purifies effort by removing the illusion that the servant controls results independently.

**The Mighty Throne Signals Absolute Sovereignty**

Allah is the Lord of the Mighty Throne. The image points to the summit of the created order and therefore to a sovereignty greater than every human authority. If the Lord of the Throne supports the Messenger, rejection by a group of people cannot make the truth small.

This remembrance also corrects fear. A ruler, institution, or hostile crowd may seem powerful, but all their power exists within Allah's kingdom. The believer does not underestimate danger, yet he refuses to treat danger as ultimate. The Lord who governs the greatest order can govern the details of a servant's trial.

When Allah is sufficient, the servant can accept praise without needing it and endure blame without being destroyed by it. He can correct his own mistake without fearing that admission will end his identity. He can refuse a corrupt alliance even when the alliance offers immediate security.

This freedom does not make a person cold. He still values friendship and grieves rejection. It prevents grief from becoming worship of approval. The heart returns to Allah after every human response and asks what obedience requires next.

**The Declaration Helps in Repeated Trials**

The verse gives words that can be repeated when people turn away. Repetition is not empty formula when the heart understands what it says. The believer remembers that Allah is enough, renews tawḥīd, entrusts the matter to Him, and recalls His sovereignty.

These words can steady a parent whose advice is rejected, a teacher whose students mock, or a community whose justice is opposed. They do not promise that the difficulty will disappear immediately. They promise that the servant is not without a Lord, a purpose, or a refuge.

The sūrah has described hypocrisy, repentance, sacrifice, learning, leadership, and mercy. Its final statement brings all these matters back to dependence on Allah. Communities need action, but action without reliance becomes anxiety and pride. They need knowledge, but knowledge without worship becomes argument. They need courage, but courage without tawḥīd becomes aggression.

The believer therefore carries this declaration into every responsibility. He serves people because Allah commands service, warns because Allah commands truth, and trusts because Allah rules the Mighty Throne. If people turn away, he does not turn away from Allah. That is the strength of the final refuge.

This final reliance also protects the servant from measuring truth by immediate success. A message may be rejected today and still be true; a crowd may applaud today and still be misguided. Allah's sufficiency gives the believer patience to repeat what is right without forcing hearts. He continues to use wisdom, accepts counsel, and learns from mistakes, but he does not sell the truth for approval. The Lord of the Mighty Throne is a greater refuge than any audience, so the servant can remain compassionate without becoming dependent on acceptance.

Trust also changes how the servant receives delay. He may not see the victory, reconciliation, or answer he hoped for, yet he continues to worship without declaring the effort pointless. The Lord of the Mighty Throne sees a larger order than the servant can see. This does not remove the duty to review strategy and correct error. It means that correction is made from faith rather than panic. Allah is sufficient for the heart that acts carefully, learns honestly, and leaves the unseen result with Him.

Reliance is thus the final calm of a servant who has done what he can and knows who holds the result.

The declaration can be spoken after careful planning, after disappointment, and after success. In every case it returns the heart to the same truth: no created cause is independent, and no rejection can remove Allah's care from the servant who trusts Him. This is why the chapter closes with reliance rather than with dependence on the crowd.
